### [Symmetry protected quantum many body scars through half-gauging](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08304v1)

**Authors:** Weslei B. Fontana, Fabrizio G. Oliviero, Yi-Ping Huang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08304v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `scars & prethermalization` **5/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

In both situations, the scarring is preserved and we are able to identify the single QMBS state at zero-energy, as de- picted in the figure by the red circle. **Summary.** This paper analyzes how Quantum Many-Body Scars persist in structured quantum chains when subjected to symmetry defects or boundary conditions via half-gauging. The core finding is that the underlying symmetries dictate the survival and structure of these non-thermal states, leading to an enlarged, protected subspace. This provides general guidelines for engineering robust quantum phases in higher dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses the robustness of topologically or symmetry-protected quantum states against local perturbations (defects) and engineered boundaries (interfaces), which is central to understanding fault-tolerant quantum computation and exotic quantum phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the persistence of Quantum Many-Body Scars (QMBS) states when symmetry defects or gauging operations are introduced into the system.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that the protected nature of the scars can be maintained, leading to an 'anomalous QMBS subspace' protected by the underlying symmetries, even after complex perturbations.

**Method.** The authors employ techniques including defect insertion analysis, duality transformations, and the specialized 'half-gauging' procedure to probe the robustness of the scarred subspace.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by a class of $\alpha$-chains, specifically $k$-chains, governed by a Hamiltonian $H^{(k)}_s$ derived from an embedding mechanism. The analysis compares scars protected by SPT anchors versus symmetry-breaking anchors.

**Key observables.** QMBS states (non-thermal eigenstates), protected degeneracies, and the structure of the symmetry algebra generated by the gauging procedure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The chain length $k$, the defect strength $\beta$, and the gauging parameterization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the half-gauging procedure successfully recovers a protected, structured subspace, and that the system's behavior can be characterized by its symmetry structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the entanglement entropy profile for the $k=2$ chain, illustrating the persistence of scarring under defect introduction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the scarred model, then analyzing the effect of defect insertion distinguishing between SPT and symmetry-breaking anchors, and finally detailing the core result using half-gauging to reveal interface-protected degeneracies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the interplay between symmetry defects insertion and the phenomena of quantum many body scarring. We exploit a class of models obtained from an embedding mechanism that defines a stochastic matrix form hamiltonian, hosting zero energy many body scarred states. Such models display an interesting playground to explore the consequences of dualities, half-gauging operations and so on. We first show that inside this class of models, what are the conditions to still have scared states after defect insertion. The main result of our work is a detailed analysis of half-gauging in such chains, which introduces a non-trivial interface in our system, leading to scarred states protected by the underlying symmetries. The conclusions for half-gauged scarred systems are general and may lead to fruitful directions for higher dimension scarred physics and their robustness.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using surface plasmon waves to overcome the experimental difficulty of exciting specific subradiant states in atomic chains. By coupling the atoms to the SP field, the authors demonstrate a method to efficiently load and store quantum excitations into these long-lived modes. This makes the atomic chain a viable platform for future quantum memory applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum memory and light-matter interaction in structured environments, which is central to quantum optics and open quantum systems research. The use of plasmonics to engineer collective atomic states is a key modern topic.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of selectively exciting and controlling subradiant states in finite atomic chains, which is difficult to achieve using free-space excitation methods.

**Main result.** Coupling the atomic chain to a surface plasmon (SP) wave allows for the controlled loading, storage, and release of excitations into specific subradiant modes, making the system a promising quantum memory.

**Method.** The authors model the system using a coupled-dipole approach, incorporating the interaction with the SP wave via modified Green's functions derived from electromagnetic theory.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a 1D chain of two-level atoms placed near a metallic film surface (Kretschmann configuration). The dynamics are governed by a coupled-dipole model that includes both free-space and surface-mediated interactions.

**Key observables.** Cooperative decay rate spectrum $\Gamma(k)$, steady-state excitation probability $E$, and the coherence time of the stored subradiant state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chain size ($N$), atomic spacing ($a$), surface/chain distance ($h$), and the properties of the metal (e.g., plasma frequency $\omega_p$, damping $\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is in the single-excitation manifold and neglects higher-order correlations in the dipole expectation values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (Kretschmann configuration) and compare the decay rate spectrum $\Gamma(k)$ for free space versus near the surface, showing the modification of the mode structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the free-space Hamiltonian and then incorporates the surface interaction via the Green's tensor. It details the SP wave generation using multilayer optics, derives the modified dynamics, and finally analyzes the excitation and decay characteristics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Subradiant states of a finite subwavelength atomic chain in free space offer a powerful resource for photon storage. Yet their preparation and control remain challenging experimentally. A standard technique is to drive the atoms by a laser and observe the florescence light of the long-lived subradiant modes of excitation after the laser is switched off. However, these modes are highly degenerate and form an almost continuous band in the subradiant manifold. Here we propose a new method of exciting a given subradiant state by coupling atoms to a surface plasmon wave propagating along the interface with a metallic film. By controlling the chain/surface distance we may load, store and release photons in the atoms, making them a promising tool for future quantum memories.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how coupling quantum batteries, modeled by Dicke and Tavis-Cummings Hamiltonians, to a $\mathcal{PT}$-symmetric lossy-gain cavity pair affects energy charging. Using advanced numerical techniques, the authors map out distinct charging regimes around the exceptional point, finding that the optimal energy extraction occurs in the broken phase. The results highlight the strong control offered by engineered non-Hermitian environments over quantum energy storage.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems, cavity QED, and quantum energy storage, providing quantitative insights into how engineered non-Hermitian environments can control energy transfer and extraction efficiency in quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how coupling a quantum battery (Dicke or Tavis-Cummings model) to a parity-time ($\mathcal{PT}$)-symmetric lossy-gain photonic environment alters its charging dynamics.

**Main result.** The charging dynamics are highly dependent on the spectral regime relative to the exceptional point (EP); the optimal charging point is found in the broken phase, and the counter-rotating terms introduce a reactive oscillation without net work.

**Method.** The dynamics are solved by numerically solving the full Lindblad equation using batched Monte-Carlo wave functions, alongside analytical derivations for short-time scaling laws.

**Model / system.** The system comprises quantum batteries (Dicke and Tavis-Cummings) coupled to a $\mathcal{PT}$-symmetric cavity pair, which acts as the environment. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Stored energy, power, and ergotropy gained/lost in the gain-side versus lossy-side battery; excitation number growth; and the location of the exceptional point.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The loss rate ($\gamma$), the coupling strength ($J$), and the proximity to the exceptional point (EP) are critical parameters defining the system's behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the batteries are identical to maintain $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry, and the Tavis-Cummings treatment is deemed sufficient despite the presence of Dicke terms.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference figures showing short-time scaling of excitation number, energy bounds for TC and Dicke models, and the dependence of observables on the loss rate relative to the EP.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured by first analyzing isolated batteries (Dicke/TC) charged by a reservoir, then analyzing the cavity pair dynamics to find the EP, and finally coupling the battery to the pair to study charging in both the broken and unbroken phases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study how a parity--time-symmetric photonic environment changes the charging of Dicke and Tavis--Cummings quantum batteries. For atoms prepared in the collective ground state the excitation number grows as the square of the charging time, with a coefficient set by the initial mean photon number; the counter-rotating terms of the Dicke model add one to four times that number. Conservation of the total excitation number turns this into an exact ceiling on the stored Tavis--Cummings energy, the transition energy times the smaller of the photon and atom numbers, met numerically to within two per cent, which the Dicke terms overshoot by a few per cent at ultrastrong coupling. We then couple a battery to a lossy--gain pair, keeping the parity--time symmetry intact by putting an identical battery in each cavity; the closed moment system puts the exceptional point at the loss rate. Below it the gain cavity fills and the gain-side battery charges while the lossy-side one stays empty; above it photons are shuttled away and dissipated. Solving the full Lindblad equation with batched Monte--Carlo wave functions, we find that the gain-side battery gains thirty-nine, twenty-six and twenty-eight per cent in stored energy, power and ergotropy in the broken phase and loses sixty-five, thirty-five and sixty-five per cent deep in the unbroken phase, where the lossy-side battery overtakes it. Restoring the counter-rotating terms leaves all of this unchanged within the statistical error; what they add is a reactive oscillation at twice the transition frequency that does no net work but nearly doubles the peak of the instantaneous power. The extra energy is of lower quality, the recoverable fraction dropping from about ninety-eight to ninety per cent. The optimum sits on the broken side, close to but not at the exceptional point.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes quantum metrology in open cavity-qubit systems to estimate the coupling strength $\chi$. It demonstrates that environmental memory effects are critical, capable of boosting sensitivity beyond what is predicted by the simpler Markovian model. The findings guide the selection of optimal quantum probes and measurement protocols for noisy quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for designing quantum sensors in realistic, noisy environments, which is crucial for advancing quantum networking and quantum computation applications utilizing cavity QED systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how photon loss and environmental memory affect the optimal selection of quantum probes and measurement strategies for estimating the dispersive coupling strength in a cavity-qubit system.

**Main result.** Finite environmental memory can significantly enhance and prolong the metrological sensitivity, allowing for non-zero Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) even when the Markovian QFI vanishes. Furthermore, separable probes can outperform entangled ones in certain single-photon regimes.

**Method.** The analysis employs quantum metrology tools, primarily calculating the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) using both density-operator and stochastic (trajectory-based) approaches, comparing Markovian and non-Markovian dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a single bosonic cavity mode dispersively coupled to a two-level qubit, governed by an effective Hamiltonian. The dynamics are analyzed under open-system conditions, ranging from Markovian (Lindblad) to non-Markovian (exponentially correlated bath).

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), Stochastic QFI (SQFI), and the error propagation uncertainty relative to the Quantum Cramér-Rao Bound (QCRB).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dispersive coupling strength ($\chi$), environmental correlation time ($	au_B$), cavity-qubit detuning ($\Delta$), and mean photon number ($\langle \hat{N} angle$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The closed-system analysis provides a benchmark, and the non-Markovian dynamics are modeled using an exponentially correlated Gaussian environment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of QFI for different initial states and regimes, showing the comparison between density-matrix QFI and ensemble-averaged SQFI, and mapping out parameter regions where sensitivity is enhanced.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the closed-system benchmark using the generator variance. It then transitions to open systems, comparing Markovian and non-Markovian dynamics using QFI diagnostics. The core of the work involves analyzing how environmental memory and probe preparation affect the achievable precision bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how photon loss and environmental memory affect probe selection and measurement sensitivity for estimating the dispersive coupling strength in a cavity qubit system. Using density operator and quantum state diffusion approaches, we examine the quantum Fisher information for representative separable and entangled initial states in Markovian and non Markovian regimes. The results show that closed system generator variance alone does not determine open system performance. The survival of parameter sensitive coherences and the influence of environmental memory also play important roles. Within the single photon preparations examined, a separable probe can outperform the entangled family considered. Comparisons of selected coherent and squeezed vacuum preparations at equal mean photon number further illustrate how photon number statistics and qubit preparation affect sensitivity. For the parameters studied, finite environmental memory can enhance and prolong sensitivity and yield nonzero QFI for a probe whose Markovian QFI vanishes. We also numerically identify a fixed Hermitian observable whose error propagation uncertainty remains close to the quantum Cramer Rao bound over a prescribed coupling interval in the Markovian regime. These findings clarify the interplay between probe preparation, environmental dynamics, and measurement choice in dispersive quantum metrology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the resource cost of preparing the ground state of the fermionic Hubbard model using Lindblad simulation techniques. It derives rigorous error bounds by analyzing the approximations involved in simulating the energy-filtering process. The results provide a concrete, albeit large, gate count estimate, highlighting energy transition filtering as the main computational hurdle.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational bottleneck of simulating complex quantum dynamics (like those in Hubbard models) on near-term quantum hardware by providing quantitative resource estimates for fault-tolerant state preparation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the practical resource cost and feasibility of using Lindblad simulation techniques for preparing the ground state of quantum many-body systems in the early fault-tolerant quantum computing regime.

**Main result.** The resource estimation for a 36-site fermionic Hubbard model is calculated to be $7.7 	imes 10^8$ T gates for one unit of time evolution, with the primary cost source identified as accurately filtering energy transitions.

**Method.** The authors employ a dilation-based simulation approach, deriving rigorous error bounds for the approximation of the Lindblad evolution via quadrature and Trotterization techniques.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the fermionic Hubbard model, a canonical model for strongly correlated electrons, whose dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation. The simulation targets preparing the ground state $|\psi_0angle$ by suppressing energy transitions.

**Key observables.** T gate count (resource cost), trace distance error ($\|	ilde{ho}_{1,s} - ar{ho}_1\|_1$), spectral gap ($\Delta$), and spectral norm ($\|H\|$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system size (e.g., 36 sites), the coupling constants ($t_{	ext{hop}}=1, U=4$), and the required time step ($	au$) are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating the energy-domain filter function integral using finite quadrature and bounding the resulting error terms from both quadrature and Trotterization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy-domain filter function $\hat{f}$ and its approximations, and show the scaling of the spectral gap, spectral norm, and required time step ($	au$) with system size.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework for ground-state preparation via Lindblad dynamics, deriving rigorous error bounds for the necessary approximations (quadrature and Trotterization), and finally applying these bounds to estimate the resource cost for the fermionic Hubbard model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances in algorithms for simulating Lindblad dynamics have clarified their theoretical potential for state preparation, but their practicality in the early fault-tolerant regime and beyond remains uncertain. In this work, we address this by investigating the cost of preparing ground states of the fermionic Hubbard model, following the single-ancilla approach of [Phys. Rev. Research 6, 033147 (2024)]. We derive rigorous error bounds including constant prefactors, and compare to empirical error behavior and practical convergence parameters obtained from circuit-level simulations. We find that empirically chosen parameters can reduce the required resources by orders of magnitude, resulting in an estimated $7.7 \times 10^8$ T gates to perform one unit of time evolution targeting the low-energy subspace of a 36-site fermionic Hubbard model. We identify the accurate filtering of energy transitions as the main source of this cost.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates $\mathbb{Z}_N$ Floquet Topological Order using a driven Kitaev model on qudits. The authors show that the drive generates robust chiral parafermionic edge modes and induces anyonic time crystal behavior in the bulk. This establishes the model as a promising platform for studying non-equilibrium topological physics on quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on Floquet engineering of topological order, particularly using qudits and parafermions, provides a concrete theoretical framework for designing quantum simulators for exotic non-equilibrium phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper explores realizing and characterizing $\mathbb{Z}_N$ Floquet Topological Order (FTO) in a non-equilibrium setting, extending beyond the well-studied $\mathbb{Z}_2$ case.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that the driven Kitaev model generates chiral parafermionic edge modes and exhibits bulk dynamics characterized by anyonic time crystal behavior.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical mapping (Fradkin-Kadanoff-type) with advanced numerical simulations, including Matrix-Product States (MPS) and stabilizer formalisms.

**Model / system.** The system is a generalized Kitaev honeycomb model incorporating clock degrees of freedom (qudits), driven periodically by a three-step Floquet drive.

**Key observables.** Chiral parafermionic edge modes, dynamical anyon transmutation (tracked by a non-equilibrium loop order parameter), and the radical chiral unitary index quantifying information flow.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system exhibits distinct regimes, notably the 'Clifford point' where the drive simplifies, and the general non-equilibrium dynamics away from this point.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the system to constrained parafermions and uses MPS simulations, which are limited by rapid entanglement growth away from the Clifford point.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the action of clock operators, the honeycomb lattice structure, the mapping contour, and the evolution of conserved $\mathbb{Z}_N$ gauge links.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the $\mathbb{Z}_N$ FTO model, mapping it to parafermions, analyzing the dynamics of string operators and anyons, and finally simulating the edge and bulk properties in different parameter regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Floquet topological order (FTO) extends topology beyond equilibrium, giving rise to phenomena, such as the dynamical transmutation of anyons. Here, we realize $\mathbb{Z}_N$ FTO in a generalized Kitaev honeycomb model with clock degrees of freedom using a three-step periodic drive. The driven system admits an emergent description in terms of constrained parafermions coupled to conserved $\mathbb{Z}_N$ gauge fields. At the boundary, the drive generates chiral parafermionic Floquet edge modes. We resolve their propagation through correlation functions, characterize their fractional quantum-information transport through the radical chiral unitary index, and use their dynamics to probe exchange statistics. In the bulk, we explore the dynamical anyon transmutation with a non-equilibrium loop order parameter. At a Floquet sweet spot, where the drive becomes Clifford, these phenomena can be accessed using stabilizer simulations. Short-time matrix-product-state simulations away from this point show that the chiral edge response persists at accessible times. Our results establish $\mathbb{Z}_N$ Kitaev models as a setting for exploring intrinsically non-equilibrium topological phenomena on qudit-based quantum processors.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that KPZ superdiffusion, a universal non-equilibrium phenomenon, can occur in chaotic quantum circuits without requiring fine-tuning to integrable models. By combining nonlinear hydrodynamics with tensor-network simulations, the authors confirm the predicted $z=3/2$ scaling for charge transport in these artificial quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, non-integrable quantum platform to observe universal non-equilibrium scaling laws (KPZ), which is highly relevant for understanding energy/charge transport in complex quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the emergence of Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) superdiffusion in chaotic quantum circuits, challenging the assumption that such phenomena are limited to integrable models.

**Main result.** KPZ superdiffusion with a dynamical exponent $z=3/2$ is demonstrated in chaotic quantum spin chains using discrete time evolution, confirmed by tensor-network simulations.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical derivations using Nonlinear Fluctuating Hydrodynamics (NLFHD) with numerical validation via Tensor-Network simulations (TEBD) on quantum circuits.

**Model / system.** The system consists of chaotic brickwork quantum circuits, initially modeled with spin-1 (qutrit) and later extended to spin-1/2 (qubit) chains, designed to conserve a staggered charge $\hat{Q}$.

**Key observables.** Charge correlation function $C(x, t)$, dynamical exponent $z$, propagation velocity $v$, and the universal scaling form predicted by the KPZ equation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The chemical potential $\mu$ controls the transport regime, and the system dynamics are analyzed in the context of the conserved charge $\hat{Q}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the coarse-grained description using local equilibrium approximations and the application of NLFHD to derive scaling laws.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between different spreading regimes (diffusive vs. KPZ) and show the numerical collapse of the charge correlation function onto the predicted stationary KPZ scaling function.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using NLFHD for correlation functions, to applying this framework to specific chaotic quantum circuits (spin-1 and spin-1/2), and finally validating the predictions using advanced tensor-network simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a family of chaotic brickwork quantum circuits exhibiting superdiffusive transport with dynamical exponent $z=3/2$ in the full Kardar--Parisi--Zhang (KPZ) universality class. This is enabled by the equilibrium current associated with a inhomogeneous conserved density having a nontrivial dependence on the chemical potential. Using nonlinear fluctuating hydrodynamics, we derive the propagation velocity, broadening scale, and universal scaling form of the charge correlation function from microscopic equilibrium properties. Tensor-network simulations of a two-site spin-$1$ (qutrit) circuit reproduce these parameter-free predictions, including the full stationary KPZ profile. We further construct a three-site spin-$\frac12$ (qubit) circuit, where transport varies from diffusive to superdiffusive with the chemical potential, while long-lived coherent quasiparticles delay the convergence of the profile to the KPZ scaling form. Our results establish that KPZ superdiffusion in quantum systems is not restricted to fine-tuned integrable models.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel, 3D-printed microwave-resonator Paul trap designed for confining electrons at GHz frequencies while preserving optical access. By characterizing the trap's electromagnetic fields and modeling decoherence, the authors predict that the device can achieve electron spin-qubit coherence times of about 10 ms, advancing the feasibility of quantum information processing using trapped electrons.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles the engineering challenge of integrating high-performance microwave confinement with optical access, a critical bottleneck for scaling up quantum memories based on trapped particles.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors address the challenge of creating a trapping platform that can simultaneously confine electrons using high-frequency microwaves while maintaining an open geometry necessary for optical access and imaging.

**Main result.** They report a 3D-printed microwave-resonator Paul trap capable of operating at GHz frequencies, predicting electron spin-qubit coherence times on the order of 10 ms.

**Method.** The study combines experimental characterization of the trap's electromagnetic properties (using resonance measurements) with theoretical modeling (FEM and decoherence analysis) to predict qubit performance.

**Model / system.** The system is a 3D-printed microwave-resonator Paul trap, which functions as a dual-frequency trap for confining both electrons and ions. The dynamics are modeled using the electron spin Hamiltonian coupled to the motional degrees of freedom, and decoherence is analyzed using master equations.

**Key observables.** Resonance frequency (2.3 GHz), Quality Factor ($Q \approx 10^3$ measured), Electron Spin-Qubit Coherence Time ($\sim 10 	ext{ ms}$), Trapping Frequencies ($\omega_x, \omega_y, \omega_z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** GHz operating frequencies, moderate $Q$ factor, surface roughness ($	ext{R}_{qp} \approx 2.5 \mu	ext{m}$), and the assumption that motional heating is dominated by Johnson noise.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The coherence time prediction assumes motional heating is dominated by Johnson noise, and the analysis relies on the accuracy of the 3D-printed structure's electromagnetic response.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the trap design (outer cavity body and central resonator rod) and show surface profilometry/SEM images comparing polished vs. unpolished samples, confirming surface quality.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the design and fabrication of the 3D-printed trap, characterizes its electromagnetic response via resonance measurements, models the trapping potential and nonlinear dynamics (Kerr parameter), and finally uses these results to estimate the electron spin-qubit coherence time.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a 3D-printed microwave-resonator Paul trap that operates at the GHz frequencies required for electron confinement while preserving an open geometry suitable for laser access and imaging ion Coulomb crystals. The resonator exhibits moderate quality factors of approximately 1000, enabling large confining electric-field amplitudes at low input power, and is in good agreement with finite-element-method (FEM) predictions despite manufacturing imperfections, which we experimentally characterize. The validated FEM model is then used to calculate the electromagnetic fields in the trapping region. These field distributions, together with surface-roughness measurements, are incorporated into a model of electron spin-qubit decoherence to estimate the coherence time. Our analysis predicts that electron spin-qubit coherence times on the order of 10 ms should be achievable under the assumption that motional heating is dominated by Johnson noise, making such systems relevant for quantum information processing. The method of obtaining the coherence time is applicable to any electron Paul trap.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on a method to significantly boost the photon bunching of classical light by implementing multiple, spatially correlated phase modulations within a modified Fabry-Pérot cavity. By cascading these modulations, the authors achieved superbunching, demonstrating enhancements in two- and three-photon correlations far exceeding those of standard thermal light. This technique has implications for correlated imaging and nonlinear optics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel, controllable method using classical optics (SLMs in an F-P cavity) to engineer super-bunching effects, which is highly relevant for understanding and manipulating photon statistics in quantum optics and quantum information applications.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to boost the photon bunching effect of classical light beyond what is achievable with standard thermal light sources.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrated superbunching, achieving two-photon bunching peaks improved by one order and three-photon peaks improved by three orders of magnitude compared to thermal light.

**Method.** The technique involves using a modified Fabry-Pérot cavity where one mirror is replaced by a Spatial Light Modulator (SLM) to cascade multiple independent, spatially correlated phase modulations onto the light wavefront.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a modified Fabry-Pérot cavity. The theoretical model describes the normalized second-order and third-order spatial correlation functions, $g^{(2)}$ and $g^{(3)}$, which are shown to accumulate enhancement when multiple phase plates are cascaded.

**Key observables.** Two-photon bunching peak ($g^{(2)}$), three-photon bunching peak ($g^{(3)}$), and ghost image visibility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of cascaded phase plates ($M$) and the number of independent transverse modulations ($N$) are key parameters determining the enhancement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the paraxial approximation and the ability to encode multiple indistinguishable two-photon paths via the SLM setup.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup using the modified F-P cavity (Fig. 2, Fig. 3), show the measured intensity distributions and $g^{(2)}$ for varying $M$ and $N$ (Fig. 4), and plot the measured bunching peaks confirming linear scaling with $M$ (Fig. 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from reviewing the concept of superbunching beyond the HBT effect, deriving the theoretical product structure for $g^{(2)}$ for $M$ plates, and finally presenting experimental measurements of $g^{(2)}$ and $g^{(3)}$ to confirm the enhancement.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We proposed to boost the photon bunching effect of classical light via a modified Fabry-Pérot cavity with one of the cavity mirrors being replaced by a spatial light modulator, through which multiple independent transverse modulations, each was to encode independently spatially correlated phase on the wave front of the light field, was cascaded in sequence in the longitudinal propagation direction in the cavity. The two-photon bunching peak and the three-photon bunching peak of the generated superbunching light field were measured to be $36.9\pm2.0$ and $\rm (2.46\pm0.12)\times10^{3}$, respectively, which, as compared to those of thermal light, were improved by one-order and three-order of magnitude, respectively. Correspondingly, the ghost image visibility reconstructed with the superbunching light was demonstrated experimentally to be improved by $\sim 40$ times as compared to that with the pseudo-thermal light. Such classical superbunching light field is of essential importance not only for fundamental physics but also for potential applications such as correlated imaging, ghost interference and nonlinear optics.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report on an all-fiber, compact source for generating polarization-entangled photon pairs using a Sagnac interferometer and PPLN waveguide. They measured high entanglement quality metrics, confirming the state is suitable for quantum teleportation. The fiber-integrated design makes this source highly practical for real-world quantum network applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly practical, fiber-integrated platform for generating entangled photons, which is a critical resource for quantum communication protocols like teleportation and quantum key distribution.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal was to demonstrate a compact, practical, and affordable source for generating non-degenerate, polarization-entangled photon pairs suitable for quantum communication.

**Main result.** The source achieved a Bell-state fidelity of 0.850 and a CHSH value of 2.3301, confirming entanglement sufficient for qubit teleportation above the classical limit.

**Method.** The experiment utilized quantum-state tomography and CHSH measurements to characterize the generated entangled state.

**Model / system.** The system is an all-fiber Type-0 Sagnac interferometer embedding a periodically poled lithium-niobate (PPLN) waveguide, which generates entangled photon pairs via spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC).

**Key observables.** Bell-state fidelity ($F_{\Phi^-}=0.850$), Purity ($P=0.836$), Concurrence ($C=0.782$), and CHSH value ($S=2.3301 \pm 0.0075$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The source operates at 0.5 mW pump power and exhibits a corrected pair-rate slope of $(0.918 \pm 0.040) 	ext{ Mcps } 	ext{mW}^{-1}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fidelity is limited by a residual relative-phase offset, suggesting local phase compensation could improve performance to $\approx 0.8865$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the source schematic, the measured broadband SPDC sideband, and polarization correlation fringes measured in H/V and D/A bases.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup, presents measurements of state metrics (fidelity, concurrence) as a function of pump power, confirms entanglement via CHSH violation, and discusses the practical implications for quantum networking.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate a compact, practical all-fiber source of non-degenerate telecom-band polarization-entangled photon pairs assembled from off-the-shelf components. The compact fiber-integrated architecture is designed to be affordable, straightforward to assemble and align, and compatible with existing optical-fiber infrastructure. Photon pairs are generated through type-0 spontaneous parametric down-conversion in a pigtailed periodically poled lithium-niobate waveguide embedded in a Sagnac interferometer. At 0.5 mW pump power, quantum-state tomography yields a Bell-state fidelity $F_{Φ^-}=0.850$, purity $P=0.836$, and concurrence $C=0.782$. A separate CHSH measurement gives $S=2.3301\pm0.0075$. The measured Bell-state fidelity is sufficient for the reconstructed polarization state to support qubit teleportation above the classical fidelity limit under ideal protocol operations. Analysis of the density matrix shows that approximately 96.7% of the population remains in the desired $|HH\rangle$-$|VV\rangle$ subspace, with a normalized coherence of approximately 0.86. A residual relative-phase offset indicates that local phase compensation could increase the Bell-state fidelity to approximately 0.8865. We further characterize the brightness--quality trade-off and obtain a corrected pair-rate slope of $(0.918\pm0.040)$ Mcps mW$^{-1}$. The broadband emission and fiber interfaces offer a route to multiuser entanglement distribution through off-the-shelf multichannel wavelength-division multiplexers with passbands matched to the signal and idler wavelengths.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how entanglement in critical quantum chains is affected by local measurements, finding that the resulting entropy is surprisingly simple. The complexity of the measurement outcome space collapses onto a single, universal compact conformal coordinate. This reveals a fundamental Luttinger-liquid mechanism governing post-measurement entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep, universal connection between local measurement outcomes and global entanglement structure in critical systems, suggesting a powerful, low-dimensional description (a single coordinate) for complex quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how much information from a single, outcome-resolved measurement record is necessary to predict the entanglement remaining between two unmeasured regions in a critical many-body state.

**Main result.** The universal outcome-resolved Rényi entropy is shown to be asymptotically determined by a single compact conformal coordinate extracted directly from the measurement record, revealing an emergent Luttinger-liquid mechanism.

**Method.** The analysis combines theoretical frameworks like compact-boson theory with exact lattice identities (for free-fermion models) and numerical techniques like Matrix Product States (MPS) for interacting chains.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on critical quantum chains, specifically the free-fermion XX model and the interacting XXZ model, analyzed using entanglement entropy calculations.

**Key observables.** Outcome-resolved Rényi entanglement entropy ($S_{\alpha,L}(m)$), the single compact conformal coordinate ($\delta_L(m)$), and the conditional kernel $G(m)_D$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rényi index ($\alpha$), system size ($L$), and anisotropy ($\Delta$ for XXZ).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The result is shown to hold for Born-typical records in the scaling limit, and some numerical calculations rely on approximations like finite bond dimension in MPS.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 'Outcome-resolved conformal decoder,' comparing exact lattice results to compact-boson predictions for the XX benchmark, and showing the decoder's robustness across different geometries and interaction strengths in the XXZ chain.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the problem by first establishing the connection between the entropy and the conformal coordinate using the XX model (free-fermion), then extends this framework to the interacting XXZ model using MPS, and finally compares these results against continuum predictions derived from compact-boson theory.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A local measurement of a critical many-body state produces exponentially many microscopic outcomes, each defining a different conditioned quantum state. We ask how much information from one such outcome is actually needed to predict the entanglement that remains between two unmeasured regions. We show that, in critical quantum chains, the universal outcome-resolved Rényi entropy is asymptotically determined by a single compact conformal coordinate extracted directly from the measurement record. Compact-boson theory explains this compression: the universal entropy is insensitive to all spatially varying components of the boundary profile, retaining only a single relative compact zero mode. At the free-fermion XX point, exact lattice identities expose the microscopic structure underlying the decoder, while large-scale outcome-by-outcome calculations confirm its scaling prediction across different subsystem geometries and Rényi indices. Numerical calculations in interacting XXZ chains further show that the same microscopic coordinate continues to decode the conditional entropy without interaction-dependent retuning. Thus an exponentially large space of measurement outcomes is reduced, for this universal observable, to a single conformal degree of freedom, revealing an emergent Luttinger-liquid mechanism for outcome-resolved postmeasurement entanglement.

</details>

**Summary.** This work shows that spontaneous Hawking radiation, typically associated with curved spacetime, can be modeled using a local quantum spin system. By calculating the local energy density using spin observables, the authors successfully reproduce the continuum QFT prediction for Hawking radiation. This establishes a powerful, microscopic route for quantum simulation of quantum field theory in curved spacetime.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, experimentally accessible many-body observable (local spin expectation value) that directly simulates a fundamental process in quantum gravity (Hawking radiation), bridging condensed matter physics with high-energy theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate a microscopic realization of spontaneous Hawking radiation generated by dynamical black-hole formation within a local quantum spin model.

**Main result.** The expectation value of a local spin observable quantitatively reproduces the continuum Quantum Field Theory prediction for the energy density associated with Hawking radiation.

**Method.** The authors map the QFT problem onto a quantum spin system, calculating the renormalized local energy density using spin observables and comparing it to continuum predictions derived from Bogoliubov mixing.

**Model / system.** The system is a local quantum spin model (e.g., related to Majorana fermions) simulating a 2D spacetime geometry evolving from flat to black-hole geometry. The dynamics are governed by a complex spin Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Renormalized local energy density ($ho_j(t)$), spatially averaged energy density ($ar{ho}(t)$), and mode functions ($\phi^-_n,j$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Surface gravity ($\kappa$), system size ($L$), and time window duration (which grows logarithmically with $L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The agreement between the spin observable and the continuum prediction is limited to a finite time window, signaling the crossover to lattice-cutoff dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the time evolution of the spatially averaged energy density ($ar{ho}(t)$) plateauing at the QFT prediction, and mode functions exhibiting exponential decay consistent with QFT predictions in the intermediate time regime.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical link between Hawking radiation and spin systems, calculates the energy density using local spin observables, and then numerically verifies the agreement with continuum QFT predictions while also analyzing the breakdown of the model at late times.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that Hawking radiation spontaneously generated by dynamical black-hole formation can be realized in a local quantum spin model. Starting from the ground state corresponding to the early-time vacuum, with no initially prepared excitations, we follow the dynamics induced by the evolving geometry. In the corresponding continuum quantum field theory, Hawking particle creation is associated with nontrivial Bogoliubov mixing between the in- and out-mode decompositions. We derive the resulting renormalized local energy density in the continuum quantum field theory, which corresponds to an energy-weighted integral of the Hawking spectrum, and identify its counterpart as a local spin observable whose expectation value quantitatively reproduces the continuum prediction, including its dependence on the surface gravity. The agreement persists over a finite time window whose duration grows logarithmically with system size, revealing the crossover from universal continuum Hawking physics to lattice-cutoff dynamics. These results establish a microscopic route to quantum simulation of quantum field theory in curved spacetime, connecting field-theoretic particle creation directly to experimentally accessible many-body observables.

</details>

**Summary.** This work benchmarks quantum computation performance using only exchange interactions on large arrays of electron spins. By measuring performance across the entire accessible Hilbert space, the authors report a very low system-level error rate. This demonstrates a scalable and high-fidelity method for controlling complex quantum many-body systems using solid-state spin qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, high-fidelity benchmark for scaling quantum computation using only native, local interactions (exchange), which is highly relevant for solid-state quantum computing architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper benchmarks quantum control using only exchange interactions on a large spin system, aiming to utilize the entire available Hilbert space rather than restricting to smaller, conventionally treated subspaces.

**Main result.** The authors achieved an effective system-level error of $3 	imes 10^{-4}$ per exchange, which is reported to be up to an order of magnitude lower than comparable benchmarks on other platforms.

**Method.** Performance is assessed using Out-of-Time-Order Correlators (OTOCs) to study scrambling dynamics, and generalized forms of cross-entropy and mirror randomized benchmarking (MRB) to quantify aggregate error.

**Model / system.** The system consists of arrays of up to 48 electron spins (quantum dots) whose evolution is governed solely by voltage-controlled spin-spin exchange interactions, operating within the total-spin-zero Hilbert space.

**Key observables.** Effective system-level error ($\epsilon_F$), scrambling dynamics (via OTOCs), fidelity, and purity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size up to 48 spins; total-spin-zero Hilbert space dimension exceeding $2^{40}$; error rate of $3 	imes 10^{-4}$ per exchange.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The control relies on $S$-conserving exchange operations, and the approach assumes the ability to measure performance across the full Hilbert space, treating previously 'leakage' states as usable.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling of the accessible Hilbert space, schematics of the QPU chip, and benchmarking procedures (XEB, RB, MRB) used to measure error rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of full Hilbert space benchmarking for exchange-only control, details the application to large spin arrays, presents measurements using OTOCs and randomized benchmarking techniques, and concludes with the reported low error rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exchange-only quantum computing benefits from high fidelity and straightforward control afforded by the exchange interaction. However, independently controllable qubits must be encoded into subsystems of at least three electron spins, restricting computation to only a fraction of the available spin Hilbert space. The remaining states are treated as leakage and used only transiently in gate sequences. By contrast, a quantitative, system-wide measure of exchange-only performance can exploit the full available Hilbert space, including states conventionally treated as leakage. Here, we apply this approach to arrays of up to 48 electron spins, accessing a total-spin-zero Hilbert space with dimension exceeding $2^{40}$. Measurements of out-of-time-order correlators (OTOCs) reveal rich scrambling dynamics and demonstrate access to regimes relevant to quantum computational advantage. Using generalized forms of cross-entropy and mirror randomized benchmarking, we also assess the aggregate performance of the full exchange-only system per fundamental two-body interaction: the two-spin exchange. We obtain an effective system-level error of $3 \times 10^{-4}$ per exchange, incorporating the complete experimental control sequence and all associated noise sources. This value is up to an order of magnitude lower than those reported from comparable benchmarks on other platforms at the time of writing.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper examines how performing post-selected von Neumann measurements alters the quantum characteristics of two-mode entangled coherent states. By analyzing observables like squeezing and photon statistics, the authors demonstrate that this measurement process acts as a tunable mechanism to enhance nonclassicality, confirming post-selection's utility in quantum state engineering.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems, as it explores how measurement back-action (specifically post-selection) can be used as a precise tool to enhance or engineer nonclassical features in continuous-variable quantum states.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how post-selected von Neumann measurements (PVNMs) modify the nonclassical properties of two-mode entangled coherent states (ECSs). It aims to show that post-selection can be a tunable method for engineering these quantum states.

**Main result.** Anomalous weak values, particularly under moderate coupling, enhance key nonclassical features such as squeezing, antibunching, sub-Poissonian statistics, and bipartite entanglement in ECSs.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the conditional pointer state resulting from PVNMs and characterizing various quantum diagnostics on this state.

**Model / system.** The system under study is two-mode entangled coherent states (ECSs), which are non-Gaussian continuous-variable states. The theoretical framework utilizes a measurement interaction Hamiltonian and analyzes the resulting conditional pointer state.

**Key observables.** Squeezing ($\Delta^2 F_i$), photon statistics (Mandel Q parameter, $g^{(2)}$), intermode correlations, entanglement (Hillery-Zubairy correlation $E$), and fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Measurement strength parameter $s = g t / \sigma$, and the coupling strength $g$ and time $t$. The analysis covers weak ($s \ll 1$) and strong ($s \gg 1$) coupling regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an ideal von Neumann measurement context where the measurement interaction Hamiltonian dominates the evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides a schematic illustrating the protocol for preparing the conditional pointer state $|\Phiangle$ via PVNMs on one mode.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically builds upon the weak measurement framework, first defining the physical system (ECSs) and the measurement protocol. It then calculates and analyzes multiple nonclassical observables (squeezing, statistics, entanglement) as functions of the measurement strength parameter, culminating in the demonstration of post-selection's engineering capability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how post-selected von Neumann measurements modify the nonclassical properties of two-mode entangled coherent states (ECSs). From the conditional pointer state, we characterize squeezing, photon statistics, intermode correlations, entanglement, fidelity, and post-selection probability. Anomalous weak values with moderate coupling enhance squeezing, antibunching, sub-Poissonian statistics, intermode correlations, and bipartite entanglement, while fidelity captures the crossover from weak perturbation to strong distinguishability. These results demonstrate post-selection as a tunable approach to engineering ECSs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper extends Quantum Darwinism by showing that the establishment of redundant environmental records—the basis for classical objectivity—does not require direct system-environment coupling. By modeling an intermediate apparatus, the authors prove that the accessible pointer information remains robust even when the environment interacts only with the apparatus. This clarifies the conditions under which indirect measurements can reliably encode quantum information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for AMO and open quantum systems as it provides a rigorous, information-theoretic framework for understanding how environmental coupling (or lack thereof) dictates the emergence of classical reality, which is central to decoherence studies.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the classical objectivity of quantum systems, described by Quantum Darwinism, can be established through an intermediate measurement apparatus rather than direct system-environment coupling.

**Main result.** Theoretically, the apparatus monitoring scheme yields the same accessible pointer information and redundancy as direct system monitoring in the noiseless limit, extending QD to indirect measurements.

**Method.** The authors use exact analytical solutions of quantum circuit models and employ quantum information measures like mutual information, Holevo quantity, and quantum discord to compare the two monitoring architectures.

**Model / system.** The model compares two architectures: direct system-environment interaction ($S 	o E$) versus indirect apparatus-mediated interaction ($S 	o A 	o E$), using a qubit model for the system, apparatus, and environment.

**Key observables.** Mutual Information $I(S:F)$, Holevo quantity $\chi(\Pi_S:F)$, and Quantum Discord $D(\Pi_S:F)$ are key diagnostics for information accessibility and correlation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on the interaction angles ($	heta$) and the noise type (bit-flip, dephasing) and location (apparatus vs. environment).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system has non-zero entropy ($H(\Pi_S) > 0$) and models the environment interaction using quantum circuit formalism.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two monitoring schemes (direct vs. apparatus-mediated) and compare the impact of different noise channels (bit-flips, dephasing) on the recoverable pointer information and redundancy.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by comparing the two monitoring schemes, then quantifies the information transfer and redundancy using information theory, and finally analyzes the robustness of the recorded information under various noise models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Darwinism explains classical objectivity through redundant environmental records of a system's pointer states. We show that these records can be established through an intermediate measurement apparatus without direct system--environment interactions. Using exact analytical solutions of quantum circuit models, we compare system monitoring with apparatus monitoring, where the environment interacts only with the apparatus. During encoding, system--apparatus correlations decrease as system--environment correlations grow. Without noise, both models yield identical accessible pointer information and redundancy, while the discord accounts for their different mutual information. The reliability of these records depends on where noise acts. Apparatus bit flips introduce errors shared by successive records, limiting the information recoverable even from the complete environment. In contrast, larger fragments can mitigate independent noise on environment qubits after encoding. Dephasing in the pointer basis leaves the record information unchanged in the model considered. These results extend the description of quantum Darwinism to indirect measurements and clarify the conditions for reliable environmental records.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on the realization and characterization of a large, $6 	imes 6$ quantum dot array for quantum computation. By employing coherent spin shuttling, the researchers overcome connectivity limitations, achieving high-fidelity single-qubit control and two-qubit gates between distant qubits. The work demonstrates a promising path toward scalable, solid-state quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, scalable solid-state platform for quantum computation, directly addressing the engineering challenge of scaling connectivity beyond simple linear chains, which is a major focus in quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving scalable, high-connectivity quantum computation using semiconductor spin qubits while overcoming physical limitations like wiring bottlenecks and poor 2D connectivity.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated a $6 	imes 6$ quantum dot array capable of high-fidelity single-qubit control and two-qubit gates via coherent spin shuttling, significantly reducing crosstalk in the process.

**Method.** The research employs coherent spin shuttling techniques to position qubits sparsely within a large array, enabling high-fidelity gate operations and state transfer between distant sites.

**Model / system.** The platform is a $6 	imes 6$ quantum dot array realized in a semiconductor heterostructure, controlled by shared barrier and individual plunger gates. Qubit operations rely on manipulating electron/hole spins via electric fields and controlled exchange interactions.

**Key observables.** Single-qubit Clifford fidelities ($>99\%$), round-trip transfer fidelity ($99.4\%$), crosstalk reduction ($42	imes$), and two-qubit CZ gate fidelities ($\sim 88.6\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Shuttling distances up to $5.2 \mu	ext{m}$; exchange interaction ($J$) tunable over orders of magnitude; gate times and detunings are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fidelity is highly sensitive to imperfections in the necessary 'recovery gates' used to compensate for shuttling-induced rotations, which is a major source of error.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $6 	imes 6$ array structure, SED measurements for charge mapping, time-resolved spin paths, and characterization plots for single-qubit and two-qubit gate fidelities.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from demonstrating basic state preparation and single-qubit control via shuttling, to implementing two-qubit gates (CZ) by bringing qubits into proximity, and finally detailing the complex calibration of the shuttling process itself.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computation requires increasing the qubit count while maintaining high performance and connectivity. Semiconductor qubits have demonstrated high-fidelity operation, but growing the system size at sufficient connectivity remains challenging, such that scaling mainly occurred in one spatial direction to preserve access for control lines in the other. Here, we realise an approach that relaxes the requirements on fabrication and demonstrate a 6$\times$6 quantum dot array, defined by a shared barrier gate and individual plunger gates. The quantum dots are tuned through coherent spin shuttling over distances up to 5.2um into a sparse configuration of 10 simultaneously operated qubits, leaving space for shuttling connections. We reduce crosstalk by 42$\times$ in this separated regime, enabling high-fidelity simultaneous single-qubit control, while control-Z operations can be implemented through shuttling and plunger-only control. These results promise high-connectivity quantum circuits, enabled by scaling to two-dimensional geometries and exploiting shuttling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes the efficiency of learning quantum measurements by comparing single-copy (separable) measurement rules against optimal entangled rules in a PAC learning setting. It demonstrates that while single-copy rules can fail asymptotically, they are guaranteed to be only polynomially worse than the optimal entangled rules under certain structural assumptions. This sets fundamental complexity bounds for quantum data processing in machine learning.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental theoretical limits on how much quantum resources (entanglement) are required in the measurement process to achieve efficient learning, which has implications for quantum machine learning protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the advantage of using entangled (non-separable) quantum measurements compared to single-copy (separable) measurements in the context of Quantum Measurement PAC learning.

**Main result.** It proves that while single-copy learning rules can be asymptotically suboptimal compared to optimal entangled rules, the advantage is bounded by a polynomial factor in the sample complexity setting.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Positive Operator-Valued Measure (POVM) framework and compares sample complexities derived from lower bounds (using specific constructions) against upper bounds (using PAC learning theory).

**Model / system.** The model involves learning a measurement (POVM) from a hypothesis class $	ext{Hyp}$ acting on quantum states $ho$ on a Hilbert space $H$. The comparison is between measurements implementable via local operations and classical communication (LOCC) versus general entangled measurements.

**Key observables.** Sample complexity ($m_0$), trace distance, diamond distance, and the excess risk ($\Delta R$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ of the Hilbert space, the error tolerance $\epsilon$, and the failure probability $\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The polynomial advantage bound relies on a natural assumption regarding the coverability of the measurement class by approximately jointly measurable subsets.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes necessary lemmas relating excess risk to distance measures, derives lower bounds on sample complexity for single-copy rules, and finally proves the upper bound showing the polynomial gap between single-copy and optimal learning rules.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Learning with data in the form of quantum states is of current interest and has led to a variety of problems that boil down to interaction with the available data via quantum measurement and classical post-processing of observed classical outcomes. In quantum measurement PAC learning, one is given a sequence of unknown, prepared quantum states and classical labels, along with a hypothesis class of candidate measurements. The task is to select a measurement from the hypothesis class that minimizes a fixed notion of error in prediction of the classical labels via measurement of a new state by the selected hypothesis. In this work, we consider the advantage of interacting with the given data in the measurement learning framework using learning rules given by measurements that cannot be implemented using local operations and classical communication (LOCC), as opposed to single-copy learning rules. We provide a construction showing that there exist learning scenarios wherein single-copy learning rules are asymptotically suboptimal compared to optimal ones. We then show that learning rules based on entangled measurements enjoy at most a polynomial sample complexity advantage over single-copy learning rules in the PAC learning setting (under a natural joint measurability covering assumption).

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a proof-of-concept for accelerating quantum chemistry calculations by running Fragment Molecular Orbital (FMO) methods in parallel across multiple physical quantum processors. By comparing synchronous and asynchronous execution modes on diamond-based QPUs, the authors achieved high parallel efficiencies while maintaining chemical accuracy. This validates the use of distributed quantum resources for molecular simulation workflows.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, hardware-level demonstration of how quantum chemistry algorithms can be mapped onto distributed, multi-QPU architectures, which is highly relevant for scaling quantum simulations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Demonstrating a practical method to leverage parallel quantum computation resources for large-scale molecular calculations in quantum chemistry.

**Main result.** The authors achieved high parallel efficiencies (up to 93.60%) in executing fragment-based quantum chemistry workflows on multiple physical quantum processors, confirming the concept's feasibility.

**Method.** The study compares synchronous shot-parallel execution versus asynchronous fragment-parallel execution using the Quantum Wave Function Sampler (QWFS) approach within the Fragment Molecular Orbital (FMO) framework.

**Model / system.** The testbed utilized Helium clusters (He$_n$) for total energy calculations. The methodology relies on decomposing the large molecular problem into smaller, concurrently solvable fragments (FMO approach).

**Key observables.** Quantum sampling throughput, parallel efficiency (e.g., 93.60% for shot-parallel), and the chemical accuracy of the calculated total energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of quantum processors (three QPU), the size of the helium cluster ($n=2, 4, 8, 10, 14$), and the efficiency metrics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that decomposing the problem via FMO allows for a practical speedup on current hardware, and that the QWFS method accurately estimates the ground state energy.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces FMO quantum chemistry as a natural parallelization route, details the QWFS method, and then experimentally compares two parallel execution modes (synchronous vs. asynchronous) on physical hardware using helium clusters.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fragment molecular orbital (FMO) quantum chemistry offers a natural route to parallel quantum computing: large molecular calculations can be decomposed into smaller quantum subproblems that can, in principle, run concurrently. Their practical benefit on real quantum hardware, however, has remained unclear. We demonstrate parallel FMO calculations on three room-temperature diamond-based 'Quoll' quantum computers installed on-site at the Oak Ridge Leadership Computing Facility. Helium clusters (He$_{n}$, $n=2,4,8,10,14$) served as a testbed for total energy calculations using the Quantum Wave Function Sampler (QWFS), a selected configuration interaction (SCI) approach. We compare two execution modes: synchronous shot-parallel execution, in which quantum processors jointly sample QWFS circuits and combine their SPAM-corrected counts into a single probability distribution; and asynchronous fragment-parallel execution, in which processors independently evaluate FMO fragments using device-specific optimized circuits. Both modes increase quantum sampling throughput without systematic loss of accuracy. The three-QPU shot-parallel workflow reaches a parallel efficiency of $93.60\%$, while the fragment-parallel workflow achieves a measured efficiency of $75.01\%$, increasing to $95.17\%$ when excluding the non-useful idle time associated with non-interruptible submitted jobs. In both cases, the assembled FMO-QWFS energies remain chemically accurate compared to the classical ideal. These results provide a proof-of-concept demonstration that parallel quantum execution can improve the practical throughput of quantum chemistry workflows on prototype hardware, motivating the development of future HPC environments with many fault-tolerant quantum processors operating as a distributed resource for molecular and materials simulation.

</details>

### [The parliament of observers - Against the observable axiom of quantum mechanics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07254v1)

**Authors:** Markus Frembs, Andreas Döring  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.07254v1>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper critically re-examines the foundational axiom linking physical observables to self-adjoint operators in quantum mechanics. By developing a minimal formal definition of an observable based on experimental contexts, the authors demonstrate that this fundamental correspondence breaks down under rigorous scrutiny. It prompts a re-evaluation of the mathematical structure underpinning quantum theory itself.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly challenges the foundational axioms of quantum theory, which is crucial for any theoretical physicist working on open quantum systems or interpreting measurement processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper fundamentally challenges the core axiom of quantum mechanics stating that every observable corresponds to a self-adjoint operator, arguing this premise fails when considering all observables simultaneously.

**Main result.** The authors argue that a basic consistency requirement derived from the concept of 'observables' (as measurement procedures) is mathematically incompatible with the structure of the algebra of self-adjoint operators.

**Method.** The approach involves developing a minimal, context-independent formal definition of an observable based on experimental contexts and then deriving necessary algebraic consistency conditions.

**Model / system.** The analysis is general, focusing on the formal structure of quantum systems ($S$) and the abstract concept of an 'observable' defined by its measurement context, rather than a specific Hamiltonian or physical realization.

**Key observables.** Observables are defined conceptually as 'equivalence classes of measurement procedures' or bounded, measurable functions on outcome spaces.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis considers the transition from Projection-Valued Measures (PVMs) to general Positive Operator-Valued Measures (POVMs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial framework assumes that observables can be characterized by an underlying, consistent set of experimental contexts, and the analysis proceeds by identifying redundancies via 'operational indistinguishability'.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 is noted to deconstruct the quantum-mechanical correspondence rule, illustrating the relationship between physical measurements and the mathematical abstraction to self-adjoint operators.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the problematic axiom, then developing a minimal formalization of observables based on experimental contexts, and finally showing that the consistency requirements of this minimal model conflict with the structure of the standard operator algebra.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The 'unreasonable effectiveness of mathematics in the natural sciences' may at times give the impression that natural science, especially modern theoretical physics, is all but a formal apparatus. However, a key challenge of physics remains to align everyday notions of experience -- be it directly or mediated through instruments of arbitrary sophistication -- with their mathematical formalisation. This applies in particular to quantum theory, arguably the most successful theory of physics ever invented. One of its core assumptions postulates: $Observables\ correspond\ with\ self-adjoint\ operators.$ Here, we argue that already this most basic premise about the quantum formalism falls apart under careful scrutiny, at least when understood for all observables at once. In other words, we challenge the role of the noncommutative algebra of bounded operators as an adequate formalisation of the 'algebra of observables', understood as (equivalence classes of) measurement procedures that admit a coherent empirical interpretation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores a 'magic Mpemba effect' in quantum systems, showing that quantum resources like 'magic' can exhibit counter-intuitive relaxation dynamics. The effect is attributed to interaction-induced dephasing, a mechanism distinct from standard thermalization. The findings are supported by theoretical analysis, simulations across multiple models, and direct measurements on quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel mechanism (interaction-induced dephasing) for anomalous relaxation of quantum resources, extending the concept of the Mpemba effect beyond classical thermodynamics into the realm of quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates an anomalous 'magic Mpemba effect' (MME) in quantum resource generation, specifically determining if a reversal in the rate of nonstabilizerness (magic) can occur without relying on conventional transport or thermalization mechanisms.

**Main result.** The MME was confirmed, showing that states with less initial quantum magic can reach a higher saturated magic value and saturate earlier than expected, attributing this to interaction-induced dephasing.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical random-phase analysis with exact numerical simulations and direct measurements on superconducting quantum processors to quantify the resource dynamics.

**Model / system.** The research utilizes several models, including the phenomenological $\ell$-bit model for Many-Body Localization (MBL) and microscopic spin chains (TFS, TNS, TDWS). Experiments are conducted on IBM superconducting quantum processors.

**Key observables.** Magic density ($M_2$), saturation time ($t_{ss}$), and level spacing statistics ($\langle r angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tilt angle ($	heta$), system size ($N$), and the distinction between localized vs. ergodic dynamical regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed effect is due to interaction-induced dephasing, which is confirmed by the absence of the effect in the non-interacting (Anderson) limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MME signatures ($M_2$ vs. time $t$) showing reversals, and also display level spacing statistics confirming the transition between ergodic (GOE) and localized (Poisson) regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from theoretical modeling ($\ell$-bit model) to detailed simulation across various microscopic spin chains, culminating in experimental verification on superconducting hardware, while consistently contrasting the findings between localized and ergodic dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Mpemba effect challenges the intuition that states closer to equilibrium must relax faster. We ask whether an analogous magic-ordering reversal can occur in the generation of quantum magic (nonstabilizerness), a key resource for universal quantum computation: can a state with less initial magic overtake one with more and reach its asymptotic value sooner? Here, we uncover interaction-induced dephasing as a distinct mechanism for this magic Mpemba effect, requiring neither transport nor conventional thermalization. For tilted product states in an interacting $\ell$-bit model, a random-phase analysis predicts a reversal of the magic ordering: states with less initial magic attain higher saturated magic, independent of their spatial pattern. Exact simulations of the $\ell$-bit model and localized spin chains further show that these states also saturate earlier. We observe both features on IBM superconducting quantum processors. An effective $\ell$-bit implementation accesses long dephasing times at fixed circuit depth by encoding time in gate angles, while a microscopic spin model exhibits the reversal universally across initial patterns in the localized regime but only selectively in the ergodic regime. These results establish interaction-induced dephasing as a route to anomalous quantum-resource relaxation beyond transport-driven thermalizing dynamics.

</details>
