**Summary.** This paper proposes using a continuous-variable flag—an auxiliary oscillator—to protect bosonic quantum error correction codes from continuous errors caused by ancillary qubit decay. By measuring this flag, the continuous noise channel is effectively discretized, allowing for robust error correction. This technique is crucial for advancing fault-tolerant quantum computation in hybrid oscillator-qubit processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, hardware-agnostic protocol for mitigating a major source of decoherence in bosonic quantum computing architectures, directly impacting fault-tolerance thresholds.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of protecting bosonic quantum error correction codes from continuous errors induced by the decay of ancillary qubits during quantum gate operations.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a continuous-variable flag mechanism, which successfully transforms continuous noise channels into discrete, correctable error channels, achieving high levels of error suppression.

**Method.** The core method involves coupling an auxiliary oscillator (the 'flag') to the ancilla to measure and subsequently counteract the continuous error accumulated on the primary bosonic mode.

**Model / system.** The system involves bosonic quantum harmonic oscillators used for quantum information storage, implemented in platforms like superconducting circuits or trapped ions, utilizing dispersive coupling for gate operations.

**Key observables.** Logical error rates (e.g., for cat codes), GKP logical lifetime, and the state of the continuous-variable flag.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ancilla decay rates, coupling strengths ($\chi$), and the number of flags used (one or two).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on modeling the noise channel via Kraus maps and making approximations regarding the timing of gate components (e.g., instantaneous operations).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the continuous error mechanism for conditional rotations and displacements, showing phase-space trajectories and the final memory positions influenced by ancilla decay.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the continuous error problem, then introducing the flag concept to discretize the error channel, and finally comparing the efficacy of single-flag versus two-flag protocols for different gate sequences ($\chi$-matched vs. ECD).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bosonic quantum error correction represents a route towards significant hardware overhead reduction for fault-tolerant quantum computation. However, controlling quantum harmonic oscillators that encode the quantum information comes with its own challenges. In many platforms such as superconducting circuits and trapped ions, the bosonic degree of freedom is controlled by an ancillary qubit whose decay during a qubit-oscillator gate propagates to the oscillator as a continuous error. Such errors generally lie beyond the scope of errors that the bosonic code is capable of correcting. We introduce the continuous-variable flag: an auxiliary oscillator coupled to the ancilla whose phase-space position encodes the continuous error corrupting the storage mode. After the gate, a heterodyne measurement of the flag estimates this continuous-variable error and a subsequent feedback control undoes it up to a bosonic code stabilizer. The scheme requires no new ingredients, as the flag is driven by the same dispersive coupling, displacements and rotations that implement the gates it is supposed to protect. We construct sequences protecting conditional rotations to all orders in ancilla decay with one flag, and conditional displacements to first order with one flag and to all orders with two. Simulations with realistic circuit-QED parameters predict more than three orders of magnitude fewer logical errors for the parity measurement of a four-component cat code, and a finite-energy GKP logical lifetime near its noiseless-ancilla bound. By discretizing continuous-variable errors, the flags introduced in this work are a useful tool for any hybrid oscillator-qubit processor, and open the route towards fault-tolerant simulation and computation with bosonic modes.

</details>

**Authors:** Debadrita Saha, Ethan Dickey, William Horvat, Sabre Kais, Gregory D. Scholes  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.07308v1>

**Summary.** This paper proposes simulating quantum spin dynamics, specifically using the TFI model, by employing 'quantum-like' (QL) resources derived from complex synchronized networks. The authors show how QL-circuits can mimic quantum time evolution, and they introduce graph-level metrics to guide the engineering of physical hardware required for such simulations. This offers a pathway to harness classical network dynamics for quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a novel, potentially resource-efficient, theoretical framework for quantum simulation that bypasses direct quantum hardware requirements by mapping quantum dynamics onto classical network evolution rules.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses simulating quantum dynamics in spin systems by utilizing quantum-like (QL) resources derived from complex synchronized networks.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that classical resources, structured as QL-circuits, can simulate quantum time evolution for models like the transverse field Ising model, while also providing graph-level metrics to guide physical implementation.

**Method.** The simulation employs QL-bits, constructed from graphs, and models time evolution using QL-circuits built from QL-gate analogues, often involving Trotterization.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the transverse field Ising (TFI) model applied to a spin chain. The computational resource is the QL-bit, which is represented by a graph structure derived from synchronized network dynamics.

**Key observables.** Magnetization, microscopic coupling, phase coherence, and effective inter-block coupling (graph-level quantities).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quench parameter $h$ in the TFI Hamiltonian, and the number of QL-bits ($N_{QL}$) which dictates resource scaling.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the dynamics can be mapped onto QL-circuits, and it uses approximations like Trotterization for time evolution. It explicitly notes the exponential cost of the Cartesian product construction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the mapping from a physical spin lattice to a QL-bit network, through time evolution via a QL-circuit, to the final evolved state representation.

**Paper structure.** The work builds from the concept of QL-bits derived from synchronized networks, applies this framework to simulate the TFI model dynamics, and then analyzes the resulting graph structure using specialized metrics to guide hardware design.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent publications have introduced the concept of quantum-like (QL) bits, along with their associated QL-states and QL-gate operations, emerging from the dynamics of complex synchronized networks. The present work builds on this framework to present a simulation of quantum dynamics in spin systems. We focus on the transverse field Ising model, a widely studied Hamiltonian for interacting spin systems. We show how circuits, built using the QL analog of quantum gates, can be used to prepare an arbitrary initial wavepacket and simulate the time evolution of the resulting state for extracting physical observables such as magnetization. Beyond the statevector dynamics, we introduce a set of metrics that characterize how QL-circuit operations transform the underlying graph connections. These metrics quantify the microscopic coupling, phase coherence, and effective inter-block coupling generated by the circuit. These graph-level quantities provide constraints and design targets for potential physical implementations, indicating where interconnection resources, phase control, and effective coupling strength must be engineered in a hardware realization. Overall, this study demonstrates how classical resources can be harnessed to simulate quantum systems and we discuss the advantages and limitations.

</details>

**Authors:** Srikar Alla, Ali Shiri Sichani, Chi-Ren Shyu  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08216v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QEMFN, a hybrid quantum-classical network that uses parameterized quantum entanglement to fuse vision and language embeddings. It demonstrates that this quantum structure improves retrieval performance over classical methods on standard benchmarks. The work provides a detailed, resource-aware evaluation, confirming the framework's utility without claiming quantum advantage.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous testing of entanglement dynamics (entropy correlation, intervention studies) and the direct comparison of performance across ideal, simulated, and real superconducting hardware provide a detailed resource characterization of quantum machine learning circuits.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of multimodal vision-language fusion, proposing to replace classical fusion operators with a structured, trainable quantum entanglement mechanism.

**Main result.** The proposed QEMFN outperforms classical fusion baselines on image-text retrieval benchmarks (COCO-5k, Flickr30k) under parameter-matched conditions, demonstrating the utility of trainable entanglement as a fusion bias.

**Method.** It employs a hybrid quantum-classical framework where embeddings are encoded into quantum states, processed through parameterized entangling circuits, and measured to yield a fused representation.

**Model / system.** The model uses a quantum circuit structure involving visual and textual qubits, connected by trainable single-qubit rotations and paired cross-modal entangling gates. The computation is evaluated across ideal simulation, shot-based estimation, and real superconducting hardware.

**Key observables.** Image-text retrieval performance (Recall@1, MRR), von Neumann entropy ($S_{norm}$), Meyer-Wallach entangling capability, and gradient variance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Matched parameter budgets, number of qubits ($n_q=6$), circuit depth ($L=4$), and noise levels ($p$ for depolarizing channel).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work explicitly does not claim quantum computational advantage, as the circuit remains classically simulable at the studied scales.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the QEMFN quantum fusion circuit. Tables compare performance across various classical and quantum fusion methods, and Table XIII details hardware performance across different noise models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QEMFN, details the quantum encoding and entangling evolution, presents ablation studies isolating the quantum module's contribution, analyzes the correlation between entanglement entropy and performance, and concludes with hardware benchmarking.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multimodal vision-language systems typically fuse image and text embeddings through classical operators such as concatenation, attention, bilinear pooling, or tensor interactions. We propose Quantum Entangled Multimodal Fusion Networks (QEMFN), a hybrid quantum-classical framework that introduces parameterized entanglement as a structured inductive bias for multimodal fusion. Pretrained visual and textual features are projected into compact latent spaces, encoded as angle-parameterized quantum states, processed through intra-modal and paired cross-modal entangling circuits, and measured to produce fused representations for retrieval. Under matched parameter budgets and identical frozen CLIP backbones, QEMFN outperforms classical fusion baselines on COCO-5k and Flickr30k, including multilayer perceptron, tensor fusion, FiLM, cross-attention, compact transformer, and a dequantized paired-topology analogue. An ablation suite isolates the quantum module's contribution from the surrounding classical projections, and quantum-centric analyses report Meyer-Wallach entangling capability, expressibility, gradient variance against barren-plateau bounds, and entropy-performance correlation under controls for training progress alongside an intervention study on the entangling component. QEMFN is executed under shot-based estimation, a noise-modeled fake backend, and a real superconducting device with zero-noise extrapolation. This work does not claim quantum computational advantage; the contribution is the framework together with a controlled empirical and quantum-centric evaluation that positions trainable entanglement as an interpretable, hardware-executable fusion mechanism at scales accessible on contemporary devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies Quantum Machine Learning (QML) to detect rare identity impersonation fraud in banking data using a one-class classification approach. The authors demonstrate that their quantum model, AQFM, significantly outperforms classical methods in terms of reducing false positives while maintaining high fraud detection recall. The work validates the approach on real hardware (NMR) and shows promise for generalizing to future data cohorts.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct application of quantum algorithms (AQFM) to a real-world, highly imbalanced, and time-sensitive industrial problem (fraud detection) provides an empirical test case for quantum advantage in machine learning.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of detecting rare, post-onboarding identity impersonation fraud in digital banking accounts, which presents an extreme few-shot, one-class learning problem.

**Main result.** The Quantum Machine Learning classifier (AQFM) demonstrated superior performance over classical baselines, achieving high recall and significantly fewer false positives, even when tested on an independent future dataset.

**Method.** The study evaluates quantum one-class classifiers using Automatic Quantum Feature Mapping (AQFM) against classical methods like Isolation Forest, focusing on performance metrics like recall and precision.

**Model / system.** The quantum model uses a quantum feature map implemented on a 3-qubit Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) device (SpinQ Triangulum II) for hardware validation, alongside GPU-accelerated simulation.

**Key observables.** Recall, Precision, F1-score, and the anomaly score $s(\mathbf{x})$ derived from the expectation value of the quantum circuit output.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The best performance was achieved using a small 2-qubit circuit configuration, suggesting performance is not monotonically related to qubit count.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the legitimate data manifold can be effectively separated from anomalies using quantum feature mapping, and that the one-class paradigm is appropriate for this fraud detection task.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference comparisons of performance metrics (Recall, Precision, F1) across different qubit counts and between simulation and hardware results.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem context (few-shot fraud detection), proposes the QML approach (AQFM), details the methodology (training on normal data, using quantum feature maps), presents comparative results against classical methods on both 2025 and 2026 cohorts, and discusses hardware limitations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Identity impersonation fraud may emerge only after digital account opening, requiring an early post-onboarding approach based on behavioral and transactional signals observed shortly after activation. Confirmed cases remain rare, creating extreme class imbalance and material operational risk for digital banks. We evaluate quantum machine learning (QML) against classical one-class methods in this setting using a real 2025 cohort of 3,419 digital account openings from Banco de Creditos e Inversiones (BCI), Chile, including 8 confirmed impersonation-fraud cases (0.23% prevalence). The data were provided through an industry-research collaboration between BCI and CoreDevX LABTAV under operational constraints. The best-performing configuration used only 2 qubits and was executed on CoreDevX's SpinQ Triangulum II, a 3-qubit nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) device, enabling hardware validation beyond classical simulation. The quantum one-class classifier based on Automatic Quantum Feature Mapping (AQFM) achieves 88% recall (7 of 8 frauds detected) and 9.21% precision, corresponding to approximately 70% fewer false positives than the best-performing classical baseline at comparable recall. When its 2025-calibrated decision threshold is applied unchanged to the independent 2026 cohort, AQFM detects 20 of 31 frauds (64.5% recall) with 8.81% precision and remains the best-performing model among those evaluated.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Quantum Twisting Microscope (QTM) as a novel tool to probe the quantum geometry and sublattice symmetries in 2D flat-band materials like twisted bilayer graphene. By using a sublattice-polarized tip, the technique allows for the direct measurement of Pauli matrix expectation values. This capability enables researchers to reconstruct the full quantum geometric tensor, providing deep insights into correlated electronic phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides unprecedented, symmetry-sensitive access to the underlying quantum geometry of correlated phases in moiré materials, which is crucial for understanding correlated electron physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a technique to measure the momentum dependence of the quantum geometric tensor and sublattice symmetries in 2D flat-band systems.

**Main result.** The authors propose and demonstrate that a Quantum Twisting Microscope (QTM) with a sublattice-polarized tip can directly measure sublattice Pauli matrix expectation values, allowing for the reconstruction of the full quantum geometric tensor.

**Method.** The method relies on calculating the tunneling current ($I$) using Fermi's golden rule, which is then analyzed by measuring linear combinations of tunneling terms derived from the sublattice-polarized tip Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on 2D flat-band systems, particularly graphene-based moiré superlattices like twisted bilayer graphene (TBG). The physics is modeled using Bloch states ($\xi_S(\mathbf{k})$) and analyzed via tunneling between the sample and the tip.

**Key observables.** Sublattice Pauli matrix expectation values ($\langle \sigma_i angle$), components of the quantum geometric tensor ($Q_{ij}(k)$), and the layer weight ($p_S(k)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), bias voltage ($eV$), and the gap induced by the tip polarization ($\Delta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the sample and tip have graphene-like symmetries, and approximations are made regarding the integration of momentum sums into integrals.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QTM setup, the energetic conditions for momentum resolution, and show the near-constant weight of the top layer component ($p_S(k)$) along the scanned path for TBG.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the measurement challenge in 2D flat-band systems to detailing the QTM setup, deriving the current measurement formula, and finally showing how linear combinations of measurements allow for the extraction of sublattice symmetries and the full quantum metric tensor.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In two-dimensional (2D) flat-band systems, as engineered, for instance, by stacking and twisting van der Waals materials, the momentum dependence of the Bloch states is a key factor in determining the interaction-induced emergence of different phases. Hence, methods of probing this form of ''quantum geometry'' are crucial to understanding these systems. Yet, extracting the momentum dependence of the underlying quantum geometric tensor from measurements is challenging in 2D. Here, we propose employing a quantum twisting microscope (QTM) with a sublattice-polarized tip to fill this gap. We show how expectation values of sublattice Pauli matrices can be probed across the scanned momentum path. This allows for the direct measurement of crucial symmetries characterizing the different candidate instabilities in graphene-based moiré systems. We furthermore show that the quantum metric component along the scan direction can also be extracted; the full quantum geometric tensor is also accessible, given that more than one sublattice polarization can be used. Our work illustrates the potential and extends the versatility of the QTM approach. If implemented in future experiments, this would provide unprecedented access to the symmetry signatures of the correlated phases and to the underlying quantum geometry in twisted bilayer graphene and related systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a detailed theoretical construction of a steering witness for quantum steering in high-dimensional quantum systems. By analyzing Generalized Equiangular Measurements (GEAMs) and solving complex spectral problems related to operator positivity, the authors derive an explicit inequality that guarantees quantum violation for all prime dimensions $d \geq 5$. This advances the understanding of non-local correlations in quantum information theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical construction of quantum witnesses for steering, particularly for high-dimensional systems, provides fundamental tools for certifying non-local correlations beyond simple entanglement measures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to construct an explicit, dimension-dependent steering witness for quantum steering in every prime dimension $d \geq 5$. This is achieved by analyzing the structure of Generalized Equiangular Measurements (GEAMs).

**Main result.** The authors successfully construct an explicit steering inequality and a sufficient criterion guaranteeing quantum violation for all prime dimensions $d \geq 5$.

**Method.** The methodology involves complex spectral analysis of operators associated with the measurements, utilizing the Gershgorin theorem to derive analytic bounds on the measurement parameters. These bounds are then combined to form a valid Positive Operator-Valued Measure (POVM).

**Model / system.** The physical scenario is bipartite quantum steering ($ho_{AB}$), analyzed using Generalized Equiangular Measurements (GEAMs) composed of $d-1$ Mutually Unbiased Measurements (MUMs) and a parameter-dependent family of Hermitian operators $\{E_j(	au)\}$.

**Key observables.** The steering witness violation criterion, $Z(d, t, 	au) > 1$, and the quantum value $Q(d, t, 	au)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The prime dimension $d \geq 5$ and the continuous parameter $	au$, which must lie within a derived asymmetric interval to ensure POVM positivity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the assumption that the non-central operators reduce to three-dimensional invariant blocks and that the spectral bounds derived from two different basis representations are complementary and accurate.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the problem (steering witness construction), then tackling the technical hurdle of ensuring the measurement operators form a valid POVM via spectral analysis (using Gershgorin bounds), and finally combining these results to yield the explicit, dimension-dependent steering inequality.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct a dimension-dependent family of generalized equiangular measurements that gives explicit steering witnesses in every prime dimension $d\geq5$. The construction consists of $d-1$ mutually unbiased measurements and an additional normalized family of $2d-1$ Hermitian operators depending on a parameter $τ$. Positivity of the latter family is the main technical problem. We show that the noncentral operators reduce to three-dimensional invariant blocks, while the remaining central operator is exactly solvable in a two-dimensional subspace. Two unitarily equivalent $3\times3$ matrix representations describe the same active spectrum, but yield different Gershgorin enclosures because Gershgorin bounds are basis dependent. Combining the stronger endpoint from each representation gives a fully analytic asymmetric interval for $τ$ that guarantees a valid POVM. Combining this POVM with the $d-1$ MUMs yields an explicit GEAM. Finally, using a maximally entangled realization and the local-hidden-state bound previously derived for generalized equiangular measurements, we obtain an explicit steering inequality and a sufficient criterion guaranteeing quantum violation in every prime dimension $d\geq5$.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work determines the resource scaling required to certify entanglement in finite quantum experiments. It establishes that the necessary number of local measurements scales polynomially with the inverse of the entanglement resolution, $\gamma^{-(d-1)}$. Furthermore, it quantifies the sample complexity needed, showing a dependence on $\gamma^{-2}$, highlighting the resource cost of verifying quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, quantitative bounds on the experimental resources needed to verify fundamental quantum properties like entanglement, which is crucial for quantum device characterization and certification protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper quantifies the complexity (in terms of required local measurements or sample size) needed to certify that an experimentally prepared bipartite quantum state is genuinely entangled.

**Main result.** The optimal effect complexity scales as $\Theta_d(\gamma^{-(d-1)})$, while the required sample complexity for certification with error $\delta$ scales as $\Theta_d(\gamma^{-2} \log(1/\delta))$ when operational identities are known.

**Method.** The analysis uses polytope theory, relating entanglement certification to the distinguishability between the set of separable states and the set of states consistent with local measurements (NCOM-realizable statistics).

**Model / system.** The study focuses on bipartite quantum states $ho_{AB}$ in a Bell circuit setting, analyzed using local multi-measurements performed by Alice and Bob.

**Key observables.** Entanglement resolution ($\gamma$), effect complexity (number of local effects), and trace-distance entanglement ($E_{tr}(ho_{AB})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local dimension $d$, entanglement resolution $\gamma$, and error probability $\delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between the case where operational identities are known versus the fully self-consistent case where they must be inferred from the same data.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by establishing bounds on the entanglement resolution ($\gamma$) using covering arguments, relating it to the covering infidelity ($\epsilon$), and then deriving the scaling laws for both the required number of measurements (effect complexity) and the number of samples (sample complexity).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In recent work Phys. Rev. X 16, 031057 (2026), we proposed a self-consistent approach to entanglement certification based on generalized noncontextuality. It requires no prior characterization of the measurement devices and, given access to all local measurements, can certify every entangled state in a Bell circuit. Here, we study the complexity of this protocol in a finite experiment. At fixed local dimension $d$, we show that the optimal number of distinct local effects needed to reach a trace-distance \textit{entanglement resolution} $γ$ is $Θ_d(γ^{-(d-1)})$. This scaling is necessary even when the measurements are tailored to the target state. Independent Haar-random projective measurements instead require $Θ_d(γ^{-(d-1)}\log(1/γ))$ effects. Finally, we show that, once the exact operational identities are known, $Θ_d(γ^{-2}\log(1/δ))$ copies of the target state are necessary and sufficient for certification with error probability at most $δ$. Determining the optimal sample complexity when those identities must instead be inferred from the same finite target-state data remains open.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how the connectivity of quantum circuit generators limits the variational space available to VQE algorithms for models like the Hubbard and XXZ Hamiltonians. The authors prove that simply increasing circuit depth is insufficient; instead, adding specific, minimal long-range or correlated gates is necessary to reconstruct the full symmetry-preserving Lie algebra. This establishes generator connectivity as the key determinant of variational accessibility in quantum simulation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, actionable principle for designing quantum circuits for condensed matter simulations, suggesting that algebraic structure (connectivity) is a more fundamental resource than simple circuit depth.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of designing expressive quantum circuits for the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) when simulating interacting many-body systems.

**Main result.** The variational accessibility of the circuit is fundamentally controlled by the generator connectivity, not just the circuit depth; minimal additions of specific symmetry-preserving generators can reconstruct the full dynamical Lie algebra, leading to high VQE fidelity.

**Method.** The authors analyze the Dynamical Lie Algebra (DLA) generated by ansatz operators and correlate its dimension and the resulting tangent-space rank ($r_T$) with VQE performance metrics like ground-state fidelity and energy error.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on spin-1/2 XXZ and Hubbard models, which are canonical models for strongly correlated electrons, studied within fixed symmetry sectors.

**Key observables.** Variational accessibility (measured by $r_T$ and Hamiltonian alignment $A_H$), ground-state fidelity ($F$), and energy error ($\Delta E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths ($J_z$ for XXZ, $U/t$ for Hubbard), and the type of generator connectivity (Nearest-Neighbor vs. Next-to-Nearest-Neighbor or correlated double-excitation).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed within fixed, conserved symmetry sectors of the Hamiltonians.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare VQE performance metrics ($r_T$, $A_H$, $\Delta E$, $F$) as a function of circuit depth and system parameters, demonstrating the dramatic improvement upon adding specific long-range or correlated gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a link between connectivity and accessibility by comparing NN-only circuits to minimally augmented circuits (NN+1 or NN+d) for both the XXZ and Hubbard models, showing algebraic reconstruction leads to physical performance gains.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Designing expressive quantum circuits for the variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) is a central challenge in simulating interacting many-body systems. Even symmetry-preserving (SP) circuits need not access the full many-body symmetry sector. We demonstrate this explicitly for the spin-$1/2$ XXZ and Hubbard models, where Hamiltonian-inspired nearest-neighbor (NN) SP circuits generate restricted dynamical Lie algebras (DLAs). While these restricted manifolds can accurately represent the ground state near the free-fermion limits, $J_z=0$ for XXZ and $U=0$ for Hubbard, interactions expose inaccessible many-body directions and severely limit VQE performance even at large circuit depth. Remarkably, a minimal modification of the generator connectivity can reconstruct the full orthogonal DLA within all relevant symmetry sectors studied in this work. For XXZ model, the addition of {\it only} one SP next-to-NN single-excitation connection triggers this dramatic reconstruction, whereas the Hubbard case requires a SP correlated double-excitation;--showing that the relevant connectivity can extend from the spatial lattice to many-body configuration space. This reconstruction enlarges the tangent space and accessibility of the energy-lowering Hamiltonian direction, leading to near-unit ground-state fidelity in VQE simulations. Our results establish a direct link between generator connectivity and variational accessibility and suggest a broader circuit-design principle: identify minimal symmetry-preserving generators that enlarge the accessible many-body space rather than increasing circuit depth alone. This perspective may have broader relevance for quantum many-body simulation at the interface of condensed-matter physics and quantum computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes quantum walks on disordered lattices with long-range hopping to understand the dimensional crossover from 1D to 2D. The key finding is that this crossover is not arbitrary but is dictated by an intrinsic transverse eigenstate scale ($\ell_e$). This establishes a fundamental link between the transport dynamics and the underlying quantum structure of the disordered system.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct connection found between the transport-derived scale ($\ell_e$) and the intrinsic eigenstate structure ($ar{\xi}$) provides a powerful, non-perturbative understanding of how dimensionality emerges in complex, disordered quantum systems, which is highly relevant to quantum simulators.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how the dimensionality of quantum transport emerges in systems with long-range, power-law hopping interactions when disorder is present.

**Main result.** The crossover from 1D to effective 2D behavior is governed by a characteristic transverse scale ($\ell_e$), which is microscopically determined by the transverse extent of the system's eigenstates.

**Method.** The study uses numerical analysis of the finite-size localization crossover filling ($ho_c$) via exponential fitting, complemented by independent spectral analysis of eigenstate spatial extents.

**Model / system.** The model consists of single-excitation quantum walks on arrays of $\ell$ coupled chains of length $L$, featuring hopping governed by a power-law decay $J_{ij} \propto |r-r'|^{-\alpha}$ and incorporating dilution-induced disorder.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal spreading width ($\eta(t)$), finite-size localization crossover filling ($ho_c$), and the characteristic transverse scale ($\ell_e$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hopping exponent ($\alpha$), transverse width ($\ell$), system length ($L$), and disorder filling ($ho$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes open boundary conditions and averages over many disorder realizations; approximations are used to relate the crossover filling to the eigenstate structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of spreading ($\eta(t)$) across different $\ell$ and $\alpha$, and compare the derived crossover scale ($\ell_e$) against the calculated transverse eigenstate extent ($ar{\xi}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the longitudinal spreading and identifies the crossover filling ($ho_c$) using finite-size scaling. It then derives the characteristic scale $\ell_e$ from this fit and independently verifies this scale by comparing it to the transverse extent of the relevant eigenstates, establishing the microscopic link.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate single-excitation quantum walks across the one-to-two-dimensional crossover in diluted power-law hopping models. Using arrays of $\ell$ coupled chains of length $L$, we identify the finite-size localization crossover from the longitudinal spreading of an initially localized excitation. The crossover filling is well described by an exponential approach to its two-dimensional value with increasing $\ell$, defining a characteristic transverse scale $\ell_e$, and effectively two-dimensional behavior emerges already for $\ell\ll L$. An independent spectral analysis shows that $\ell_e$ is set by the transverse extent of the eigenstates participating in the dynamics, providing a microscopic interpretation of the dimensional crossover. Our results reveal an intrinsic eigenstate scale governing emergent dimensionality in diluted systems with power-law hopping, a regime directly relevant to recently developed arrays of polar molecules, Rydberg atoms, and other quantum simulators with long-range interactions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates a hidden symmetry proving that the classical ground state energy and degeneracy are identical for local-moment magnets on all close-packed lattices. However, the authors show that while the classical physics is equivalent, quantum fluctuations, such as zero-point energy, are structure-dependent, suggesting that the true low-temperature order can distinguish between these seemingly equivalent lattices.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that classical symmetries break down when considering quantum fluctuations (zero-point energy) is a key concept in understanding order-by-disorder selection in frustrated quantum magnets, which is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum magnetism.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether local-moment magnets on different close-packed lattices are physically equivalent regarding their classical ground state.

**Main result.** The classical ground state energy and degeneracy are identical across all close-packed lattices, but fluctuations (zero-point energy and free energy) are structure-dependent, indicating a breakdown of symmetry beyond the classical limit.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Luttinger-Tisza approach to determine classical ground states and compares the resulting energy landscapes and degeneracy spaces across different lattice geometries.

**Model / system.** The system is a local-moment magnet described by a Heisenberg antiferromagnet Hamiltonian restricted to nearest ($J_1$) and next-nearest ($J_2$) neighbor couplings on any close-packed lattice (e.g., fcc, hcp).

**Key observables.** Ground state energy ($E_{g.s.}$), ground state degeneracy, zero-point energy ($O(S)$ correction), and free energy minimum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling ratios ($J_1/J_2$), lattice geometry (fcc, hcp, etc.), and the spin magnitude $S$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The equivalence is proven for the classical limit (Heisenberg model) and extends to XY and Ising models, but fluctuations are not related by the symmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of close-packing, construct a unified phase diagram based on $J_1$ and $J_2$, and compare the location of LT minima and the calculated zero-point energy across different structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the hidden symmetry relating classical ground states via the Luttinger-Tisza method, constructing a unified phase diagram. It then analyzes the degeneracy and structure of the ground states in different phases, concluding by contrasting the robust classical equivalence with the structure-dependent nature of quantum fluctuations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Heisenberg antiferromagnet on the fcc lattice is a canonical example of frustration. The fcc lattice is but one of an infinite number of close-packed structures, all of which have the same frustration-inducing local geometry. In this letter, we demonstrate a hidden symmetry that relates classical magnets on all close-packed lattices. This symmetry derives from a similar property in tight binding models that may explain the disordered low-temperature structures of lithium and sodium metals. In magnets, the symmetry holds when couplings are restricted to nearest ($J_1$) and next-nearest neighbours ($J_2$). It guarantees that all close-packed lattices are equivalent at the Luttinger-Tisza level. For any given values of $J_1$ and $J_2$, they have the same ground-state energy. Their ground state momenta are identical when projected onto the stacking plane. We construct a unified phase diagram as a function of $J_1$ and $J_2$ couplings for Heisenberg, XY and Ising magnets on any close-packed lattice. In each phase, the ground-state degeneracy is the same on all close-packed lattices, even after accounting for multi-Q spirals. This phase diagram can be easily adapted to any magnet composed of stacked triangular layers with similar couplings. Fluctuations above the classical ground state(s) are not related by any symmetry. This suggests that close-packed structures can show interesting differences in order-by-disorder selection.

</details>

### [Measurement Complexity of Quantum Compressed Sensing](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08234v1)

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes the measurement complexity of Quantum Compressed Sensing (QCS) compared to classical methods. It shows that QCS leverages quantum parallelism to map sparse signals onto measurement indices, establishing lower bounds for the required effective index samples ($M_s$). The findings clarify the quantum resource advantage in measurement science for sparse signal recovery tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous theoretical framework for quantifying the information-theoretic advantage of quantum measurement techniques over classical ones in the context of sparse signal recovery.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares the measurement complexity of Quantum Compressed Sensing (QCS) against classical Compressed Sensing (CS) to determine the quantum advantage in signal recovery.

**Main result.** QCS establishes lower bounds on measurement complexity, showing that while it can surpass classical bounds in some regimes, the required effective index sample complexity ($M_s$) scales as $\Theta(K \ln K)$ for exact support recovery.

**Method.** The authors establish lower bounds using information-theoretic and quantum-physical perspectives, analyzing the required effective index samples ($M_s$) under various recovery objectives.

**Model / system.** The model compares classical CS (non-adaptive linear measurements) with QCS, which utilizes quantum parallelism via a unitary domain-alignment evolution acting on a superposition of $N$ basis states.

**Key observables.** Effective index samples ($M_s$), number of experimental rounds ($M$), and the required signal dimension ($N$) and sparsity ($K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sparsity ($K$), signal dimension ($N$), error tolerance ($\epsilon$), and the classical lower bound $\Omega(K \log(N/K))$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a known sparse basis, unknown support set, and relies on specific measurement models (e.g., independent single-index projective measurements).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the classical CS lower bound, then details the QCS protocol involving unitary evolution and index-resolved measurements, and finally derives the scaling laws for $M_s$ based on the required recovery task (statistics, support, amplitude).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional compressed sensing (CS) has a measurement lower bound of M = Ω(K log(N/K)) under non-adaptive measurements. Recent experiments on quantum compressed sensing (QCS) have reported numbers of measurements below this classical lower bound. In this work, we establish lower bounds on the measurement complexity of QCS from an information-theoretic and quantum-physical perspective. QCS exploits quantum parallelism, enabling a unitary domain-alignment evolution to act on a superposition of all N basis states and map the sparse basis onto the measurement basis in a one-to-one manner. Consequently, the locations of nonzero components are explicitly carried by measurement-index labels. If only basic statistical information about the K nonzero components is required, the total number of effective index samples satisfies the coarse necessary condition Ms = Ω(K). Under independent single-index projective measurements, when the support is unknown, the nonzero probabilities satisfy pn = Θ(1/K), and exact support recovery is required with a fixed success probability, the sampling-coverage requirement leads to Ms = Θ(K ln K). If each nonzero amplitude coefficient is further required to achieve a coordinate-wise relative root-mean-square error ε, the optimal effective index sample complexity is Ms = Θ(K ln K + K/ε2), which reduces to Θ(K ln K) for fixed ε. Multimode photon-number-resolving detection can reduce the number of experimental rounds but not the total effective index sample complexity. These results show that quantum parallelism is not only a core resource for quantum computing but can also play a transformative role in measurement science.

</details>

### [Operational quasiprobability formulation of non-signaling in time for macrorealism violations](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08146v1)

**Authors:** Stefano Gherardini, Paolo Solinas  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08146v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.08146_figures/2610.08146_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1: Illustration of the relation between MRqp and MRstr. The positivity of P(Ω), achieved if and only if MRps is obeyed, entails that the NSIT conditions of Eq. (1) are satisfied, thus certifying the MRstr. The reverse is not generally true.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quasi non-signaling in time (QNSIT) and a corresponding quasiprobabilistic criterion for macrorealism ($	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}}$). It demonstrates that $	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}}$ is a stricter test than standard Non-Signaling in Time (NSIT), offering a more sensitive tool to detect violations of macrorealism, particularly through quantum non-demolition measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, operational framework using quasiprobabilities to test fundamental assumptions about physical reality (macrorealism) in time, which is crucial for understanding the limits of classical descriptions of quantum measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a more sensitive operational test for macrorealism violations than the standard Non-Signaling in Time (NSIT) conditions.

**Main result.** The proposed criterion, $	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}}$, is strictly stronger than NSIT, although for two-level systems, it becomes equivalent to the standard macrorealism condition ($	ext{MR}_{	ext{str}}$).

**Method.** The authors introduce a quasiprobabilistic characterization of macrorealism ($	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}}$) based on a real multi-time quasiprobability density function $P(\Omega)$.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers a general quantum system undergoing sequential measurements at distinct times ($t_0, t_1, t_2$), involving system and detector degrees of freedom.

**Key observables.** The quasiprobability distribution $P(\Omega)$ and its positivity, which serves as the diagnostic tool for $	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system Hamiltonian, measurement projectors $\{\hat{\Pi}(X)\}$, and the time evolution operators $\hat{U}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on extending the concept to quasiprobabilities and assuming the validity of the QNSIT conditions, which are shown to be always satisfied.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QNSIT and $	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}}$, proves $	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}} \implies 	ext{NSIT}$, and then analyzes the conditions under which the equivalence holds, showing the strictness of $	ext{MR}_{	ext{qp}}$ in general dimensions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we introduce the concept of quasi non-signaling in time (QNSIT), which extends non-signaling in time (NSIT) to quasiprobability distributions. QNSIT requires that the marginals of the quasiprobability distribution under consideration at intermediate times remain unperturbed by the measurement back-action induced by a sequence of quantum measurements. Associated with QNSIT, we propose a quasiprobabilistic characterization of macrorealism, ${\rm MR}_{\rm qp}$, which requires that both the QNSIT conditions are satisfied and the quasiprobability distribution constructed from the measurement outcomes is positive. We prove that ${\rm MR}_{\rm qp}$ implies NSIT, whereas the converse does not hold because of hidden superposition paths generated or preserved by the system dynamics. We also prove that, for a two-level system, the ${\rm MR}_{\rm qp}$ and NSIT conditions coincide. ${\rm MR}_{\rm qp}$ therefore provides a more sensitive tool for testing macrorealism and can be implemented experimentally through a quantum non-demolition measurement scheme.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the critical engineering bottleneck of manually designing superconducting quantum chips. The authors introduce a novel, formal geometric abstraction and a three-stage automated design flow to solve this. The resulting open-source tool generates complete, manufacturable chip layouts rapidly, enabling the scaling of quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenge of scaling quantum hardware, which is crucial for realizing complex quantum algorithms and large-scale quantum computation architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The manual layout of superconducting quantum chips is a major bottleneck, making scaling difficult and time-consuming.

**Main result.** The authors propose and demonstrate an end-to-end, fully automatic design flow that generates manufacturing-ready layouts for superconducting quantum chips up to 69 qubits, achieving speedups up to 25x over state-of-the-art methods.

**Method.** The approach involves creating a formal geometric abstraction that translates physical device characteristics into a rigorous problem formulation, followed by a three-stage automated flow: global partitioning, ILP-based port assignment, and hierarchical routing.

**Model / system.** The system is planar superconducting quantum chips (SCQC) containing qubits, couplers, and launchers, which are patterned lithographically. The design is modeled as a geometric composition of components, governed by physical constraints like minimum bending radii and crosstalk separation.

**Key observables.** Manufacturing-ready layouts, execution times (seconds to minutes), minimum wire clearance (e.g., 185 µm), and successful routing of complex connectivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating frequencies (4 GHz to 8 GHz), cryogenic temperatures (10 mK to 20 mK), and impedance (50 Ω for CPWs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary focus is on the geometric abstraction and automated flow, assuming that the initial placement of components is valid; the flow is designed for planar architectures, though the abstraction is extensible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the SCQC layout architecture, detailing component types (qubits, couplers, launchers) and showing the abstracted representations of small and medium-sized quantum chip layouts.

**Paper structure.** The paper identifies the manual layout bottleneck, introduces a formal geometric abstraction bridging physics and automation, details the three-stage design flow (partitioning, port assignment, routing), and validates the flow with performance comparisons against prior art.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconducting circuits have emerged as one of the most promising and scalable platforms for quantum com- puting, with substantial industrial adoption driving qubit counts steadily upward. Yet the layout of the corresponding chips is still primarily performed manually, consuming multiple days or weeks of expert effort, even for moderately sized designs. Existing automation approaches sidestep this bottleneck by simplifying the underlying problem and relaxing physical constraints. Therefore, they fall short of fabrication-ready results. To overcome this scalability wall without such compromises, we first introduce a formal abstraction that translates physical device characteristics into a rigorous geometric problem formulation. This abstraction bridges physical realization and design automation. Building upon this abstraction, we then propose a three-stage design automation flow comprising geometry-aware global partitioning, ILP-based port assignment, and a hierarchical routing pipeline. Evaluations conducted by our interdisciplinary team of design au- tomation researchers and superconducting hardware experts con- firm that the resulting flow produces complete, manufacturing- ready layouts that comply with the physical design rules at the push of a button. That is, it operates fully automatically within seconds to a few minutes, and solves complex instances up to 25x faster than the state of the art. All methods are released as the fully open-source tool mqt-scpd as part of the Munich Quantum Toolkit, providing the first readily usable, end- to-end design flow for planar superconducting quantum chips that explicitly addresses the underlying physical characteristics and requirements.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper rigorously analyzes the concept of quantum dynamical divisibility, especially when the evolution maps are not invertible. It establishes that Heisenberg CP-divisibility is equivalent to an operator-norm monotonicity condition, providing necessary and sufficient mathematical criteria for consistent time evolution. The findings are important for understanding the mathematical structure of open quantum systems beyond the standard unitary or trace-preserving assumptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools to analyze the consistency of quantum evolution when the system dynamics are non-unitary or non-invertible, which is crucial for modeling open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mathematical conditions for the divisibility of quantum dynamics, particularly when the underlying dynamical maps are non-invertible in the Heisenberg picture.

**Main result.** Heisenberg CP-divisibility is characterized by an operator-norm monotonicity condition, and for qubit dynamics, this guarantees a completely positive generalized-inverse propagator.

**Method.** The authors use functional analysis, comparing standard divisibility definitions with necessary kernel inclusion conditions for non-invertible maps, and employing generalized inverse constructions.

**Model / system.** The study concerns general finite-dimensional quantum dynamics on matrix algebras ($M_d$), represented by dynamical maps ($\Lambda_t$ in Schrödinger picture, $\Lambda_t^*$ in Heisenberg picture).

**Key observables.** Divisibility (existence of intermediate maps), CP-divisibility, operator-norm monotonicity, and the structure of reduced propagators on accessible operator systems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the system ($d$), and the time evolution parameter $t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often proceeds by assuming the existence of reduced propagators or by analyzing the failure of these assumptions when maps are non-invertible.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the failure of standard divisibility definitions for non-invertible maps, then characterizing Heisenberg CP-divisibility via norm inequalities and kernel inclusions, and finally developing gauge-invariant formulations to handle singular time generators.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study divisibility of finite-dimensional quantum dynamics in the Heisenberg picture when the dynamical maps need not be invertible. Heisenberg divisible dynamics determines unique reduced propagators on the accessible operator systems, and Heisenberg CP-divisibility is shown to be equivalent to an operator-norm monotonicity. Although each reduced propagator admits a unital completely positive extension, independently chosen extensions need not satisfy a composition law. Generalized inverses restore this law algebraically, but do not, in general, preserve complete positivity. We characterize the resulting obstruction and give a sufficient construction based on unital completely positive projections onto the accessible operator systems. It is shown that for qubit dynamics Heisenberg CP-divisibility always guarantees a completely positive generalized-inverse propagator. Finally, we analyze time-local generators at singular times and identify the associated gauge freedom on dynamically inaccessible directions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reinterprets the black hole Page transition using quantum relative entropy, treating it as a flow of information-theoretic costs between two competing semi-classical gravitational descriptions. By analyzing how the relative entropy changes over time, the authors establish an information-theoretic criterion that precisely diagnoses the transition point where the description shifts from the Hawking saddle to the replica wormholes saddle.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on gravity, the use of relative entropy flow to diagnose a transition point based on the relative efficiency of two competing descriptions is a powerful, generalizable tool for analyzing non-equilibrium processes in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the information-theoretic structure underlying the Page transition during black hole evaporation by comparing competing semi-classical gravitational descriptions.

**Main result.** The Page transition is recast as a flow of relative entropy costs between the Hawking saddle and the replica wormholes saddle, with the sign change of the difference in relative entropy diagnosing the transition.

**Method.** The authors develop a computational framework using the quantum relative entropy, calculated via the replica trick, to quantify the information-theoretic cost associated with approximating the exact quantum state.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed on Jackiw-Teitelboim (JT) gravity applied to an End-of-the-World (EoW) brane (PSSY model), modeling black hole evaporation.

**Key observables.** Relative Entropy (RE) between the exact radiation state and the two saddle states, and the difference in these relative entropies ($\Delta S$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Page time, which marks the sign reversal of $\Delta S$; the replica index $n$ and the large $k$ limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the support of the exact state is contained within the support of the saddle states, and the effective irreversibility is not taken to imply a breakdown of fundamental unitarity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of the relative entropies for both the Hawking and wormhole saddles, showing the monotonic increase of the Hawking cost and the decay of the wormhole cost, culminating in a sign change for $\Delta S$ at the Page time.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the framework by defining the RE using the replica trick, applies it to the two competing semiclassical saddles ($\sigma_{	ext{Haw}}$ and $\sigma_{	ext{WH}}$), derives explicit time-dependent expressions for the REs, and finally interprets the flow of these costs across the Page transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a computational framework for the quantum relative entropy based on the replica trick and apply it to analyze the evaporation process method of Jackiw-Teitelboim black holes. We show that the relative entropies between the exact radiation state and the Hawking saddle state and the replica wormholes saddle state quantify information-theoretic costs inherent to coarse-grained semiclassical descriptions. During evaporation, the relative entropy for the Hawking saddle increases monotonically, reflecting a progressively higher thermodynamic cost of sustaining the Hawking description. Conversely, the relative entropy associated with the replica wormholes saddle decreases, which indicates that the description of connected geometries becomes progressively more accurate. Moreover, the sign of the difference between the two quantities reverses precisely at the Page time, providing an information-theoretic diagnosis for the transition between competing gravitational saddles. Our results recast the Page transition as a flow of relative entropy costs between competing semi-classical descriptions, revealing an information-theoretic structure underlying the emergence of effective irreversibility in coarse-grained black hole evaporation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an Inertial Counterdiabatic (CD) driving protocol that improves quantum control by treating the control problem as a moving-frame diagonalization task. It uses the Magnus expansion to account for accumulated errors across time steps, surpassing standard methods. The technique is shown to be particularly effective for fast driving near quantum phase transitions in spin chain models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a sophisticated, non-local theoretical tool for designing quantum control pulses, which is highly relevant for controlling complex quantum systems like superconducting qubits or trapped ions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of achieving high-fidelity quantum state preparation faster than the adiabatic limit using coherent control techniques.

**Main result.** The novel Inertial Counterdiabatic (CD) protocol consistently outperforms standard action-minimization variational CD protocols, especially near quantum gap-closing regions.

**Method.** It reformulates the control problem as a moving-frame diagonalization problem, utilizing the Magnus expansion to construct a variational propagator that accounts for accumulated diabatic errors.

**Model / system.** The method is benchmarked on a general two-qubit system and the one-dimensional quantum Ising chain, allowing for the study of controlled dynamics in interacting quantum spin systems.

**Key observables.** Infidelity ($1 - F(t)$), suppression of off-diagonal Hamiltonian terms, and the final ground state fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization hyperparameters, such as the penalty coefficient ($\mu$) and the number of time discretization steps ($M$), are crucial for performance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation involves using the Magnus expansion to approximate the time evolution operator, and the standard CD approach relies on local, instantaneous optimization.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the performance of different CD schemes (Action-based vs. Inertial) and show the dependence of fidelity and control amplitude on the number of time steps ($M$) and regularization strength ($\mu$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the general CD framework, contrasts it with standard variational methods, details the Inertial CD approach using the Magnus expansion, benchmarks it on two-qubit and Ising models, and analyzes the role of nonlocality near critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce an inertial counterdiabatic (CD) protocol that reformulates shortcut-to-adiabaticity design as a moving-frame diagonalization problem. The method employs the Magnus expansion to construct a variational propagator generated by an accessible CD ansatz. In the new rotating frame, the controls are optimized to suppress the off-diagonal Hamiltonian terms, and the CD terms are defined to counteract the corresponding inertial contribution. The propagator generated with the Magnus expansion naturally exploits additional control directions generated through its nested commutator, while the sequential design of the propagator introduces temporal nonlocality into the optimization. We benchmark the method on a two-qubit system and on the one-dimensional quantum Ising chain, where it consistently outperforms variational CD protocols based on action minimization. The largest improvements are observed near gap-closing regions, demonstrating the advantage of combining temporal and algebraic nonlocality for suppressing diabatic excitations.

</details>

### [Shape theorem for excited random walks on the integer lattice](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07724v1)

**Authors:** Tuan-Minh Nguyen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.07724v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.07724_figures/2610.07724_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIGURE 1. Simulation of the random walk excited to the center on Z2 with θ = 0.5 after n = 1,500,000 steps. Colors indicate the first-visit time divided by n. The dashed curve is the boundary of the predicted ℓ1 ball of radius (3n/(4θ))1/3.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives the precise limit shape for the spatial extent (range) of a specialized random walk on the integer lattice. The walk incorporates a memory effect via 'cookies' that bias movement toward the origin upon first visit. The key result shows this range converges to a specific $\ell^1$ ball, using advanced tools from potential theory and probability theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical random walks, the techniques involving scaling limits, local time analysis, and shape theorems are highly relevant to understanding the spatial extent and collective behavior of excitations in many-body quantum systems or non-equilibrium quantum dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine the limit shape of the range of a specific type of random walk on the integer lattice $\mathbb{Z}^d$.

**Main result.** The rescaled range of the walk converges in probability in Hausdorff distance to an explicit $\ell^1$ ball, providing an answer to a question posed by Kozma (2007).

**Method.** The proof involves bounding expected range sizes using martingale decomposition and studying the local times of the walk via potential theory and integral equations.

**Model / system.** The model is the "random walk excited to the center" on $\mathbb{Z}^d$ ($d\ge 2$). The walk's transition rules depend on whether a cookie is available (first visit to a non-zero site, causing a bias toward the origin) or if the walker is at the origin or has visited the site before (simple symmetric random walk).

**Key observables.** The range of the walk up to time $n$, $R_n$, and its rescaled limit shape.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d \ge 2$, the bias parameter $\theta$, and the time scaling factor $n^{-1/(d+1)}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing convergence in probability using Hausdorff distance and involves complex control over local times and martingale inequalities.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 simulates the random walk on $\mathbb{Z}^2$ and compares the observed range boundary to the theoretically predicted $\ell^1$ ball boundary.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing bounds on expected range size using martingale methods, then analyzing the rescaled range using the Poisson equation, and finally proving the main shape theorem by controlling the Hausdorff distance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the limit shape of the range of the random walk excited to the center on $\mathbb Z^d$, with $d\geq2$. A cookie is placed at each nonzero vertex. On its first visit to such a vertex, the walker consumes the cookie and takes its next nearest-neighbor step with a bias toward the origin. On a visit to the origin, or on later visits to a nonzero vertex, no cookie is available and the walker moves to a neighbor uniformly at random. We prove that the set of vertices visited up to time $n$, rescaled by $n^{-1/(d+1)}$, converges in probability in Hausdorff distance to an explicit $\ell^1$ ball. This answers a question raised by Kozma (2007).

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a method to incorporate the quantum effects of a black hole horizon into the classical geometry by treating the environment as a conserved, deterministic source. This 'dressing' modifies key geometric features, such as the horizon location and particle orbits, establishing a necessary mean-field background for future stochastic quantum gravity calculations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on general relativity, the methodology of deriving a deterministic mean-field background from an environmental source, which is then used as the starting point for a subsequent stochastic treatment (Einstein-Langevin), mirrors techniques used in open quantum systems theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses whether the inherent quantum openness of a black hole horizon, suggested by Hawking radiation, can be incorporated deterministically into the gravitational background geometry before resorting to full stochastic quantum gravity treatments.

**Main result.** The authors construct a deterministic, dressed mean-field geometry by introducing a conserved, anisotropic source representing the near-horizon quantum atmosphere, which modifies the horizon location and orbital dynamics in a correlated manner.

**Method.** They solve the Einstein equations for a static black hole by adding an effective, conserved stress-energy tensor derived from the quantum atmosphere, thereby defining a mean-field background geometry.

**Model / system.** The system is a black hole geometry, initially Schwarzschild, which is modified by the inclusion of a conserved, Hawking-scale anisotropic source ($T_{\mu
u}$) representing the coarse-grained quantum environment.

**Key observables.** Renormalized mass function, self-consistently shifted horizon ($\Delta r_h$), modified surface gravity, and changes in the Innermost Stable Circular Orbit (ISCO) radius.

**Important parameters / regimes.** ADM mass ($M$), the strength of the environmental coupling ($\epsilon$), and the exponent $n$ characterizing the source decay.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses only on the deterministic mean-field sector, treating the environmental effect as a conserved source rather than a stochastic noise term; the source is assumed to be localized near the Schwarzschild radius.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mean-field dressing by comparing the Schwarzschild and dressed geometries, showing the shift in the horizon ($\Delta r_h$), and demonstrating the inward drift of the ISCO radius correlated with the thermal response.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the problem of horizon openness, then solving the Einstein equations with the conserved source to find the mean geometry, and finally analyzing the consequences for thermodynamics (horizon shift) and dynamics (ISCO drift) before outlining the necessary next step into the Einstein-Langevin framework.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Black holes are usually described either as classical geometries on which quantum fields propagate or as objects whose complete quantum description lies beyond semiclassical gravity. Hawking radiation already shows, however, that a black hole is not an exactly isolated system: the horizon carries a thermal scale and, after coarse graining, may be associated with fluctuations, dissipation and environmental correlations. Whether this horizon-scale openness can be encoded directly in the gravitational background, before any stochastic treatment of the metric, remains an open question. Here we answer it by introducing, for the first time, a conserved Hawking-scale anisotropic source representing the coarse-grained near-horizon atmosphere and solving the Einstein equations for a static black hole at fixed ADM mass. The dressed geometry has a renormalized mass function, a self-consistently shifted horizon and a modified surface gravity. Because the same lapse governs the horizon and timelike geodesics, the thermal response is accompanied by a correlated orbital restructuring: the effective potential is renormalized, the ISCO moves inward and the near-horizon residence time increases. The construction adds no Langevin force to a fixed Schwarzschild background; rather, it defines a deterministic dressed mean geometry, providing a controlled background on which stochastic metric fluctuations may subsequently be treated in the Einstein-Langevin framework. This establishes the deterministic mean-field sector as the necessary first step for future stochastic gravity programs addressing horizon-scale openness.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes anomalous 'waterfall-like' spectral features in a kagome metal using ARPES and DMFT. The authors argue that these features, which appear near a Van Hove Singularity, are likely caused by inelastic scattering of photoelectrons rather than requiring strong electronic correlations. This suggests that photoelectron energy-loss processes must be considered when interpreting spectral features in correlated materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the analysis of spectral function broadening and the distinction between intrinsic (self-energy) and extrinsic (photoelectron scattering) effects is highly relevant to understanding spectral signatures in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the origin of anomalous 'waterfall-like' spectral features observed in ARPES spectra of a kagome metal, determining if they stem from strong electronic correlations or from other physical mechanisms.

**Main result.** The authors propose that the spectral weight enhancement is likely due to inelastic scattering of outgoing photoelectrons near a Van Hove Singularity, rather than solely requiring strong electronic correlations.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements (ARPES, DFT) with advanced theoretical calculations (DMFT) and modeling of photoemission energy-loss processes.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the kagome metal LuFe6Ge6, a member of the FeGe-derived family, whose electronic structure is analyzed using DFT and DMFT frameworks.

**Key observables.** Pronounced vertical spectral feature (waterfall) at the L point, coincidence of feature onset with a Van Hove Singularity (vHs), and quasiparticle weights (Z).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The energy scale of the vHs ($\approx 0.3 	ext{ eV}$ below $E_F$), and the degree of electronic correlation quantified by DMFT parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption tested is whether the spectral weight can be explained by inelastic scattering processes involving the high density of states near the vHs, rather than solely by intrinsic self-energy renormalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize ARPES band dispersions along high-symmetry lines, showing the anomalous feature, and theoretical models illustrate how self-energies can generate waterfall features.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first presenting experimental observations (ARPES/DFT) of the waterfall feature, then applying DMFT to check for correlation signatures, and finally proposing and modeling the inelastic scattering mechanism as the source of the spectral weight.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Waterfall-like spectral features observed by angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) are commonly associated with strong electronic correlations or electron-boson coupling. Here, we investigate this connection in the kagome metal LuFe6Ge6, a structurally simple member of the FeGe-derived family, using polarization-dependent ARPES, density functional theory and dynamical mean-field theory. We observe a pronounced vertical spectral feature at the L point extending over several hundred meV in binding energy. Its orbital character is identified as predominantly Fe dyz, and its energy onset coincides with a van Hove singularity located approximately 0.3 eV below EF . Despite the waterfall-like spectral response, dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) reveals only weak electronic correlations, while the calculated electronic structure closely reproduces the experimental bulk bands. We propose that the anomalous spectral weight may arise from inelastic scattering of the outgoing photoelectrons, potentially associated with the large density of states near the van Hove singularity. Our results suggest that waterfall-like features can emerge without strong electronic correlations and highlight the possible role of the underlying band structure and photoelectron energy-loss processes in their interpretation.

</details>
