**Summary.** This paper rigorously examines the eigenstate quantum-mechanical bootstrap, demonstrating that even when moment constraints converge to the physical spectrum, they do so without ever achieving exactness at any finite computational step. Using a sextic oscillator as a key example, the authors show that persistent gaps exist between the calculated bounds and the true physical value. This highlights fundamental limitations in using finite moment data to fully characterize quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep theoretical insights into the limitations of moment-based reconstruction techniques in quantum mechanics, which is crucial for understanding the robustness of spectral determination methods in quantum field theory and many-body physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the convergence properties of the eigenstate quantum-mechanical bootstrap method when applied to determining physical quantum spectra.

**Main result.** It proves that the bootstrap can converge to the physical moment set without achieving exactness at any finite computational order, demonstrating persistent gaps between finite bounds and the true physical value.

**Method.** The analysis employs moment methods, semidefinite programming (SDP) constraints derived from operator identities, and functional analysis on the Weyl algebra to constrain quantum expectation values.

**Model / system.** The primary model analyzed is a one-dimensional quasi-exactly solvable sextic oscillator, though the methods are generalized to confining polynomial Schrödinger operators.

**Key observables.** The centered observable $u^2$, and the moment bounds $\langle q^{2n}_j angle$ and $\langle p^{2n}_j angle$ for position and momentum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The truncation order ($K$) of the moment space, and the physical energy eigenvalue ($e$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The convergence theorems rely on specific polynomial hypotheses (sum-of-squares conditions) being satisfied by the potential, and the analysis often involves assuming the existence of a compact resolvent.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not describe specific figures, but mention numerical tables showing the convergence of objective functions for different truncation orders ($K=3, 4, 5$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the non-exactness of finite bounds for the sextic oscillator, then proving uniform moment bounds for general confining potentials, and finally extending the analysis to show that the failure of finite exactness persists even with additional localizing constraints.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The eigenstate quantum-mechanical bootstrap can converge to the physical moment set while remaining nonexact at every finite order. We prove this for a one-dimensional quasi-exactly solvable sextic oscillator at explicitly known eigenvalues, including excited levels. At every finite order, the lower and upper bounds on $u^2$ remain strictly below and above its physical value, even after adjoining arbitrary finite polynomial Gram tests and all eigenstate-annihilator relations visible in the retained moment space. Hence neither side of the centered observable admits a finite sum-of-squares certificate modulo the eigenstate annihilator. The gap persists under any finite set of additional localizing constraints whose weights are strictly positive on polynomial vectors. We then prove convergence for polynomial Schrödinger operators satisfying explicit sum-of-squares confinement and derivative bounds. The eigenstate relations give moment estimates uniform in the truncation order; these yield a regular Schrödinger representation supported on the prescribed eigenspace. Finally, a four-mode extension combines this fixed-observable obstruction with feasible position moments having no positive representing measure at every finite order. At level three, this construction yields nonphysical feasible points with strictly positive definite moment matrices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work challenges the assumption that the difficulty of quantum annealing (spectral gap) must correlate with the entanglement complexity of the system. By analyzing various spin models and graph structures, the authors provide explicit counterexamples showing that these two quantities can scale independently. This suggests that a more sophisticated geometric characterization of the state evolution is needed to understand quantum resource requirements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep theoretical understanding of the resources required for quantum computation, suggesting that geometric properties of the state evolution manifold, rather than just gap size, are the critical bottleneck.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the difficulty of adiabatic quantum annealing, quantified by the minimum spectral gap, must correlate with the complexity of the quantum state traversed, measured by entanglement complexity.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that gap scaling and entanglement complexity are not uniquely linked, showing examples where one scales differently from the other.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical constructions, including $p$-spin models, bipartite graph models, and random graph instances, to calculate spectral gaps and entanglement entropy.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum annealing processes applied to various spin models, including the fully connected $p$-spin model and structured optimization problems on bipartite and random graphs.

**Key observables.** Minimum spectral gap ($\Delta_{\min}$), Bipartite entanglement entropy ($S$), and Schmidt rank.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$), interaction order ($p$), and the annealing parameter ($s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing different theoretical regimes (first-order vs. continuous transitions) and analyzing specific Hamiltonian structures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate finite-size scaling of $\Delta_{\min}$ and $S_{\max}$ for $p$-spin models, and show entanglement evolution across bipartitions for graph models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem using $p$-spin models, analyzes two conventional regimes (exponential gap/bounded entanglement vs. polynomial gap/logarithmic entanglement), and then provides counterexamples using structured and random graph models to decouple the two quantities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exponentially small spectral gaps are the central obstacle to adiabatic quantum annealing, yet whether such bottlenecks demand correspondingly complex entanglement remains unclear. We show that gap scaling and entanglement complexity need not be linked. Conventional first-order transitions combine exponentially small gaps with bounded $O(1)$ bipartite Schmidt rank, even though the critical states retain global multipartite correlations, while continuous transitions combine polynomially closing gaps with logarithmically growing entanglement. We show that each pairing can also be inverted: in a known first-order transition in a staggered-field antiferromagnetic Ising chain the gap closes polynomially despite bounded Schmidt rank, while we construct a mirror-twin manifold model whose gap closes exponentially even as its entanglement entropy grows logarithmically with system size. We derive bounds connecting Schmidt rank to Hamming-space geometry, and identify manifold density and connectivity as the ingredients that determine which combination occurs. These results show that entanglement complexity alone cannot characterize annealing difficulty, and point toward a broader search for quantum resources that capture the geometry of state evolution in Hilbert space.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides an analytical model to optimize quantum repeater chains for long-distance entanglement distribution by accounting for memory decoherence. It compares three scheduling strategies—Parallel, Tree, and Hybrid—to determine the entanglement rate as a function of repeater count. The key finding is that an optimal number of repeaters exists, balancing the benefits of shorter links against the cumulative decoherence penalty.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical limitations of scaling quantum networks by quantifying the detrimental effects of decoherence and classical communication delays on entanglement distribution protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops an analytical model to estimate the impact of decoherence during the heralding process in quantum repeaters used for long-distance entanglement distribution.

**Main result.** The analysis reveals a fundamental trade-off: while increasing the number of repeaters boosts entanglement generation, the additive penalty from memory decoherence eventually dominates, proving the existence of an optimal repeater count.

**Method.** The authors developed an analytical framework comparing three scheduling strategies (Binary-Tree, Parallel, Hybrid) to calculate the entanglement rate by accounting for memory exposure during entanglement generation and swapping.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a homogeneous linear chain of N quantum repeaters connecting two end nodes over a total distance L_tot. The process involves sequential entanglement generation (EG) followed by entanglement swapping (ES) operations.

**Key observables.** Entanglement rate (measured using logarithmic negativity) and the optimal number of repeaters (N).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Memory decoherence time ($\tau$), total distance ($L_{tot}$), and the number of repeaters ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to prepare-and-swap strategies, and it models memory decoherence as an exponential time-decay of the Werner parameter.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the entanglement rate vs. N for a metropolitan scenario across different $\tau$; Figure 2 shows the rate vs. N for an intermetropolitan scenario, demonstrating an optimal N exists.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and the need for an analytical framework. It then details the comparison of three scheduling strategies (Parallel, Tree, Hybrid) by quantifying memory exposure during the two main phases (generation and swapping). Finally, it presents results showing the dependence of the optimal repeater count on the decoherence time and distance scale.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

First-generation quantum repeaters are essential in distributing entanglement over long distances since they could mitigate losses in optical fiber. Repeaters split the total distance into shorter, easier to generate links. Then, entanglement swapping establishes long range entanglement by performing local measurements at the repeaters and heralding their results. In this work, we develop an analytical model that estimates the impact of decoherence during heralding for a given protocol. Furthermore, we identify a competition between two phenomena: as repeater count increases, so does entanglement generation, but the additive impact of memory decoherence on final entanglement rate increases too. Therefore, we find that an optimal number of repeaters exists. These findings could lead the design of future quantum networks, both in the inter-repeater distance and swapping timings.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work calculates advanced correlation corrections (self-energy and vertex diagrams) to the static screening of a 2D electron gas. The key finding is that these exchange corrections provide a significant quantitative and qualitative improvement over standard RPA theory. This improvement is demonstrated by sharpening the Kohn anomaly and enhancing the long-range Friedel oscillations, offering deeper insight into electron-electron interactions in low dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed study of correlation effects beyond RPA in 2D systems provides crucial benchmarks for understanding strongly correlated electron behavior, which is relevant to high-Tc superconductors and quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper calculates the leading beyond-Random-Phase-Approximation (RPA) corrections to the static irreducible polarizability of the two-dimensional electron gas to accurately determine the static dielectric function and screened interaction.

**Main result.** Exchange corrections significantly improve screening predictions by converting the Kohn kink into a sharp two-sided peak and enhancing Friedel oscillations in real space compared to RPA. The combined self-energy and vertex corrections are found to be finite and reproduce the Hartree-Fock compressibility sum rule.

**Method.** The authors analytically compute the two exchange self-energy and one exchange vertex diagram, summing them to obtain the full polarizability correction ($\Pi_1$). They then use this to derive the static dielectric function $\epsilon(q)$ and analyze the small-$q$ limit.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG) interacting via the bare Coulomb potential. The analysis focuses on the static screening properties, which are governed by the polarizability $\Pi(q)$ and the resulting screened interaction $W(q)$.

**Key observables.** Static dielectric function $\epsilon(q)$, screened interaction $W(q)$, Friedel oscillation amplitude, and the local-field factor $G_x(q)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless momentum $x = q/k_F$, the Wigner-Seitz radius parameter $r_s$, and the Fermi wave vector $k_F$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation is performed in the static limit and uses first-order perturbation theory for the corrections. The analysis compares the systematic $O(r_s)$ result against approximations like the Hubbard model.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the RPA vs. RPA + exchange corrections for the polarizability ($\Pi$), show the asymptotic form of the polarizability, and illustrate the enhanced, growing Friedel oscillations in real space ($\delta n(r)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the corrections to the polarizability, analyzes the resulting asymptotic behavior in momentum space, compares the exact result against simpler approximations (like Hubbard), and discusses the physical consequences for Friedel oscillations and pairing instabilities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We compute analytically the leading beyond-ring corrections to the static irreducible polarizability of the two-dimensional (2D) electron gas, namely the two exchange self-energy diagrams and the exchange vertex diagram, each of first order in the Coulomb coupling, and insert the resulting polarizability into the RPA geometric series to obtain the static dielectric function and screened interaction. The self-energy and vertex contributions individually diverge; we obtain both divergent pieces in closed form. Their sum is finite and reproduces exactly the Hartree-Fock compressibility, which renormalizes the Thomas-Fermi wave vector. We show that exchange converts the one-sided 2D Kohn kink at 2k_F into a sharp two-sided peak, a qualitative improvement over Thomas-Fermi and RPA screening. In real space this makes the Friedel oscillations around a charged impurity 2-3 times larger than in RPA, with an amplitude that increases with distance over the range studied; implications for Kohn-Luttinger pairing are discussed. Relation to earlier works on the same diagrams is discussed.

</details>

**Type:** both · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.07196v1>

**Summary.** This paper explores using Quantum Variational Autoencoders (QVAEs) to learn structured and interpretable representations from quantum data. By analyzing the latent space structure and testing on synthetic spectral data, the authors prove that QVAEs can successfully disentangle generative factors, assigning distinct physical meanings to individual qubits. This establishes a foundation for applying quantum ML to complex physical modeling.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum machine learning with physical interpretation, showing how quantum circuits can enforce structure (disentanglement) on latent variables, which is highly relevant for modeling complex quantum many-body systems where factorized representations are sought.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how Quantum Variational Autoencoders (QVAEs) can learn disentangled and semantically interpretable latent factors from complex data, addressing the challenge of defining individual quantum latent dimensions.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that QVAEs can successfully discover factorized and interpretable latent representations, showing that individual qubits can function as meaningful latent factors for tasks like encoding spectral line parameters.

**Method.** The approach involves developing theoretical insights into quantum latent dimensions and applying empirical studies on synthetic spectral data and MNIST variants, optimizing a loss function combining reconstruction fidelity and regularization.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Quantum Variational Autoencoder (QVAE), which maps classical data into a quantum latent space. The system utilizes quantum circuits (e.g., RealAmplitudes ansatz) to encode and decode quantum states, operating within a Hilbert space whose dimensionality is exponentially large relative to the number of qubits.

**Key observables.** Mean Squared Error (MSE) for predicting specific generative factors (e.g., position, width), eigenvalues of the ensemble density matrix, and the visualization of latent states on the Bloch sphere.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The regularization strength $\beta$ (controlling the prior match) and the number of qubits ($n$) are critical parameters. The spectral data is controlled by continuous parameters like peak position ($\mu$) and width ($\sigma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the structure of the latent space can be effectively analyzed by decomposing the ensemble density matrix and that regularization can enforce disentanglement, although the notion of an 'individual quantum latent dimension' remains theoretically challenging.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QVAE architecture, the latent state distributions on the Bloch sphere for different regularization strengths ($\beta$), and the MSE of predictors quantifying the information content of specific latent subsystems (qubits).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for interpretable quantum representations, developing theoretical tools (density matrix analysis), demonstrating the model's capability on synthetic spectral data (position/width factors), and concluding with architectural details and future directions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational autoencoders are powerful representation learning models that map complex data into low-dimensional latent spaces, enabling the discovery of interpretable and disentangled factors. Such representations can facilitate the interpretation and controllable generation of data describing complex scientific systems. Understanding how these factors are organized and encoded in latent space is therefore important for developing reliable representation learning models. Recently, quantum variational autoencoders (QVAEs) have been proposed as quantum representation models, demonstrating informative latent representations and improved latent-space occupancy through quantum regularization. However, it remains unclear whether and how QVAEs can learn disentangled and interpretable latent factors. A key challenge in investigating quantum latent factors is that a small number of qubits spans an exponentially large Hilbert space, making the notion of an individual quantum latent dimension nontrivial. Here, we investigate what constitutes an individual quantum latent dimension and whether it can encode a distinct factor. We develop theoretical insights into quantum latent dimensions and support them with empirical studies on representative synthetic problems, including MNIST variants. Across three datasets, we demonstrate that QVAEs can discover factorized and semantically interpretable latent representations, with individual qubits functioning as meaningful latent factors. These results establish a foundation for understanding quantum latent spaces and their potential for structured and interpretable representation learning.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents Magnus, a novel, open-source computational tool for calculating neutrino flavor oscillation probabilities. It overcomes the computational bottlenecks of traditional solvers by employing the Magnus expansion, which is highly accurate and efficient even when the matter density changes rapidly. This shifts the focus of research from developing complex numerical solvers to simply specifying the underlying particle physics model.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of the Magnus expansion to solve differential equations governing quantum evolution is a powerful numerical technique applicable to any system where the Hamiltonian changes slowly or where high accuracy is required over long evolution times, making it relevant for open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Calculating neutrino flavor transition probabilities when the matter density varies along the propagation path, which lacks a simple closed-form solution.

**Main result.** The authors introduce Magnus, an open-source Python code that computes these probabilities accurately and efficiently, outperforming existing solvers.

**Method.** It utilizes the Magnus expansion to compute the evolution operator, whose error scales highly with the step width and whose computational cost depends on the rate of density variation rather than the number of oscillations.

**Model / system.** The system is neutrino flavor oscillation, governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(l)$ that incorporates neutrino masses, mixing, and various matter interactions (e.g., non-standard interactions).

**Key observables.** Flavor-transition probability $P_{
u_\alpha 	o 
u_eta}(l)$, and phase-averaged probabilities.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Hamiltonian $H(l)$ itself, the number of flavors, and the specific density profile along the path (e.g., Earth, Sun).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the evolution is governed by a Hermitian Hamiltonian and does not handle non-unitary processes like decay or decoherence.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the $
u_e$ survival probability calculated using Magnus against a high-order Runge-Kutta solver across various flavor numbers and density profiles.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theory of neutrino oscillation, introduces the Magnus expansion for the evolution operator, describes the implementation in the open-source code, and provides extensive usage examples covering various astrophysical and terrestrial environments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interpreting neutrino oscillation measurements requires computing the probability that a neutrino born with one flavor is detected with another. In vacuum and in matter of constant density, this probability has exact formulas. Where the density varies along the path, as in the Earth, the Sun, or a supernova, it does not. There, the usual approaches either replace the profile by constant-density steps, whose error falls only as the square of their width, or integrate the evolution equation with a general-purpose solver, which must shorten its steps to meet a tighter tolerance. Both become slow at high accuracy, yet an analysis needs the probability at many energies, directions, and parameter values. Each new experiment or model often requires writing and validating a new solver. We present Magnus, an open-source Python code that avoids these limitations. It uses the Magnus expansion, whose error falls as a high power of its step width and whose cost is set by how fast the density varies, not by how many oscillations the path holds. Magnus accepts any density profile, any number of flavors, and any Hermitian Hamiltonian, such as those of non-standard interactions, sterile neutrinos, Lorentz-invariance violation, and pseudo-Dirac neutrinos. Where a cheaper route exists, it takes it automatically. For example, where the density varies slowly, as in the Sun, it follows the instantaneous eigenstates and applies the expansion only at resonances. Besides oscillating probabilities, Magnus returns phase-averaged ones, which describe solar and astrophysical neutrinos. A typical probability takes a few milliseconds, and batched scans are one to two orders of magnitude cheaper per point. Among the public codes we compared, Magnus is the fastest at high accuracy on a varying profile. Magnus shifts the effort of studying a new experiment or model from writing a solver to specifying its physics.

</details>

### [Nematic order in monomer-dimer models of Heilmann and Lieb with lateral attraction in dimensions $d\ge 2$](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08600v1)

**Authors:** Qidong He  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08600v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.08600_figures/2610.08600_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: A typical configuration of Model V on a 10 × 10 × 10 torus with parameters β = 1.8, µ = 0.2, and b = 0.3. Nematic order is evident.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves the existence of nematic liquid-crystalline order in generalized monomer-dimer models on the $d$-dimensional lattice. It achieves this by establishing the uniqueness of the Gibbs measure and proving the exponential decay of correlations using advanced techniques like cluster swapping. The results provide a rigorous mathematical characterization of orientational order in these lattice models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical techniques used (e.g., bounding correlations, proving uniqueness of Gibbs measures) are foundational to understanding phase transitions in complex many-body quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to rigorously establish the existence and properties of nematic order in $d$-dimensional monomer-dimer models of liquid crystals.

**Main result.** Under specific conditions on the chemical potential ($\mu$) and attraction strength ($b$), the model admits a unique Gibbs measure exhibiting anisotropic exponential decay of correlations within the oriented phase.

**Method.** The proof combines techniques from statistical mechanics, including cluster swapping, strong-percolation frameworks, and bounding probabilities of local configurations using mesoscopic box estimates.

**Model / system.** The system is a monomer-dimer model on the $d$-dimensional hypercubic lattice ($\mathbb{Z}^d$), describing liquid crystals. The Hamiltonian includes terms for the number of dimers ($\mu$) and a lateral attraction ($b$) between parallel dimers.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the probabilities of observing aligned versus misaligned dimers in the ordered phase, and the decay rate of correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the chemical potential $\mu$, the coupling constant $b$, and the dimension $d$. The analysis requires the conditions $(4d-6)b > \mu$ and $\mu + 2(d-1)b > 0$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of a critical inverse temperature $eta_d$ and the assumption that the relevant clusters are rare enough to be controlled by the cluster swapping technique.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a typical configuration of Model V on a torus, providing visual evidence of the predicted nematic order.

**Paper structure.** The paper generalizes Heilmann-Lieb's models to $d \ge 2$. It first establishes the mathematical framework using strong-percolation and then uses cluster swapping to prove the uniqueness of the Gibbs measure and the exponential decay of correlations in the ordered phase.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the $d$-dimensional generalization of Models II ($d=2$) and V ($d=3$) for liquid crystals introduced by Heilmann--Lieb (1979), which consists of dimers on the hypercubic lattice at chemical potential $μ$, interacting via a hard-core repulsion and a lateral attraction between parallel dimers with coupling constant $b>0$. For $d\in\{2,3\}$, these models were conjectured to exhibit liquid-crystalline order at low temperatures provided that $μ+2(d-1)b>0$. We establish nematic order for all $d\ge 2$ under the additional condition $(4d-6)b>μ$, which corresponds to the physical regime in which misaligned dimers are rare on scales comparable to the correlation length of the one-dimensional reference system. While the treatment of orientational order here follows the approach of our recent work on Model I, the proof of the absence of translational order relies on a custom implementation of cluster swapping in the sense of Sheffield (2005). Consequently, we deduce uniqueness of Gibbs measure and anisotropic exponential decay of correlations within each oriented phase.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a powerful technique using Tannaka duality to classify non-invertible symmetry-protected topological (SPT) phases in 1+1D QFTs, using only the fusion ring data. By reconstructing fiber functors, the authors identify multiple distinct phases, providing explicit mathematical structures for their associated anomalies and interface algebras.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical machinery connecting fusion rings to physical phases (SPT phases) is highly relevant for understanding topological order in condensed matter systems, providing tools to classify phases based on symmetry data.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine if a symmetry in a (1+1)-dimensional QFT with non-invertible symmetry is anomaly-free, which is crucial for characterizing trivially gapped phases.

**Main result.** The authors developed a method using Tannaka duality to find multiple fiber functors, thereby identifying several non-invertible SPT phases, particularly for the fusion ring related to extraspecial groups of order 27.

**Method.** They extract fiber functors directly from the fusion ring by solving for monoidal structure maps and tensor automorphisms, bypassing the direct solution of the pentagon equations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on (1+1)-dimensional Quantum Field Theories (QFTs) characterized by non-invertible symmetries, where the symmetry is implemented by topological line defects.

**Key observables.** Fiber functors, F-symbols, and the anomalous algebra computed on the interface between different SPT phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the fusion ring (e.g., the rank-11 ring from extraspecial groups) and invariants $(\epsilon, \iota)$ derived from the reconstruction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The procedure is tested on the maximal tensor-automorphism case, and the classification for $p>3$ is noted as conjectural.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention Table 4, which provides a comprehensive list of ring automorphisms used to generate all specific parametric forms associated with $	ext{Rep}(	ext{Esp}9)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds theory from fusion categories and Tannaka duality, develops a bootstrap procedure to find fiber functors from the fusion ring, applies this to known examples (Tambara-Yamagami), and concludes with explicit calculations for extraspecial groups.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a (1+1)-dimensional QFT with non-invertible symmetry, the fusion rules of topological lines are often the most accessible data, yet they do not fully determine whether the symmetry is anomaly-free, i.e. whether it admits trivially gapped phases. Such phases are characterized by fiber functors of the corresponding fusion category. Building on Tannaka duality, we extract fiber functors from the fusion ring alone by solving simultaneously for monoidal structure maps and tensor automorphisms. This allows us to find categorifications admitting a fiber functor without solving the pentagon equations directly. We test this procedure on the known Tambara--Yamagami ring of $\mathbb{Z}_2\times\mathbb{Z}_2$. Our main result concerns the rank-11 fusion ring shared by the representation categories of the two extraspecial groups of order 27, which has a multiplicity 3 channel and is relevant to self-triality. In both cases we find more than one fiber functor, i.e. several non-invertible SPT phases. We use the explicit data of these fiber functors to derive the $F$-symbols of the two representation categories in a compact form depending on a non-degenerate alternating bicharacter and an invertible object $ε$. Using the reconstructed data, we compute the anomalous algebra on the interface between two different SPT phases.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how chiral four-spin exchange generates an odd-parity magnon spin in a honeycomb antiferromagnet. By analyzing the magnon spectrum and calculating the thermal spin accumulation response, the authors show that this interaction leads to a measurable spin density change proportional to the temperature gradient. This work provides insight into spin-momentum locking mechanisms in frustrated magnetic insulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study connects lattice geometry (honeycomb structure) and specific higher-order spin interactions (chiral four-spin exchange) to measurable transport properties (thermal spin current), which is relevant for understanding spin-charge conversion in magnetic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the microscopic interactions responsible for generating an odd-parity magnon spin polarization in a compensated honeycomb antiferromagnet while maintaining a reciprocal magnon energy spectrum.

**Main result.** Chiral four-spin exchange generates an odd-parity magnon spin, and a temperature gradient induces a measurable spin density along the Néel axis that reverses sign upon reversing the four-spin coupling.

**Method.** The analysis employs a spin-wave expansion using Holstein-Primakoff bosons to diagonalize the quadratic Hamiltonian derived from the full spin model.

**Model / system.** The system is a honeycomb antiferromagnet, motivated by MnPS3, modeled with spins on two sublattices (A and B). The Hamiltonian includes nearest-neighbor exchange and a chiral four-spin interaction term.

**Key observables.** Odd-parity magnon spin ($	ext{L}_{n\mathbf{q}}$), magnon energies ($E_{1,2}(\mathbf{q})$), and the thermally induced spin density ($\delta s_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tilt angle ($	heta_0$) of the Néel vector, the strength of the chiral coupling ($AD$), and the temperature gradient ($
abla T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the harmonic approximation (quadratic Hamiltonian), and the calculation of thermal spin accumulation assumes well-defined quasiparticles.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the model geometry, the momentum-odd nature of the magnon spin ($	ext{L}_{1\mathbf{q}}$), and the temperature dependence of the thermal spin accumulation coefficient ($\chi_y$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the Hamiltonian, solves the bosonic eigenvalue problem to find magnon energies and spins, and then uses the relaxation-time approximation to calculate the non-equilibrium thermal spin accumulation response.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Chiral four-spin exchange generates a momentum-dependent magnon spin in a compensated honeycomb antiferromagnet at zero magnetic field. For a Néel vector tilted by an angle $θ_0$ from the layer normal, the interaction contributes to intersublattice magnon pairing already at harmonic order, with an amplitude proportional to $\cosθ_0\sin^2θ_0$. Biaxial anisotropy mixes the two longitudinal-spin sectors, producing nonquantized mode spins that are odd in momentum, while a sublattice-exchange relation keeps the magnon energies reciprocal. A temperature gradient then induces a spin density along the Néel axis through a redistribution of magnon occupations. Reversing the four-spin coupling reverses this spin density and the mode spins without changing the magnon energies. MnPS$_3$ motivates the magnetic geometry; realizing the interaction requires a structural modification compatible with the symmetry assumptions of the model.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes the precise minimum amount of GHZ entanglement that can be extracted from a quantum state given a measured violation of a MABK Bell inequality. It provides uniform analytic proofs for $n \ge 4$ parties and analyzes the stability of this bound using trace-norm distance estimates. The work is crucial for understanding the fundamental limits of quantum correlations in multipartite systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical determination of entanglement measures based on Bell inequalities provides fundamental tools for quantifying quantum correlations, which is central to quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper determines the minimum Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) extractability compatible with a given Mermin-Ardehali-Belinskii-Klyshko (MABK) Bell score for $n>2$ parties.

**Main result.** The exact minimum extractability is found to be $1 + t^2$, where $t$ parameterizes the deviation from the biseparable bound, and the authors provide uniform analytic proofs for $n \ge 4$ parties.

**Method.** The analysis uses operator inequalities, Jordan's lemma to reduce verification to qubit sectors, and spectral analysis to establish lower bounds on the target overlap based on the measured Bell score.

**Model / system.** The study concerns multipartite entangled quantum states, specifically testing entanglement using MABK Bell inequalities on systems of $n$ parties, which can be qubits or qutrits.

**Key observables.** GHZ extractability ($\Xi_n(ho)$), MABK Bell Score ($eta$), and the normalized score parameter $t$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of parties ($n \ge 3$), the local dimension of the Hilbert space, and the small error parameter $\epsilon$ used in stability analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results hold for normal states on tensor products of arbitrary local dimension and arbitrary binary measurements; stability bounds rely on smallness conditions for the excess error $\epsilon$.

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general affine minimum bound using operator inequalities, then generalizing this proof to higher numbers of parties ($n \ge 4$), and finally analyzing structural questions like the stability of the bound using trace-norm distance estimates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We determine the least Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) extractability compatible with a Mermin-Ardehali-Belinskii-Klyshko (MABK) Bell score for every number of parties greater than two. Extractability is the largest squared overlap with a GHZ state obtainable by separate local quantum channels. Between the biseparable bound and the quantum maximum, the exact minimum is the affine interpolation from one half to one. The bound holds for normal states on tensor products of arbitrary local dimension and arbitrary binary measurements. We settle the previously unresolved range of six or more parties with an analytic proof that is uniform from four parties onward. We also determine the exact minimum when every local system is a qubit, and show that it lies strictly above the unrestricted bound at every interior score. One qutrit and qubits at all remaining parties attain every point of the bound with measurements fixed as the score varies. For at least four parties and strict interior scores, we classify all states attaining the bound with the minimum product of local support dimensions. For at least four parties on one qutrit and qubits elsewhere, we also prove that, when the extractability excess above the affine minimum is small, the trace-norm distance from the attaining mixture, up to local unitaries, is bounded by a constant times the square root of that excess. The exponent $1/2$ is optimal, and the constants are independent of the number of parties on every fixed interior interval of normalized scores.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper classifies the mathematical conditions required for Perfect State Transfer (PST) to occur across all vertices of a mixed graph. It establishes that the set of possible transfer times must satisfy a specific algebraic condition related to rational cosines. The work provides deep structural insights by linking quantum dynamics to number theory and constructing examples of graphs realizing these complex transfer patterns.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between spectral graph theory (PST) and deep number theory (imaginary quadratic fields) provides a powerful, non-standard mathematical toolset for analyzing quantum dynamics in structured physical systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to classify the complete classes and determine the normalized transfer times ($\Lambda$) for Perfect State Transfer (PST) on unweighted mixed graphs.

**Main result.** A set $\Lambda$ forms a complete PST class if and only if $\cos(2\pi(x - y)) \in \mathbb{Q}$ for all $x, y \in \Lambda$, linking the spectral properties to number theory via imaginary quadratic fields.

**Method.** The proof combines arithmetic arguments involving imaginary quadratic fields with graph-theoretic constructions, utilizing spectral analysis of the graph's adjacency matrix.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by unweighted, finite, simple mixed graphs whose time evolution is governed by $U_H(t) = e^{-itH}$, where $H$ is the Hermitian adjacency matrix.

**Key observables.** The set of normalized transfer times $\Lambda \subset \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$, the minimum vertex period $T$, and the return phase $\omega$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the graph (connected, oriented), the specific values of the transfer times $\Lambda$, and the resulting bounds on the size of the PST class (at most six or three).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the case where the source vertex is nonstationary and periodic, and the graphs are unweighted and finite.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes reference arrays (A.1 and A.2) detailing square roots of cell sizes for realizing PST classes of size six and five.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing necessary and sufficient conditions for PST using field theory (Theorem 1.1), deriving constraints on periodicity and transfer times using lemmas (e.g., Lemma 2.2, Lemma 3.1), and finally constructing examples and characterizing the limits of these classes using advanced graph constructions (e.g., equitable-partition method).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For perfect state transfer (PST) on unweighted mixed graphs, we classify the normalized transfer times of complete PST classes. A finite set $Λ\subset\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ containing zero occurs at a nonstationary periodic vertex if and only if $\cos(2π(x-y))\in\mathbb Q$ for all $x,y\inΛ$. Every admissible set has a connected oriented realization. We also classify the possible return phases of oriented realizations at the minimum vertex period. Transfers at rational multiples of the common minimum vertex period partition a complete class into sets of size at most six, or at most three in an oriented graph with return phase $-1$; both bounds are sharp. We construct complete classes of every finite size, including classes in which all transfers between distinct vertices occur at irrational multiples of the period and no switching automorphism maps a class vertex to a distinct class vertex. We also characterize simultaneous realization in connected oriented graphs with prescribed relative minimum vertex periods and return phases. The proof combines an imaginary quadratic field restriction with an unweighted construction that selects the complete target set.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, post-selection-free algorithm for Quantum Automated Learning (QAL) that trains quantum models by encoding the entire learning path into a single coherent quantum circuit. By employing fixed-point amplitude amplification, the method achieves high-probability guarantees on both the learned model's quality and the associated resource costs. This work significantly advances the theoretical feasibility of implementing robust quantum machine learning protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous, resource-aware theoretical guarantees for quantum machine learning protocols, which is crucial for designing fault-tolerant quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of Quantum Automated Learning (QAL) by developing a method that eliminates the need for post-selection, which typically constrains the probability of obtaining the learned quantum model.

**Main result.** A post-selection-free QAL algorithm is proposed that organizes the entire training path into a coherent quantum circuit, using fixed-point amplitude amplification to achieve high-probability learning guarantees.

**Method.** The core method involves using fixed-point amplitude amplification (FPAA) on a coherent circuit representation of the training path, which is built using unitary dilations and flag qubits.

**Model / system.** The model is Quantum Automated Learning (QAL), which trains quantum models by directly updating quantum states via projector-mixing updates, avoiding variational parameter optimization.

**Key observables.** Success probability ($P_{	ext{chain}}$), output error ($\lVert e_{ho\omega} - ho_{\omega} Vert_1$), and learning loss ($	ext{Tr}(ar{H}ho_{\omega})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Failure probability ($\delta$), spectral floor ($p_{\star,	au}$), and resource costs associated with amplification and path execution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires fixing a classical training schedule ($\omega$) beforehand and relies on spectral assumptions for the initial theoretical guarantees.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare resource costs and success probabilities for different QAL execution strategies (e.g., Measured global restart vs. FPAA), and one figure illustrates the parameter space where the cost inequality is satisfied.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing joint path certificates, then detailing the coherent circuit construction using flag qubits, and finally applying fixed-point amplitude amplification to yield unconditioned, high-probability learning guarantees.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum automated learning (QAL) trains quantum models by directly updating quantum states, offering an approach to quantum machine learning that avoids optimizing variational circuit parameters. However, post-selection constrains the probability of obtaining the final learned model. We develop a post-selection-free QAL algorithm that organizes a complete training path into a coherent quantum circuit and uses fixed-point amplitude amplification to increase the probability of obtaining the learned model. We provide theoretical guarantees on output error and learning loss, and identify conditions under which additional coherent resources reduce the cost of restarting failed trajectories. The construction gives QAL a coherent implementation with explicit learning guarantees and resource costs, enabling a model with certified learning quality and controlled output error to be obtained with high probability.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes quantum algorithms to accelerate four key components of density peak clustering (DPC). By leveraging quantum query techniques and quantum Monte Carlo methods, the authors derive polynomial quantum speedups for tasks like cluster counting and full clustering. The work demonstrates the feasibility of applying quantum computation to complex, data-intensive unsupervised learning problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is in data science, the core methodology involves designing and analyzing quantum algorithms (e.g., quantum counting, quantum minimum finding) based on distance oracles, which is a fundamental topic in quantum algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops quantum algorithms to achieve speedups for four related tasks within density peak clustering (DPC): full clustering, decision clustering, cluster counting, and heavy cluster counting.

**Main result.** Quantum query complexity bounds are established for these tasks, showing potential polynomial speedups over classical methods under specific scaling assumptions regarding the error tolerance ($\epsilon_{crit}$).

**Method.** The approach combines quantum Monte Carlo methods for density estimation with quantum query algorithms, utilizing black-box oracles for distance queries and quantum minimum finding subroutines.

**Model / system.** The context is unsupervised data clustering, where the input is a dataset $S$ and the core operation relies on a distance oracle $d(x_i, x_j)$. The quantum computation is framed in the black-box model.

**Key observables.** Cluster assignments, density estimates ($ho_i$), cluster counts ($K$), and the mass of heavy clusters ($m_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dataset size ($N$), number of clusters ($K$), error parameters ($\epsilon, \epsilon_{crit}$), and the failure probability ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum algorithms are probabilistic, and the speedup proofs rely on assumptions about the scaling of dataset-dependent parameters, such as $\epsilon_{crit} = \omega(N^{-1})$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of density computation and the general process of DPC, while appendices contain pseudocode for the various quantum algorithms (e.g., Algorithm 3 for DPC, Algorithm 5 for quantum cluster counting).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the classical DPC framework, then details the quantum preliminaries (like quantum minimum finding), and finally presents and analyzes the quantum algorithms for the four specific clustering tasks, concluding with complexity bounds and failure probability analyses.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Clustering is a fundamental task in unsupervised learning. The goal is to partition unlabeled data into clusters such that similar elements are assigned to the same cluster, while elements in different clusters are dissimilar. We propose a quantum Monte Carlo approach to density estimation, a key routine in density peak clustering. Building on this approach, we develop quantum algorithms for the full clustering problem and three related tasks: decision clustering, cluster counting, and heavy cluster counting. For each one, we establish a polynomial quantum query speedup under assumptions on the scaling of dataset-dependent parameters. Numerical experiments suggest that these conditions are satisfied for several practically relevant settings.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a 1D classical statistical model with a helical symmetry, demonstrating that it exhibits spontaneous symmetry breaking at any temperature. This is achieved by constructing an uncountable infinity of distinct Gibbs states using the DLR formalism. The work highlights how the infinite nature of the state space allows the model to circumvent standard theorems that restrict SSB to higher dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work challenges fundamental theorems in 1D statistical mechanics by exploiting the structure of the state space, providing a theoretical framework for when and how SSB can occur in low dimensions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the possibility of spontaneous symmetry breaking (SSB) at any finite, positive temperature in a local one-dimensional model, challenging standard theorems that forbid such breaking in 1D systems.

**Main result.** The authors construct an uncountable infinity of Gibbs states, demonstrating that the model exhibits spontaneous symmetry breaking at any temperature due to the structure of the state space.

**Method.** The analysis relies on the DLR formalism and transfer matrix methods to characterize the Gibbs states and analyze the measure concentration phenomenon.

**Model / system.** The model is a classical statistical physics system on an infinite 1D lattice ($\mathbb{Z}$) with a local state space ($\mathbb{Z}^2$ or $\mathbb{Z}_2$), governed by nearest-neighbor interactions and possessing a helical symmetry group.

**Key observables.** The existence and number of distinct DLR (Gibbs) states, and the measure concentration into symmetry-related clusters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), the hyperbolic matrix $A \in SL(2, \mathbb{Z})$, and the parameters $c \in \mathbb{R}^2$ labeling the distinct states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the countably infinite on-site state space allows for a loophole in standard theorems that forbid thermal SSB in 1D.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the model and symmetries, constructing the manifold of Gibbs states using the DLR formalism, and then rigorously arguing that the measure concentration into distinct symmetry sectors implies SSB.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a one-dimensional classical statistical physics model on an infinite lattice with nearest-neighbor interactions and local state space $\mathbb{Z}^2$, with a ``helical" symmetry group $\mathbb{Z}^2\rtimes \mathbb{Z}$ mixing flavor symmetry with spatial translation. For any $0<β<\infty$, we construct an uncountable infinity of DLR (Gibbs) states parameterized by $\mathbb{R}^2$, with a free $\mathbb{Z}^2$ flavor symmetry group action; as such, we interpret the model as exhibiting spontaneous symmetry breaking at any temperature. The most general DLR state we construct is interpreted as an infinite sequence of localized domain walls separating a pair of asymptotic ``symmetry sectors". Although any normalizable Gibbs state necessarily breaks a countably infinite freely-acting symmetry group, a natural regularization of the problem to (arbitrarily) large but finite systems with periodic boundary conditions has measure concentration in disjoint and far-separated symmetry-related clusters in the (now unique) Gibbs state, which justifies our interpretation of spontaneous symmetry breaking on the infinite line. A modification of the model with local state space $\mathbb{R}^2$ must break a continuous helical symmetry at any temperature. This is not a contradiction with the Mermin-Wagner Theorem, because this continuous helical symmetry is non-compact and does not commute with translation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous thermomechanical framework to calculate the pressure tensor for hard-sphere fluids using Fundamental Measure Theory (FMT). By analyzing the free energy's response to infinitesimal deformations, the authors derive a closed-form expression for the stress tensor. This confirms the theory's consistency by matching known results for bulk fluids and confined geometries.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical fluids, the rigorous derivation of stress tensors from free energy functionals using deformation methods is a foundational technique in statistical mechanics that underpins many modern theories of many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives the local pressure tensor for hard-sphere fluids using the Rosenfeld Fundamental Measure Theory (FMT) by analyzing the system's response to infinitesimal spatial deformations.

**Main result.** The resulting pressure tensor is shown to be in a closed one-body form for monodisperse hard spheres, successfully reproducing known equations of state (like Percus-Yevick) and boundary conditions in various limits.

**Method.** The core method is a thermomechanical approach, calculating the stress tensor from the variation of the excess free energy functional with respect to an infinitesimal deformation gradient.

**Model / system.** The system studied is hard-sphere fluids, modeled using the Rosenfeld FMT, which provides a non-local description of the excess free energy functional.

**Key observables.** The symmetric pressure tensor (P_ij), normal stress, tangential stress, and disjoining pressure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fluid density ($ho$), the confinement width ($H$), and the deformation parameters ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the applicability of the thermomechanical route to nonlocal functionals and assumes the straight chord approximation for the deformation kernel.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes detail derivations involving stress tensor components (C_zz, C_xx) and the long-wavelength expansion of the FMT kernel.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the pressure tensor by first establishing the general deformation relation, then applying it to the FMT functional, and finally verifying the results in specific limits like bulk fluid, planar confinement, and analyzing stress anisotropy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive the local pressure tensor generated by the original Rosenfeld fundamental measure theory (FMT) for hard-sphere fluids from an arbitrary infinitesimal deformation. The variation of a finite-distance FMT kernel is written via the line integrals of the local deformation gradient along the straight segment connecting its endpoints. The resulting symmetric pressure tensor consists of a local term expressed through the standard FMT weighted densities and a finite-range chord term. For a monodisperse hard-sphere fluid, differential relations between the Rosenfeld measures reduce the tensor to a closed one-body form. We demonstrate that no independent pair density or inhomogeneous Ornstein--Zernike equation is required. In the limit of homogeneous fluid the tensor reproduces the Percus--Yevick compressibility equation of state. In planar confinement the normal stress satisfies the hard-wall contact relation, the mechanical surface tension agrees with the surface excess grand potential, and the disjoining pressure agrees with the derivative of the confined grand potential with respect to slit width. The finite-range chord term extends the deformation method from local field theories to nonlocal weighted-density functionals.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents R-TNR, an advanced tensor network renormalization technique that incorporates full categorical symmetry constraints into the RG flow. By doing so, it achieves a stable, non-perturbative method to calculate detailed conformal field theory data for critical systems. This realization of a 'topological bootstrap' offers a powerful tool for understanding quantum phase transitions by linking symmetry, topology, and renormalization group flow.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection between algebraic structures (fusion categories) and critical phenomena via renormalization group flow provides powerful, non-perturbative tools for analyzing quantum phase transitions, which is central to modern condensed matter theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a complete holographic formulation within tensor-network renormalization that can correctly incorporate sector partition functions that are modular covariant in general.

**Main result.** The proposed R-TNR method successfully flows initial tensors to stable gapless fixed points for suitable symmetry categories, yielding highly accurate sector-resolved conformal data.

**Method.** It introduces R-TNR, which embeds categorical constraints from a fusion category into local tensor spaces, extending the standard Tensor Network Renormalization Group (TNR) approach.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to critical systems described by Conformal Field Theories (CFTs), using examples like the Ising, tricritical Ising, and three-state Potts models.

**Key observables.** Scaling dimensions, conformal spins, and magnitudes of sector-resolved structure constants.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The choice of the fusion category R, which dictates the symmetry constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the underlying physics can be described by a stable symmetry-topological-order (symTO) protected by the categorical constraints.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces R-tensors and R-Loop-TNR, details the holographic interpretation involving modular covariance, applies the method to specific CFT examples, and concludes by defining and demonstrating the stable symTO.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Emergent generalized symmetries and the holographic principle play very important roles in understanding quantum phase transitions. In recent years, tensor-network renormalization with generalized symmetry and the corresponding fixed-point tensor formulation have been proposed as a discrete spacetime framework for reformulating conformal field theory. Nevertheless, a complete holographic formulation within this framework is still lacking, and the current formulation does not directly accommodate sector partition functions that are modular covariant in general. In this work, we formulate $\mathcal{R}$-TNR, which builds the categorical constraints of a fusion category $\mathcal{R}$ into local tensor spaces and preserves these constraints under Loop-TNR. For suitable choices of $\mathcal{R}$, the initial tensors constructed here flow under $\mathcal{R}$-Loop-TNR to stable gapless fixed points. In the Ising, tricritical Ising, and three-state Potts conformal field theory examples, a broad range of initial parameters leads to the same gapless fixed point in each example. The method resolves conformal towers by anyon sector and yields scaling dimensions, conformal spins, and magnitudes of sector-resolved structure constants with extremely high accuracy compared to previous methods. These results realize a topological bootstrap based on stable symmetry-topological-order in which finite categorical and geometric input, together with an initial tensor that need not be fine-tuned, suffices to recover the infrared conformal field theory and its sector-resolved conformal data.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a unified mathematical approach to analyze the quantum harmonic oscillator, the inverted harmonic oscillator, and the Berry-Keating Hamiltonian. By mapping these systems onto generators of the $\mathfrak{su}(1,1)$ Lie algebra, the authors provide a single theoretical framework to determine their spectra and eigenstates across different physical regimes. This unification is achieved using advanced techniques involving generalized function spaces.

**Why it may be interesting.** The unification of these three canonical Hamiltonians under the $\mathfrak{su}(1,1)$ algebra provides deep structural insight into quantum systems, which is relevant for understanding non-Hermitian or time-dependent quantum dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a unified mathematical framework to analyze three fundamental quadratic Hamiltonians in quantum mechanics: the harmonic oscillator, the inverted harmonic oscillator, and the Berry-Keating Hamiltonian.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that these three Hamiltonians can be unified by treating them as generators of $\mathfrak{su}(1,1)$ Lie algebras, providing a consistent spectral analysis across different physical regimes.

**Method.** The analysis involves linear transformations of the Hamiltonians into a canonical form and employing advanced mathematical tools, including Lie algebra theory ($\mathfrak{su}(1,1)$) and working in generalized function spaces beyond standard $L^2(\mathbb{R})$.

**Model / system.** The models are defined by quadratic Hamiltonians in position ($\hat{q}$) and momentum ($\hat{p}$) operators, specifically $H_{	ext{HO}}$, $H_{	ext{IHO}}$, and $H_{	ext{BK}}$. The analysis extends to general linear combinations of these operators.

**Key observables.** The spectrum (eigenvalues) of the Hamiltonians, which can be real (HO) or purely imaginary (IHO), and the structure of the associated eigenstates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coefficients ($\alpha_0, \alpha_1, \alpha_2$) defining the linear combination of the Hamiltonians, and the diagnostic parameter $\xi$ which determines the nature of the spectrum.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis requires moving beyond the standard $L^2(\mathbb{R})$ space to a Fréchet space $G$ to properly handle the spectrum of the inverted oscillator.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the unified Hamiltonian $H_\alpha$, establishing the spectral classification via the sign of $\xi$, and then rigorously analyzing the spectrum and algebraic structure by relating the Hamiltonians to the $\mathfrak{su}(1,1)$ algebra using various sets of generators ($K_j$ and $L_j$).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose an unified view to three well known quadratic Hamiltonians widely considered in quantum mechanics, i.e. those of the harmonic oscillator, of the inverted harmonic oscillator, and the Berry-Keating Hamiltonian. We describe an unified approach to determine the discrete spectrum of these operators, and to deduce their (generalized) eigenstates. We also look at these Hamiltonians as the generators of $\mathfrak{su}(1,1)$, and of a generalized version of it relevant in presence of non Hermitian generators.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a rigorous asymptotic analysis of machine learning performance when data features follow heavy-tailed $\alpha$-stable distributions. By employing the replica method, the authors characterize the generalization error, establishing a universal scaling law dependent only on the tail index $\alpha$. This is crucial for understanding reliable inference in real-world datasets with extreme outliers.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core is statistical learning theory, the use of the replica method and the analysis of high-dimensional random matrices/optimization problems share deep mathematical techniques with disordered systems and spin glass models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the asymptotic generalization error of Empirical Risk Minimization (ERM) when the input data features are heavy-tailed, specifically $\alpha$-stable.

**Main result.** It establishes a heavy-tail universality law, showing that the generalization error scales differently than in the Gaussian case, and provides scaling laws for typical prediction errors.

**Method.** The analysis heavily employs the replica method from statistical physics to reduce the complex high-dimensional optimization problem to an effective one-dimensional problem characterized by a functional order parameter.

**Model / system.** The model is high-dimensional linear regression where the training inputs are assumed to be entry-wise i.i.d. $\alpha$-stable distributed, contrasting with standard Gaussian assumptions.

**Key observables.** Generalization error $E^{(\alpha)}_{N,D}$, test prediction error $\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{N,D}$, and the functional order parameter $\hat{P}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tail index $\alpha$ ($0 < \alpha < 2$), the sample-to-dimension ratio $\kappa = N/D$, and the functional order parameter $\hat{P}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The loss function $\ell$ must be convex, and the analysis relies on the proportional high-dimensional limit ($N/D 	o \kappa$).

**Figures summary.** Figures validate the theoretical predictions for the limiting coefficient error $q_{\alpha}$ across various ratios $\kappa$ and tail indices $\alpha$, comparing theoretical lines to numerical results.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the ERM problem for heavy-tailed data, introducing the functional order parameter, and then using the replica method to derive asymptotic results, culminating in establishing universality laws and comparing entry-wise vs. elliptical models.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many real-world datasets exhibit unusually large values far more frequently than predicted by Gaussian models. Heavy-tailed distributions capture this behavior, yet evaluating learning performance under them remains challenging because rare, large feature entries retain non-vanishing effects even in high dimensions. Even in the canonical setting of empirical risk minimization for linear regression with entry-wise i.i.d. symmetric $α$-stable data, a precise asymptotic characterization of prediction has been lacking. In this work, we introduce a functional order parameter that describes the random effective problem associated with each coefficient. Using the replica method, we fully characterize the generalization error in the proportional high-dimensional limit where the sample size and feature dimension diverge at a fixed ratio. Additionally, this analysis establishes a heavy-tail universality law, scaling laws relating typical errors to prediction reliability, and the Bayes-optimal prediction error. In addition to characterizing the effects of extreme entries on the learning process, our method applies broadly to other systems with persistent local heterogeneity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a modular neural network built from coupled Hopfield Models, showing its mathematical equivalence to a modular Restricted Boltzmann Machine. It develops theoretical bounds to prove that this network can effectively learn to classify complex, mixed patterns by ensuring the learned weights remain stable attractors during training.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between associative memory models (like HMs) and modern machine learning architectures (like RBMs) provides a theoretical framework for understanding how physical constraints (like energy minimization) govern learning in complex, coupled systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the learning dynamics of a modular associative neural network, composed of coupled Hopfield Models, when adapted for a complex classification task.

**Main result.** It proves that under certain conditions, the RBM weights derived from the associative model form a fixed point of the learning dynamics, and it shows the setup remains effective for classifying mixtures of patterns.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving the contrastive-divergence learning rule for the modular RBM and bounding the expected reconstruction error using techniques from statistical physics and information theory.

**Model / system.** The model is a modular associative network of L Hopfield Models, coupled competitively (Hebbian intra-module, anti-Hebbian inter-module). This system is shown to be equivalent to an assembly of L coupled Restricted Boltzmann Machines (RBMs).

**Key observables.** Expected reconstruction error (E[Delta y]), RBM weights, and the residual drift of the learning dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of modules (L), network size (N), coupling strength (lambda), and dataset corruption variable (r).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the HM-RBM equivalence and uses approximations like $ar{\eta}_\mu$ as a proxy for the true pattern $\xi_\mu$ when the dataset entropy is low.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the modular model structure for L=3, showing the intra-module Hebbian and inter-module inhibitory connections.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and its equivalence to coupled RBMs. It then details the training setup for classification using contrastive divergence. Finally, it rigorously derives and bounds the expected reconstruction error to prove the stability and effectiveness of the learned weights.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider a modular associative neural network made of $L$ Hopfield models (HMs), coupled so that intra-module interactions are Hebbian and inter-module interactions are anti-Hebbian; this competitive coupling is known to endow the network with pattern-disentanglement capabilities. The integral representation of this system coincides with an assembly of $L$ restricted Boltzmann machines (RBMs) whose hidden layers are coupled, thereby extending the HM-RBM duality to the modular setting. We then train this modular RBM, via one-step contrastive divergence, to perform a classification task in which a query encoded on the visible layers is mapped onto an $L$-tuple of labels read off the hidden layers. When the query is composed of $L$ patterns that are mutually orthogonal on average, we prove that the RBM weights obtained as empirical means over the training dataset, as suggested by the HM-RBM equivalence, constitute a fixed point of the learning dynamics, and we derive an explicit, non-asymptotic bound on the residual drift. We then turn to a harder classification task in which each module is queried with a mixture of $L$ patterns and we show numerically that the same setting for the RBM weights still provides an effective set-up, letting the network jointly classify and disentangle the mixture.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a simplified Coupled Cluster theory (RAS-CCSD) designed to accurately model multireference and open-shell molecules. By restricting the cluster operator, the method overcomes the convergence failures and mathematical complexities encountered by standard CC methods near bond dissociation or conical intersections. It demonstrates the method's reliability by showing it correctly predicts energy profiles and size-extensivity across various molecular systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics as it provides a sophisticated, computationally feasible quantum chemistry method for accurately modeling bond breaking, excited states, and interactions in open-shell molecules, areas where standard single-reference methods break down.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of developing a coupled cluster theory that accurately describes the complex electronic structure of multireference and open-shell molecular systems.

**Main result.** The proposed Restricted Active Space Coupled Cluster (RAS-CCSD) framework successfully describes molecular properties, such as bond dissociation and conical intersections, where standard CCSD methods fail due to convergence issues or complex eigenvalues.

**Method.** The authors introduce a simplified coupled cluster approach by restricting the cluster operator to excitations from doubly occupied to virtual orbitals, allowing for a manageable and robust treatment of multireference character.

**Model / system.** The work is applied to molecular systems, including ethylene (for conical intersection studies), $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$ complexes, and $	ext{N}_2$ dissociation. The theoretical framework relies on coupled cluster theory adapted for restricted active spaces.

**Key observables.** Correlation energy, excitation energies ($\omega_{	ext{S}}, \omega_{	ext{T}}$), and the size-extensivity error ($\Delta E$) when separating molecules.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The active space selection (e.g., $	ext{CAS}(2,4)$ on $	ext{Be}$), the degree of correlation included (CCSD vs. CISD), and the geometry (e.g., torsion angle $	heta$ or bond distance $R$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation is restricting the cluster operator $T$ to specific excitations (doubly occupied to virtual orbitals) to ensure mathematical tractability and avoid non-commuting terms inherent in standard CC theory for open-shell systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy profiles (e.g., ethylene torsion, $	ext{N}_2$ dissociation) showing RAS-CCSD's stability versus CCSD's failure near critical points. Other figures illustrate orbital energy scans and size-extensivity checks for separated fragments.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the need for a specialized CC theory for multireference systems. It then details the RAS-CCSD formalism, demonstrating its ability to handle challenging geometries like conical intersections. Finally, it rigorously tests the method's reliability by checking size-extensivity across various dissociation and complex formation scenarios.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a simple coupled cluster approach for multireference and open-shell systems. In the restricted active space coupled cluster framework, the cluster operator only includes excitations from doubly occupied to virtual orbitals, and this ensures the individual operators commute among each other. The ground and excited states are obtained by diagonalisation of the coupled cluster similarity transformed Hamiltonian in a restricted active space. We demonstrate the framework for the RAS-CCSD model, where single and double excitations are included in the cluster operator, and likewise for a complete active space. We consider systems with multireference character and open-shell systems in a spin-adapted manner. The approach captures significantly more dynamical correlation than without the coupled cluster similarity transformation.

</details>
