**Summary.** This paper develops the theory of cyclic triorthogonal codes in prime dimensions, linking algebraic code properties to the physical requirement of implementing non-Clifford quantum gates. By establishing precise conditions and finding new code families, it significantly advances the theoretical understanding of quantum error correction for qudits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical characterization of codes that support specific non-Clifford gates is fundamental for building fault-tolerant quantum computation architectures, directly impacting the feasibility of quantum computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper studies the construction and characterization of cyclic triorthogonal codes in prime dimensions, which are crucial for implementing logical non-Clifford gates in quantum error correction.

**Main result.** The authors provide a complete description of triply-even cyclic codes for qubits and discover new, high-performing code families for qudits, improving upon existing bounds.

**Method.** The analysis relies on translating the physical requirement of gate implementation into algebraic conditions on the spectral support of cyclic codes, leading to exhaustive searches and theoretical proofs.

**Model / system.** The context is quantum error correction using qudits (dimension $p$) and qubits ($p=2$). The codes are cyclic and are designed to support transversal third-level diagonal gates acting as logical non-Clifford gates.

**Key observables.** Code parameters $[[n, k, d]]$, overhead exponent, and distillation threshold.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Prime dimension $p$, code length $n$, and the spectral support $S$ of the code.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For odd $p$, the definition of triorthogonality used does not require self-orthogonality, simplifying the search space.

**Figures summary.** The notes indicate the presence of large tables summarizing the 'Best code at each length' for qubits, qutrits, and ququints, detailing the achievable distance $d$ for various lengths $n$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem (triorthogonality and gate implementation) to deriving spectral criteria, proving results for qubits, and finally presenting extensive tables summarizing the best known codes for various dimensions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study cyclic triorthogonal codes in prime dimension $p$, on which a transversal third-level diagonal gate acts as a logical non-Clifford gate. For odd $p$, we use a definition of triorthogonality that does not require self-orthogonality. For cyclic codes of length coprime to $p$, triorthogonality reduces to a sumset condition on the spectral support, which makes exhaustive search feasible: for two natural constructions, we enumerate all maximal codes of this kind for $p=3$ up to length $121$ and $p=5,7$ up to length $96$. For qubits, we prove that a cyclic code of odd length is triply even if and only if it is classically triorthogonal, giving a complete description of triply-even cyclic codes, and we determine all such codes up to length $763$. For qudits, we find codes such as $[[19,1,3]]_3$ and $[[7,1,3]]_7$, which improve on the shortest Reed-Muller and Reed-Solomon codes in both overhead exponent and distillation threshold. The latter is the first member of an infinite family of $[[p,1,2\lfloor p/6\rfloor+1]]_p$ codes.

</details>

**Authors:** Stefano Pirandola  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08786v1>

**Summary.** This paper derives a unified, energy-constrained weak-converse bound for two-way quantum communication over noisy bosonic Gaussian channels. It establishes a common benchmark capacity $F(N)$ that applies across thermal loss, noisy amplification, and additive noise models. The result is significant because it quantifies the fundamental limits of quantum information transfer under realistic energy restrictions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous, energy-dependent capacity theorems for Gaussian channels, which are fundamental models in quantum optics and quantum communication, directly impacting the theoretical limits of quantum networking.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the persistent gap between achievable rates and converse bounds for two-way quantum communication in the presence of energy constraints and excess noise.

**Main result.** A unified, adaptive weak-converse bound, $F_{	au,v}(N)$, is derived for noisy bosonic Gaussian channels, providing a common energy-dependent benchmark that improves upon existing bounds in relevant regimes.

**Method.** The authors employ advanced information-theoretic techniques, including deriving central and adaptive bounds using regularized relative entropy of entanglement (REE) and asymptotic analysis.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on bosonic communication channels, specifically modeling three types of noise: thermal attenuation, noisy amplification, and additive Gaussian noise.

**Key observables.** Quantum transmission capacity ($Q_2$), entanglement distribution capacity ($D_2$), private classical capacity ($P_2$), and secret-key capacity ($K_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Unconditional mean transmitted-photon-number constraint ($N$), channel parameters ($	au, v, 
u$), and the energy constraint itself.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes local preparations and public randomness, and the derived bounds apply to arbitrary adaptive protocols with quantum memories.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the derived bounds for the three channel types (Thermal loss, Noisy amplifier, Additive noise), comparing the sector upper bound, hashing lower bound, and the PLOB bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the core weak-converse bound for the three channel families, then uses asymptotic analysis and the replica method to prove the concavity and structure of the bounding function $F(N)$, culminating in the final comparison with achievable hashing rates.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A gap between achievable rates and converse bounds persists for energy-constrained bosonic communication in the presence of excess noise. We derive a unified adaptive weak-converse bound for thermal attenuation, noisy amplification, and additive Gaussian noise under an unconditional mean transmitted-photon-number constraint. The bound applies to arbitrary adaptive protocols with quantum memories and constrains the two-way quantum, entanglement-distribution, private, and secret-key capacities. It vanishes throughout the entanglement-breaking region and approaches the corresponding PLOB bound at infinite energy. At finite energy, it improves the evaluated Gaussian squashed-entanglement and PLOB bounds in relevant parameter regimes, while comparison with hashing rates quantifies the remaining gap to achievability. The result provides a common energy-dependent benchmark for noisy bosonic Gaussian channels and extends finite-energy converse methods beyond the quantum-limited setting.

</details>

### [Exact Catalysis Cannot Overcome the Gaussian-Steering Barrier for Remote Wigner Negativity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08509v1)

**Authors:** Sudip Chakrabarty  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08509v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

(b) Heralding probability and excess negativity of Alice’s state for Bob’s photon-number outcomes one to six (from right to left). The solid lines are the bound on the right of Eq. (15), and the shaded region is excluded for all measurements when there is no loss. Crosses show the outcome-resolved bound in the middle of Eq. (15) for the lossless case.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes that Gaussian steering imposes a fundamental barrier to generating remote Wigner negativity in quantum states. By analyzing the 'steered negativity' resource, the authors prove that neither multiple copies nor catalytic assistance can bypass this limit when Alice is restricted to Gaussian operations. This result constrains the achievable nonclassical correlations in continuous variable quantum information protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a fundamental resource theory result for continuous variable quantum information, establishing a hard limit on nonclassical correlations achievable via Gaussian channels, which is crucial for quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether additional quantum resources, such as multiple copies, ancillas, or catalysts, can overcome the fundamental limitation imposed by Gaussian steering when attempting to generate remote Wigner negativity.

**Main result.** Gaussian steering remains a strict barrier to achieving remote Wigner negativity, even when utilizing catalysts or multiple copies, provided the catalyst is returned exactly.

**Method.** The analysis introduces and utilizes the 'steered negativity,' a multiplicative monotone, and employs rigorous mathematical proofs based on the properties of the Wigner function and trace norms for Gaussian states.

**Model / system.** The study concerns bipartite quantum states $\rho_{AB}$ shared between Alice and Bob, restricted to one-sided Gaussian protocols where Alice's operations are Gaussian.

**Key observables.** Wigner Negativity, Steered Negativity ($N(\rho_{AB})$), and Gaussian Steerability ($G_{A\to B}(\rho_{AB})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The restriction to Gaussian operations, the requirement of exact catalyst return, and the initial degree of unsteerability of the state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Alice is restricted to Gaussian operations; the analysis focuses on one-sided Gaussian protocols; the catalyst must be returned exactly.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares the theoretical bound (steered negativity) against numerical integration and photon counting, showing the bound is not attained by simple counting.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the steered negativity as a bound on negativity, proving its multiplicative properties, and then using these properties to rule out activation by copies or catalysts, culminating in the proof that Gaussian steering is an insurmountable barrier.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gaussian steering sets a sharp threshold for creating Wigner negativity at a distance. A measurement by Bob can make Alice's part of a shared Gaussian state Wigner negative only if Alice can steer him with Gaussian measurements. We ask whether multiple copies, ancillas, or catalysts can lift this requirement when Alice is restricted to Gaussian operations. We introduce the steered negativity, a multiplicative monotone whose logarithm equals the Gaussian steerability for Gaussian states. It bounds the negativity that Bob can herald and rules out activation by copies, by ancillas of Bob, and by catalysts without remote negativity of their own. For a strictly unsteerable Gaussian state and a single round of feed-forward to a Gaussian unitary of Alice, we further prove that even a Wigner-negative or steerable catalyst cannot help if it is returned exactly. Gaussian steering thus remains a barrier to remote Wigner negativity beyond the single-copy setting.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper explores the origin of entanglement properties in generalized entanglement wedges (GWs) within the context of AdS/CFT. By using integral geometry in AdS$_3$, the authors demonstrate that the strong subadditivity of GWs is mathematically guaranteed by the strong subadditivity of the boundary CFT entropy. This reinforces the idea that bulk geometric structures are dictated by the underlying quantum field theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between bulk geometric properties (GWs) and boundary quantum information properties (CFT entropy) is a cornerstone of modern quantum gravity, providing a concrete realization of the holographic principle.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the fundamental properties of Generalized Entanglement Wedges (GWs) in general spacetimes, such as strong subadditivity, originate from the structure of the dual Conformal Field Theory (CFT) in the AdS/CFT correspondence.

**Main result.** In vacuum AdS$_3$, the strong subadditivity of GWs is shown to be a direct consequence of the strong subadditivity of the boundary CFT von Neumann entropy, suggesting a deep holographic link.

**Method.** The authors employ integral geometry, specifically the Crofton formula, to relate the perimeter of a bulk region to a measure on the space of geodesics, which is then connected to boundary entanglement entropies.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed in vacuum Anti-de Sitter spacetime (AdS$_3$) on a static slice, utilizing the AdS/CFT correspondence where the bulk geometry is dual to a 2D CFT on the boundary.

**Key observables.** Generalized Entropy ($S_{	ext{gen}}$), von Neumann entropies of CFT intervals, and the perimeter of the geodesic convex regions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Leading order in $G$ (Newton's constant), and the geometry of $	ext{AdS}_3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to vacuum $	ext{AdS}_3$ and assumes the validity of the Crofton formula and the AdS/CFT dictionary for the leading order terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the characterization theorem, showing examples of regions that qualify as GWs based on convexity and perimeter relations, and relating bulk regions to the kinematic space $\Gamma$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the characterization theorem for GWs in $	ext{AdS}_3$, then uses integral geometry to relate bulk perimeters to boundary entropy measures, and finally proves the strong subadditivity of GWs by leveraging the known strong subadditivity of the boundary CFT entropy.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Generalized entanglement wedges (GWs), proposed by Bousso and Penington for gravitating regions in general spacetimes, share many properties of ordinary entanglement wedges, including nesting, monotonicity and strong subadditivity of their generalized entropies. In AdS/CFT, the corresponding properties of entanglement wedges are consequences of the structure of the dual CFT. In this paper we ask whether the properties of GWs have a similar origin. We explore this question in vacuum AdS$_3$ at leading order in $G$. We first show that every GW on a static slice is a union of geodesically convex regions. Each such region is an intersection of ordinary entanglement wedges, so every GW is built from unions and intersections of entanglement wedges. We then use the Crofton formula of integral geometry to write the perimeter of a convex region as the measure of the set of geodesics that intersect it. In AdS$_3$ every geodesic is the Ryu--Takayanagi surface of a boundary interval, so this measure can be written in terms of the von Neumann entropies of CFT intervals. Strong subadditivity of the CFT entropy guarantees that the measure is positive. Monotonicity and strong subadditivity of GWs then follow from simple inequalities between sets of geodesics. The strong subadditivity of GWs is therefore a direct consequence of strong subadditivity on the boundary. Our results further suggest that GWs correspond to subsystems of the CFT that are more general than boundary subregions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses neutron diffraction on LuInCo4 to characterize its magnetic state, confirming a collinear ferromagnetism. Key findings include evidence for a gradual localization of Co-3d electrons upon cooling, which the authors propose enhances the residual ferromagnetic instability. This mechanism is suggested to explain the unusual, anisotropic metamagnetic transition observed at low temperatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of electron localization driving a magnetic transition is central to strongly correlated electron physics, providing a concrete experimental platform to test theories of itinerant magnetism beyond simple mean-field models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the microscopic magnetic state and the origin of the low-temperature metamagnetic transition in the itinerant-electron ferromagnet LuInCo4.

**Main result.** The magnetic structure is confirmed to be a collinear ferromagnet, and the metamagnetic transition is proposed to arise from a ferromagnetic instability enhanced by the gradual localization of itinerant Co-3d electrons.

**Method.** The research employed powder and single-crystal neutron diffraction measurements, supplemented by analysis of diffuse magnetic scattering intensity evolution.

**Model / system.** The physical system is LuInCo4, a cobalt-based metallic pyrochlore compound exhibiting itinerant-electron ferromagnetism. The focus is on the interplay between electron localization and magnetic ordering in this lattice structure.

**Key observables.** Ordered Co magnetic moment ($\mu_{	ext{Co}}$), diffuse magnetic scattering intensity evolution, and the critical field ($H_m$) dependence on temperature and field direction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ordered moment is found to be around $0.9 \ \mu_{	ext{B}}$ at zero field, and the metamagnetic transition is observed below 100 K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed diffuse scattering broadening is indicative of a gradual localization of the Co-3d electron character, which enhances the magnetic instability.

**Figures summary.** Figures show PND profiles confirming a $\mathbf{q}=0$ collinear ferromagnetism, while diffuse scattering maps illustrate the temperature-dependent broadening of intensity towards higher q-space regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the basic magnetic structure using zero-field neutron diffraction, then analyzing the temperature dependence of the magnetic moments and diffuse scattering to pinpoint the mechanism (electron localization), and finally connecting this mechanism to the anomalous metamagnetic transition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigated the microscopic magnetic state of the itinerant-electron ferromagnet LuInCo4 with a cobalt pyrochlore lattice by means of powder and single-crystal neutron diffraction measurements. The powder neutron diffraction profiles at zero field are well described by a simple q = 0 collinear ferromagnetic structure with an ordered Co moment of 0.9 uB. Single-crystal neutron diffraction experiments further suggest the absence of both antiferromagnetic order and long-period helical magnetic modulation. In addition, diffuse magnetic scattering was observed around q = 0, with its intensity distribution gradually broadened in reciprocal space upon cooling. Based on these observations, we propose a possible microscopic origin of the low-temperature metamagnetic transition in terms of a ferromagnetic instability enhanced by gradual localization of the itinerant Co-3d electrons. These results suggest that LuInCo4 provides a model system for investigating the interplay between electron localization and itinerant-electron ferromagnetism in cobalt-based metallic pyrochlore compounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides an analytical framework showing that nonlinear neural networks effectively decompose the task they learn into a set of local, piecewise linear regressions defined over distinct activation regions. This structure is mathematically equivalent to a decision tree, suggesting that network selectivity is organized modularly based on these regions. The work establishes activation regions as a unifying concept for understanding network computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit mathematical link between optimization principles (least-squares) and the structural organization (decision trees) of high-dimensional representations is relevant for understanding how complex physical systems, including quantum information processing, might naturally organize their computational subspaces.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to understand how the geometry of internal representations in nonlinear neural networks is determined by the specific tasks they are learning.

**Main result.** The network's computation decomposes into a piecewise least-squares structure across distinct activation regions, which can be interpreted as a decision tree.

**Method.** An analytical framework is developed for gradient-aligned learning in piecewise-linear networks, deriving stationarity conditions based on regional gradient contributions.

**Model / system.** The model analyzed is a piecewise-linear feedforward and recurrent neural network, whose activity is partitioned into 'activation regions' ($S_r$) defined by the fixed pattern of active/inactive hidden units.

**Key observables.** Activation regions ($S_r$), regional deviation ($\Delta_r$), selectivity geometry ($\Sigma$), and the resulting decision-tree structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The per-region training loss ($\ell_r$) and the network's baseline activity level, which regulates representation diversity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a stationary point in gradient-aligned learning and that the regional input data ($X_r$) has full row rank for the least-squares decomposition.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the decomposition into a tree structure (Fig. 2) and show that the network's implemented map converges toward the local least-squares solution as training error decreases (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the analytical framework for gradient decomposition, showing the resulting piecewise least-squares approximation, interpreting this structure as a decision tree, and finally validating the prediction of selectivity geometry using task statistics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonlinear neural networks develop structured internal representations, yet how their geometry is determined by the tasks being learned remains poorly understood. Here, we develop an analytical framework for piecewise-linear feedforward and recurrent networks that links learning, activation-region structure, and neural selectivity. We show that, at any stationary point of gradient-aligned learning, a nonlinear network decomposes into local linear regressions over its activation regions. Deviations from the corresponding least-squares solutions are jointly constrained by network weights shared across regions and vanish in the low-error regime, yielding an approximate piecewise least-squares decomposition of the task. This structure admits a decision-tree interpretation, which we recover numerically by fitting trees to predict network activation patterns from the input. We further derive how the regions in which neurons are active determine the task statistics they capture, thereby organizing neural selectivity into distinct subpopulations. The resulting predictions closely match the selectivity geometry observed in simulated networks and two empirical neural datasets. Finally, we show that neural baseline regulates activation pattern diversity, placing networks along a continuum between coarse, generalizing representations and fine-grained, expressive representations. Together, these results establish activation regions as a unifying framework for describing how nonlinear networks decompose task structure into local computations and how those computations can be revealed through neural activity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, memory-efficient algorithm for performing Pauli decomposition on quantum operators acting on qubits and qudits. By leveraging character theory and the Fast Walsh-Hadamard Transform, the authors achieve a fast computational scaling while crucially avoiding the memory bottleneck associated with storing the full exponential operator matrix. This advancement is vital for implementing large-scale quantum simulations and algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly efficient, mathematically rigorous tool for analyzing quantum circuits and Hamiltonians, which is fundamental for simulating complex quantum dynamics in quantum information and computation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently decomposing a general quantum operator into the Pauli string basis, which is crucial for many quantum algorithms.

**Main result.** They developed a fast, memory-bounded algorithm using character theory (or DFT for qudits) to compute the Pauli coefficients, achieving a complexity of O(n 4^n) while avoiding the materialization of the full exponential-sized operator matrix.

**Method.** The core method involves applying Fourier analysis (specifically the Walsh-Hadamard transform for qubits) on an abelian shift-character lattice derived from the Pauli group structure.

**Model / system.** The theory applies to quantum computation involving n qubits (generalizable to qudits) and focuses on decomposing an arbitrary operator T acting on this system.

**Key observables.** The Pauli coefficients $d_{x,z}$ that define the decomposition $T = \sum d_{x,z} P(x, z)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits/qudits ($n$), the dimension ($p$), and the computational complexity ($O(n 4^n)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the structure of the Pauli group as a central extension and relies on the mathematical framework of character theory and DFT.

**Figures summary.** Figure 4 illustrates the performance benchmarking, showing active worker-thread count over time for different numbers of workers, comparing achieved speedup against theoretical limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the scientific problem (Pauli decomposition), develops the mathematical framework using character theory and DFT, presents the efficient algorithm for both qubits and qudits, and concludes with performance benchmarking and generalization discussions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pauli decomposition underpins Trotterization, phase estimation, sparse-Hamiltonian simulation, and variational observables, at exponential naive cost. From the character theory of $(\mathbb{Z}_2)^n$, characters $χ_z(v)=(-1)^{\langle v,z\rangle}$ are the diagonal Pauli strings, so any diagonal operator is already Pauli-decomposed. A general operator still needs the shifts: each $X^{\otimes x}$ couples to a character $χ_z=Z^{\otimes z}$, and the products $X^{\otimes x}Z^{\otimes z}$ -- up to phases in $\{\pm 1,\pm i\}$ -- form the Pauli group, the central extension of $(\mathbb{Z}_2)^n\times(\mathbb{Z}_2)^n$ by that phase group fixed by the symplectic pairing. A section of that extension remains a choice: the bare products $X^{\otimes x}Z^{\otimes z}$, or the genuine operators that insert $Y=iXZ$ at every qubit where $x$ and $z$ overlap. As Fourier analysis on an abelian shift-character lattice (a natural DFT), this formulation is not qubit-bound: $(\mathbb{Z}_p)^n$ recovers Heisenberg-Weyl qudits. The fast algorithm is the FFT for the group at hand; on $(\mathbb{Z}_2)^n$ that FFT is Walsh-Hadamard, at cost $O(n\cdot 4^n)$. paulikit is a memory-bounded realization of that qubit transform: peak usage need not materialize the dense $2^n\times 2^n$ operator, unlike prior codes whose $O(1)$ extra sits on that buffer, checked beyond a billion oscillator terms against Work-Span and bandwidth ceilings.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the robustness of GHZ-based quantum key distribution against noise, showing the advantage over pairwise methods depends on the underlying Pauli error structure of the noise, not just its strength. Key findings include that noise should be spread evenly across all parties and that the dealer should be the party with the least noisy link. The study provides crucial theoretical bounds but notes that transmission loss remains the most significant physical omission.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that the advantage depends on the Pauli error composition rather than the channel type provides a powerful, general structural test for quantum communication protocols, applicable across different physical noise sources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions under which the multipartite advantage of GHZ-based conference key agreement over pairwise BB84 persists when subjected to various types of quantum noise.

**Main result.** The key advantage is determined by the channel's Pauli error composition, not its specific identity, and the rate is maximized by distributing noise evenly or assigning the dealer role to the cleanest party.

**Method.** The authors simulate both protocols exactly for small $N$ under different noise channels and derive closed-form rate expressions for arbitrary Pauli noise.

**Model / system.** The model uses quantum key distribution (QKD) protocols, specifically comparing GHZ-based conference key agreement against pairwise BB84 in a star network topology.

**Key observables.** Asymptotic conference key rates ($r_{GHZ}$) and pairwise key rates, measured via error rates like $Q_x$ (collective X error) and $Q_{1,i}$ (pairwise Z error).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of parties ($N=3, 4, 5$), noise types (Dephasing, Depolarizing, Amplitude Damping), and the distribution of a fixed total noise budget.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes independent single-qubit channels on each arm and explicitly omits transmission loss, detector efficiency, and finite-key corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the preparation and distribution of an N-qubit GHZ state, while tables compare key rates under different noise distribution strategies.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by deriving closed-form rates for Pauli noise, compares the GHZ vs. pairwise protocols, analyzes the dependence on noise distribution (even vs. concentrated), and derives structural corollaries regarding the dealer role.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

GHZ-based conference key agreement distributes a shared key to $N$ parties in one network use, whereas pairwise BB84 needs $N-1$. The survival of that advantage has been studied against party number, loss, and topology, but almost always with the channel fixed at depolarizing. Simulating both protocols exactly for $N = 3, 4, 5$ under dephasing, amplitude damping, and depolarizing noise, we find the crossover is set by the channel's Pauli error composition, not its identity: normalized to average infidelity, amplitude damping and depolarizing agree to within about 1\% at each group size, while dephasing can tolerate roughly $2.4\times$ more at $N = 3$.   We derive a closed-form rate for arbitrary per-party Pauli noise and use it to prove that concentrating a fixed noise budget on one arm is strictly worse than spreading it, and that giving the dealer role to the cleanest arm improves the rate by roughly 18\%. One structural fact drives every result: the phase-error term is a product over all $N$ arms, while the bit-error term stays local to a pair.

</details>

### [Pauli Flat Quantum States Mimicking Maximal Magic](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07172v1)

**Authors:** Paweł Cieśliński  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.07172v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<sub>FIG. 1. Matrix representation of the sign vectors ⃗r for best- found Pauli flat states of N = 4 and N = 5 qubits. Each matrix is constructed by reshaping ⃗r into a 2N dimensional array with rows corresponding to its consecutive 2N-tuples.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2610.07172_figures/2610.07172_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Ratio rP for pure states with an approximate Pauli flat state structure of up to five qubits. Top bar values corre- spond to the number of non-zero and equal in absolute value Pauli expectations divided by the number of all non-identity Pauli strings. Due to their definition, all other expectations are strictly 0, and rP has an interpretation of correlation den- sity.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper explores Pauli flat quantum states, which are defined by having equal absolute expectation values across all Pauli operators. The authors demonstrate that these mixed states can mimic the correlation structure of highly magical pure states, providing a new mathematical lens to understand the necessary resources for fault-tolerant quantum computing.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between Pauli correlation structures (a mathematical tool) and the resource concept of quantum magic is highly relevant for understanding the boundaries of quantum computation and entanglement detection in many-body quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the correlation structure of quantum states that maximize quantum magic, focusing on the concept of Pauli flat states.

**Main result.** Mixed Pauli flat states can be constructed for arbitrary number of qubits and have been shown to reproduce the correlation structure of highly magical pure states, suggesting a link between mixed states and quantum resource theory.

**Method.** The authors analyze Pauli expectation values using a witness approach and employ mathematical constructions (including SDP optimization) to determine the existence and properties of these specialized quantum states.

**Model / system.** The system under study is an N-qubit quantum state, $ho$, analyzed through its expectation values on all Pauli strings. The core focus is on defining and characterizing 'Pauli flat states' based on these expectation values.

**Key observables.** Pauli expectation values ($	ext{Tr}[ho P_i]$), the non-stabiliserness witness value ($	ext{Tr}(ho W)$), and the parameter $\gamma$ defining the state structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($N$), the constant absolute value of Pauli expectations ($\gamma$), and the threshold value $2N-1$ for detecting magic.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining Pauli flat states by equal absolute Pauli expectations and uses the non-stabiliserness witness to prove the presence of quantum magic.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention figures illustrating sign vectors for $N=4$ and $N=5$, and a table summarizing the best-found values of $\gamma$, Purity, and the non-stabiliserness ratio ($\eta_S$) for small $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining quantum magic and Pauli flat states, analyzing pure state limitations (especially for $N=2$), developing the mixed state framework, proving existence theorems for mixed states, and concluding with explicit, highly technical derivations of the density matrix elements for $N=4$ and $N=5$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum magic is a necessary resource for fault-tolerant quantum computing, capturing the departure of quantum states from the classically simulable stabiliser set. Motivated by its role in quantum information processing, we investigate the correlation structure of states that maximize the Rényi stabiliser entropies, focusing on the T-type and Hoggar states. Inspired by their distinctive properties, we explore the notion of Pauli flat states, defined as N-qubit states whose expectation values on all Pauli strings are equal in absolute value. These states reproduce the correlation structure of highly magical states, which could also be interpreted as a mixed variant of Pauli covariant N-qubit symmetric informationally complete (SIC) Weyl-Heisenberg fiducial states, while not necessarily possessing nontrivial quantum magic. We investigate their existence and possible constructions, including examples both inside and outside of the stabiliser polytope. To identify the latter, we examine a magic witness tailored to the introduced family of states. Finally, we discuss their entanglement and Bell nonlocality, providing a broader characterization of their quantum properties. For completeness, we find pure states with a relaxed condition on the Pauli expectations, which forces unequal elements to zero, and discuss their non-stabiliserness. Our results offer a new perspective on the relation between quantum magic and Pauli correlation structures. They hold relevance to geometrical approaches to quantum mechanics, entanglement and magic detection, as well as estimation tasks.

</details>

### [Quantum Algorithms for Finding Vacua in the ADK Landscape](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07439v1)

**Authors:** Shirabe Endo, Yuta Hamada  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.07439v1>

A bit string m specifies one of the 2N vacua, whose energy is obtained by summing the selected contributions. The shaded band denotes the target range for the cosmological constant.</sub>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

**Summary.** This paper develops quantum algorithms to efficiently search the vast space of possible vacuum states in the ADK string theory landscape. By reformulating the search as a collision problem, the authors improve the query complexity from $O(2^{N/2})$ to $O(2^{N/3})$. This demonstrates a quantum advantage over classical methods for finding low-energy vacua.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the physical context is high-energy theory, the core contribution is a rigorous quantum complexity analysis, directly relevant to quantum algorithms for solving hard search problems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational challenge of finding a vacuum state with a small cosmological constant within the complex ADK Landscape of string theory.

**Main result.** The authors propose quantum algorithms that achieve a query complexity of $O(2^{N/3})$, significantly improving upon classical meet-in-the-middle approaches ($O^*(2^{N/2})$) for physically relevant parameters.

**Method.** The problem is reformulated as a collision problem, allowing the application of quantum search techniques like the sorted-list algorithm and quantum walks.

**Model / system.** The system is the toy model for the string Landscape (ADK model), where the vacuum energy $\Lambda_m$ is a linear combination of terms determined by $N$ binary choices ($m \in \{0, 1\}^N$).

**Key observables.** The vacuum energy $\Lambda_m$, and the target window defined by $0 \le \Lambda_m \le \Lambda_{	ext{target}}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of scalar fields ($N$), and the ratio $\Lambda_{	ext{target}}/M^4_{	ext{Pl}}$ (e.g., $10^{-120}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum algorithms assume access to Quantum-Accessible Classical Memory (QRACM) and rely on the linearity of the vacuum energy to reduce the search to a collision problem.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ADK landscape, the state register for the oracle, and the process of the meet-in-the-middle search, comparing complexities for different algorithms.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem (ADK Landscape search), derives the oracle complexity, compares plain Grover search ($O(2^{N/2})$) to classical methods, and finally develops and analyzes two quantum algorithms achieving $O(2^{N/3})$ complexity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study quantum algorithms for finding a vacuum with a small cosmological constant in the toy model for string Landscape proposed by Arkani-Hamed, Dimopoulos, and Kachru. First, we construct a function-value oracle and use it for Grover search with query complexity $\mathcal{O}(2^{N/2})$, where $N$ is the number of scalar fields. Then, we point out that, by exploiting the linearity of the vacuum energy, the problem can be reduced to a collision problem between two blocks of the bit string. With this structure, we construct two quantum algorithms with query complexity $\mathcal{O}(2^{N/3})$. Compared with the pseudo-polynomial classical algorithm of Denef and Douglas, the quantum algorithms yield a large reduction in computational cost for the physically motivated parameter values.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a comprehensive theory for synthesizing quantum circuits implementing multivariable polynomial transformations on noncommuting matrices. It achieves optimal query complexity bounds and extends the methodology to synthesize complex quantum channel transformations. This work positions multivariable approximation as a fundamental language for designing higher-order quantum information processing protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework for designing complex quantum circuits by treating multi-variable polynomial operations as a structured, synthesizable resource, which is crucial for advanced quantum algorithm design.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a complete constructive theory for synthesizing multivariable polynomial transformations of noncommuting matrices, and extending this framework to quantum channel transformations.

**Main result.** The authors provide a synthesis procedure achieving optimal query complexity for these transformations and show how this framework can synthesize any jointly contractive family of noncommutative polynomial maps as completely positive quantum operations.

**Method.** The core method involves developing a finite algorithmic Schur–Agler theorem to derive a semidefinite certificate for contractivity, which then dictates the structure of the required quantum query circuit.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum algorithms utilizing polynomial transformations applied to noncommuting matrices and generalized to quantum channels described by Kraus operators and Choi data.

**Key observables.** Query complexity ($O(D/\sqrt{	au})$ or $D$), contractivity certificate, and the resulting quantum channel operations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Degree $D$, contraction parameter $	au$, and the number of joint accesses.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The synthesis relies on a compact finite-state description of the polynomial and the existence of a joint block access model.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention figures illustrating the interleaved structure of the complete quantum circuit and the definition of the Gram matrices used in the fixed-query criterion.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the lack of a synthesis theory for multivariable transformations to developing the constructive theory using the Schur–Agler theorem, and finally applying this theory to synthesize quantum channel transformations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Polynomial transformations are basic primitives in quantum algorithms: quantum signal processing and singular value transformation compile univariate polynomials into circuits with query complexity essentially set by degree. Multivariable transformations of noncommuting matrices, however, lack a comparable synthesis theory. We develop a complete constructive theory under joint block access for all polynomials contractive on the prescribed matrix domain. Given a compact finite-state description of a degree-$D$ polynomial $P$ and $0<τ<1$, we synthesize $P$ with $(1+τ)$-optimal normalization using $O(D/\sqrtτ)$ queries, and exactly $D$ under row-block access, matching a degree lower bound. The complete circuit realization is classically computable in polynomial time with polylogarithmic dependence on accuracy. The key to our construction is a finite algorithmic Schur--Agler theorem: the coefficient recurrence defines a polynomial-dimensional continuation space supporting a complete semidefinite certificate for contractivity on the prescribed domain. Factoring this certificate yields the contractive realization underlying the query algorithm.   Beyond matrix transformations, our framework lifts multivariable polynomial synthesis to quantum channel transformations. Given coherent Kraus access $K=\{K_a\}_a$, we synthesize any finite jointly contractive family of noncommutative polynomial maps $K\mapsto\{F_b(K)\}_b$ as completely positive operations, allowing coherent interference among Kraus histories. Furthermore, channel-level transformations specified by causal Choi data can be synthesized explicitly as fixed-order quantum combs. Collectively, these results point to a broader program: multivariable approximation as a language for multi-operator quantum algorithms and higher-order quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This work applies concepts from spin-glass theory to analyze how independently trained neural networks learn complex functions, specifically binary string reconstruction. By measuring replica overlaps and defining gaps, the authors distinguish between distinct learning states like 'memorization' and 'retreat.' This provides a theoretical understanding of generalization failure in deep learning models using tools from condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of spin-glass concepts (replica overlaps, glassy dynamics) to the generalization failure of deep learning models provides a powerful theoretical framework for understanding complex, non-equilibrium learning processes.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the dynamics of independently trained Transformer neural networks reconstructing a binary string from local domain walls, characterizing the resulting ensemble of learned functions.

**Main result.** The self–cross gap ($\chi_{SG}$) successfully distinguishes three distinct learning regimes: memorization, retreat, and recovery, revealing differences in residual correlations between these states.

**Method.** The analysis uses statistical physics tools, calculating overlaps ($m, q_{	ext{self}}, q_{	ext{cross}}$) and gaps ($\chi_{SG}$) across different learning protocols and data sizes.

**Model / system.** The model is a Transformer network trained on a binary string reconstruction task defined by parity constraints, treated as an ensemble of 'replicas' whose behavior is analyzed using concepts from spin-glass theory.

**Key observables.** Truth alignment ($m$), self-confidence ($q_{	ext{self}}$), cross-replica agreement ($q_{	ext{cross}}$), and the self–cross gap ($\chi_{SG}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Training set size (small vs. large), string length ($L$), and the resulting learning regimes (memorization, retreat, recovery).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed regimes are noted to be 'operational and finite-size rather than thermodynamic phases.'

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate trajectories of block accuracy for CNN vs. Transformer, the crossover of learning probability with data size, and position-resolved residual gaps showing failure peaks.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the physical system and observables, establishing the three learning regimes using overlaps, and then providing detailed diagnostics (residual correlations, position-resolved gaps) to distinguish between memorization and retreat dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study how independently trained Transformer neural networks reconstruct a binary string from its local domain walls. Runs sharing the data and training protocol can realize different functions. We treat them as replicas and measure truth alignment $m$, prediction confidence $q_{\mathrm{self}}$, and cross-replica agreement $q_{\mathrm{cross}}$. Confident disagreement defines the finite-size replica fragmentation that we call glass-like. With small training sets, replicas predict all training examples correctly but remain confident in incorrect predictions for unseen inputs, a regime we call memorization. With larger sets, runs can generalize and then retreat. Retreat occurs when outputs start to deviate from truth while confidence remains high. The frontier between learned and unlearned outputs recedes toward shorter strings. Many later recover as the frontier advances again. The self--cross gap $q_{\mathrm{self}}-q_{\mathrm{cross}}$ clearly distinguishes the three learning regimes of memorization, retreat, and recovery. With overall and position-dependent truth alignment subtracted, their residual correlations also differ: memorizing replicas have weakly and uniformly correlated residuals, while retreat has the largest fraction of replica pairs whose residuals are anti-correlated. That is, on the inputs where one replica of such a pair does better than its average, the other tends to do worse. These finite-size observations distinguish persistent memorization from ongoing retreat--recovery dynamics. The thermodynamic and long-time limits, and extensions to other learning tasks, remain open.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the resource limitations of using Grover's algorithm to solve the NP-hard Minimum Vertex Cover problem. The authors introduce three advanced quantum encoding strategies—Dicke-Parallel, Edge-Counting, and Edge-Centric—to minimize qubit count and circuit depth. By analyzing trade-offs based on graph density, they provide practical guidance for selecting the most hardware-efficient quantum algorithm for this optimization task.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on graph theory, the resource analysis provides deep insights into optimizing quantum circuit structure (oracle design) to manage qubit and gate overhead, which is relevant for any complex quantum simulation.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Minimum Vertex Cover (MVC) problem, a fundamental NP-hard combinatorial optimization problem.

**Main result.** The authors propose and analyze three resource-aware Grover-based formulations (Dicke-Parallel, Edge-Counting, Edge-Centric) that offer trade-offs in qubit count, circuit depth, and gate complexity depending on the graph structure.

**Method.** They develop and compare novel quantum encoding and oracle-design strategies to minimize the quantum resource overhead associated with running Grover's algorithm for MVC.

**Model / system.** The model is the quantum computation framework utilizing Grover's algorithm to search the solution space of the MVC problem on a given graph structure.

**Key observables.** Qubit count, circuit depth, gate complexity, and Grover iteration count.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph density (relationship between edges $m$ and vertices $n$), and the required fixed cardinality $k$ for the vertex cover.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The resource cost is not solely determined by search space size; the internal organization of the oracle, especially cardinality enforcement, is a major bottleneck.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows the quantum circuit diagram for the Dicke-Parallel Grover search; Figure 11 summarizes resource results across various graph density levels for the different formulations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the resource overhead of standard Grover MVC formulations, then introduces and analyzes three novel designs (Dicke-Parallel, Edge-Counting, Edge-Centric) by comparing their resource costs across different graph densities.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Minimum Vertex Cover (MVC) problem is a fundamental NP-hard combinatorial optimization problem with applications in network analysis and resource allocation. Grover's algorithm provides a quadratic reduction in query complexity for unstructured search, but existing Grover-based MVC formulations can incur substantial quantum resource overhead due to costly vertex-counting circuits and complex oracle constructions. We develop and evaluate several encoding and oracle-design strategies for reducing the qubit count, circuit depth, and gate complexity of Grover-based MVC search. First, we construct a Dicke-Parallel formulation that restricts the search to fixed-cardinality subsets, eliminating explicit vertex counting, together with a parallel edge-verification oracle that reduces feasibility-checking overhead. We then develop an Edge-Counting formulation that replaces per-edge auxiliary storage with a logarithmic-size counting register, substantially reducing ancillary-qubit requirements. Finally, we propose an Edge-Centric encoding that represents endpoint selections directly and derives vertex-selection states through incident-edge Boolean operations, enabling more depth-efficient oracle construction. Our resource analysis reveals complementary trade-offs among qubit width, circuit depth, gate count, and Grover iteration count. Edge-Counting is particularly attractive under tight qubit constraints, while Edge-Centric can reduce both circuit depth and Grover iteration count when the graph has a moderate edge count and sufficient representation multiplicity. Dicke-Parallel provides a more robust choice when such multiplicity is limited or graph density makes the edge-based search space large. These results provide practical guidance for selecting Grover-based MVC formulations according to hardware constraints and graph structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores free-electron Space-Time Wave Packets (STWPs) capable of carrying tunable topological textures, specifically Skyrmions and Merons. By constraining the momentum-space spectrum, the authors show how to create propagation-invariant wave packets. This work establishes a novel platform for using electron wave packets in topological sensing and information transfer.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from quantum optics (tunable wave packets) with condensed matter topology (Skyrmions) using matter waves (electrons), providing a platform for topological information transfer.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the properties of Space-Time Wave Packets (STWPs) for free electrons, focusing on their ability to carry and maintain tunable topological textures like Skyrmions and Merons.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that free-electron STWPs can be constructed to be propagation-invariant and host robust, real-space Skyrmionic expectation value textures by tuning the spectral geometry.

**Method.** The study involves constraining the spatiotemporal spectrum of the electron wavefunction in momentum space and analyzing the resulting topology using spectral geometry.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using non-relativistic free-electron wave packets, analyzed in both momentum and real space. The topological structure is encoded in the local spin expectation value (SEV) of the electron spinor.

**Key observables.** Skyrmion number (Q), local spin expectation value (SEV), and the propagation invariance of the topological texture.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Group velocity ($v_g$), carrier wavenumber ($k_0$), and the spectral tilt angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to non-relativistic free-electrons, and the initial construction is noted to be a scalar construction lacking vector structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of momentum space Skyrmions via closed spectral loops, the allowed parameter space for hosting Skyrmions/Merons, and the resulting robust real-space SEV textures.

**Paper structure.** The paper methodically constructs the STWP in momentum space, analyzes the topological constraints (Skyrmion vs. Meron), and then demonstrates the physical realization and robustness of these topological textures in real space.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Space-Time Wave Packets (STWPs) have enabled numerous nontrivial properties, such as tunable group velocities, nondiffraction and nonlocal spin textures in optical fields. However, this has not been properly studied in matter waves. Here, we investigate free-electron STWPs, which are propagation-invariant and group-velocity-tunable matter wave packets, constructed by constraining the spatiotemporal spectrum of the electron wavefunction in momentum space. Due to the unique dispersion of free-electrons, the electron STWP can carry two different topologies, and host both meron and skyrmion topological textures in momentum space. We demonstrate the topological phase transitions and tunable skyrmion numbers by spectral geometry tuning. We also show the topologically robust propagation of the electron pulses and corresponding real-space skyrmionic expectation value textures encoded within each single electron wavefunction. Our results open a platform for topological free-electron wavepackets uses in sensing and information transfer.

</details>

<a id="paper-2610.08062"></a>
### [Total Ordering of Resources: A Quantum-Classical Separation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08062v1)

**Authors:** Jingsong Ao, Julio I. de Vicente, Alexander Streltsov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2610.08062v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2610.08062_figures/2610.08062_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Schematic representation of quantum incomparability. The free states form a proper subset F ⊊Dd. For d ≥ 3, Theorem 1 guarantees resource states ρ and σ for which neither conversion direction is possible under the maximal free-operation set OF.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves that quantum resource theories in dimensions three or higher cannot be totally ordered, meaning there will always exist pairs of quantum states that cannot be ranked relative to each other. This impossibility is a unique feature of quantum mechanics, as classical systems of any size can always be perfectly ranked. The result establishes a rigorous mathematical divide between quantum and classical state transformation capabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental mathematical constraint on the structure of quantum resources, establishing a clear boundary between quantum and classical physics in the context of state transformation theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether quantum resource theories can be characterized by a single, total ordering of states, which would simplify the understanding of state convertibility.

**Main result.** It proves that for quantum systems of dimension $d \geq 3$, such a total ordering is fundamentally impossible due to the existence of incomparable states. This property is shown to be a purely quantum phenomenon.

**Method.** The authors use structural proofs by contradiction, analyzing the properties of resource monotones and leveraging convexity arguments on the state space to demonstrate the existence of incomparable states.

**Model / system.** The study concerns quantum resource theories defined over the space of density operators ($\mathcal{D}_d$) on a $d$-dimensional quantum system. The analysis contrasts these quantum systems with classical systems.

**Key observables.** Resource monotone ($M$), the existence of incomparable states ($ho, \sigma$), and the dimension ($d$) of the system.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ (specifically $d \geq 3$), and the structure of the free set ($F$) and allowed operations ($\mathcal{O}_F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The resource theory is non-trivial, and the analysis focuses on deterministic, unassisted state transformations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the concept of quantum incomparability within the state space.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the goal of total ordering, proves the impossibility for $d \geq 3$ quantum systems (Theorem 1), and contrasts this impossibility with the fact that classical resource theories *can* be totally ordered in any finite dimension.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central goal in quantum information science is understanding which physical states can be converted into others. The ideal scenario would be a totally ordered system, where every state can be ranked by a single value, and a higher-ranked state can always be converted into a lower-ranked one. Such an ordering would provide a complete description of state convertibility, making the resource structure maximally simple to characterize and compare. We prove that such a simple ordering is fundamentally impossible for any quantum system with dimension larger than 2. The underlying properties of quantum mechanics guarantee that some states will always be incomparable. Furthermore, we show this is a uniquely quantum phenomenon by constructing classical systems of any size that can be perfectly ranked. This establishes a rigorous and striking divide between quantum and classical state transformations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the geometric constraints on variational quantum circuits, showing that simply having enough parameters is insufficient for effective training. By focusing on the discrete routing structure of CNOT gates, the authors quantify how this geometry limits local optimization directions, proposing a method to actively guide the circuit's structure during training to ensure convergence.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep geometric understanding of the limitations of variational quantum algorithms, suggesting that structural modifications (routing) can be used to guide optimization away from local minima or plateaus.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether training variational quantum circuits requires more than just sufficient representational capacity; the training process must also have access to directions that improve the objective function.

**Main result.** The paper establishes a concrete link between representational capacity, local accessibility, and the ability to repair training stagnation by analyzing the role of discrete CNOT routing geometry.

**Method.** The authors use mathematical tools like operator Schmidt rank, finite-field formulas, and tangent space analysis to characterize the geometry of the reachable unitary set defined by the circuit's routing structure.

**Model / system.** The model is a variational quantum circuit built from CNOT gates and arbitrary one-qubit gates, parameterized by a mixed encoding involving discrete routing matrices (from GL(n, F2)) and continuous rotations.

**Key observables.** Operator Schmidt Rank (OSRS), local gradient coverage (tangent space projection $\Pi_{T_A}$), and the ability to restore descent directions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the system ($n$), the number of layers ($t$), and the specific structure of the CNOT routing matrix $A$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The local results do not establish efficient global search, asymptotic barren-plateau suppression, or noisy-hardware advantage.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the mechanism: tracing reachable sets, showing ambient descent directions orthogonal to the accessible space, and demonstrating how identity-initialized routing blocks can restore descent.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the global constraints using OSRS and cut-rank formulas for single and multiple CNOT blocks. It then moves to local analysis, demonstrating gradient vanishing when the accessible tangent space is orthogonal to the objective gradient. Finally, it proposes and tests a 'same-point augmentation' technique to guide the opening of new descent directions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Finding useful variational quantum circuits requires more than representational capacity: training must also have access to directions that improve the objective. We study how discrete CNOT routing shapes both capacity and local accessibility. For circuits built from CNOT and arbitrary one-qubit gates, we separate binary routing matrices from continuous rotations to relate global capacity to local tangent accessibility. An exact finite-field formula for the operator Schmidt rank of a CNOT block and cumulative multiblock bounds yield capacity constraints and a Bell-pair fidelity obstruction. Locally, routing propagates generators into a routing-dependent tangent space, so all parameter gradients can vanish even when the ambient objective gradient is nonzero; frame bounds distinguish loss of gradient coverage from poor conditioning. A routing-conditioned identity insertion preserves the current unitary, state, loss, and existing gradients while adding new tangent directions. Candidate-only and incremental projection scores provide local criteria for ranking insertions. Exact-statevector tests show strong short-horizon score-descent correlations on controlled Bell tasks and effective equal-cost selection on connected Heisenberg chains up to twelve qubits, with complementary TFIM selector controls. A broader TFIM routing ensemble exhibits routing-dependent near-stagnation. Together, these results connect representational capacity, local accessibility, and same-point repair through a common discrete routing description. An architecture need not remain fixed during training: task-dependent geometric information can guide the opening of new descent directions while preserving the learned circuit at insertion. This provides a concrete local approach to a broader problem: how useful quantum circuits can be discovered from the information a task supplies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced numerical technique to approximate the Dirac coupling operator within a multiwavelet basis. By adapting the proven X2C method, the authors enable the block-diagonalization of the Dirac Hamiltonian for bound state problems. This provides a powerful, adaptable tool for calculating relativistic quantum properties in molecular and atomic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational challenges of solving relativistic quantum mechanics (Dirac equation) in complex, non-uniform basis sets, which is highly relevant for molecular and atomic structure calculations in AMO physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a method to accurately approximate the Dirac coupling operator in a multiwavelet basis, adapting the established X2C technique.

**Main result.** The method successfully extends the energy-independent block-diagonalization of the Dirac Hamiltonian to the adaptable multiwavelet basis, allowing for robust calculations in bound state quantum mechanics.

**Method.** It approximates the coupling operator using a projector constructed from atomic eigenstates, which are then represented in the multiwavelet basis via Gaussian projections.

**Model / system.** The system involves Dirac spinors describing bound states, governed by the Dirac Hamiltonian. The core challenge is handling the coupling operator $\hat{X}$ that links the large and small components of the spinor.

**Key observables.** The coupling operator $\hat{X}$, the effective Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_{ASC}$, and the energy eigenvalues $E$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mass term $mc^2$, the potential $\hat{V}$, and the basis set parameters related to the multiwavelet decomposition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the full system's basis can be approximated by a direct sum of constituent atomic bases, and it relies on projecting Gaussian-type atomic orbitals onto the multiwavelet basis.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework for the X2C method in fixed bases, then details the adaptation of this technique to the multiwavelet basis by projecting Gaussian functions, and finally applies this to solve the eigenvalue problem for the Dirac Hamiltonian.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a method to obtain the proven exact 2-component (X2C) method, normally applied to Gaussian-type fixed Hilbert bases, in an adaptative non-uniform grid basis of the Hilbert space, namely Multiwavelets. The X2C method manages to block-diagonalise the Dirac Hamiltonian restricted to the subspace of the basis, in an energy-independent manner. The method takes inspiration from the success of the atomic mean-field X2C method on the Gaussian bases to represent the coupling operator as a projector onto the small components of the eigenstates of the constituent atoms of the system.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel, reconfigurable MEMS platform for studying moiré graphene, enabling continuous and precise control over the twist angle. By using Raman spectroscopy, the researchers map the moiré potential and observe how electronic and vibrational band structures evolve deterministically as the twist angle is tuned. This establishes a powerful, reproducible tool for investigating correlated and topological quantum phenomena in moiré materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to deterministically tune the Hamiltonian parameter ($	heta$) in a solid-state system using an engineered platform is highly valuable for studying emergent quantum phases relevant to AMO physics and many-body dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving precise and reproducible control over the twist angle in moiré graphene systems to systematically map the full phase diagram of correlated and topological phenomena.

**Main result.** The authors developed a reconfigurable MEMS platform that allows continuous, deterministic tuning of the twist angle, providing a reproducible Raman reference for twisted bilayer graphene.

**Method.** The study uses Raman spectroscopy measurements performed on a custom MEMS-based platform that mechanically rotates and assembles two graphene layers in situ.

**Model / system.** The physical system is twisted van der Waals (vdW) graphene, forming a moiré superlattice. The platform allows mechanical control of the twist angle ($	heta$) between two independently fabricated layers.

**Key observables.** Raman spectra (G-mode and 2D-mode), LO phonon dispersion (mapped via R-mode), and the evolution of the 2D mode line shape.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), moiré momentum ($q_{	ext{moiré}}$), and the resonant excitation energy ($E_L$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a linear band dispersion of the Dirac cone at large twist angles, and the R-mode visibility relies on momentum matching conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MEMS operation schematics, Raman spectra showing R-peak dispersion with angle, and the evolution of the 2D mode and G-mode signatures as hybridization occurs.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the MEMS platform, details the use of Raman spectroscopy to map the moiré potential via LO phonon dispersion, and analyzes the angle-dependent evolution of electronic (2D mode) and vibrational (G-mode) signatures.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Twisted van der Waals (vdW) systems provide a powerful platform for exploring emergent correlated phenomena, including superconductivity, magnetism, and topological phases. The twist angle offers a continuous tuning parameter for modifying the underlying Hamiltonian, however, precise and reproducible control remains challenging. Existing approaches are limited by single-use device architectures, restricted angular precision, or vertical probing geometries. Here we introduce a reconfigurable graphene-based moiré platform in which two independently fabricated layers are mechanically assembled and rotated in situ using a micro-electromechanical system (MEMS). Raman spectroscopy confirms the formation and robustness of the twist-induced moiré potential and enables continuous, deterministic control of the twist angle. The moiré potential folds the electronic band structure and activates the longitudinal optical (LO) phonon mode, allowing us to map its dispersion and directly visualize the moiré momentum. Angle-dependent electronic band hybridization is resolved through the evolution of the double-resonant 2D mode. Near \(0^\circ\), an emergent asymmetry of the \(Γ\)-point G-mode phonon reveals phonon band folding and hybridization driven by the moiré superlattice potential and interlayer relaxation. Our platform provides a reproducible Raman reference for twisted bilayer graphene while eliminating device-to-device variability. More broadly, it establishes the twist angle as a deterministically controllable parameter for systematic studies of correlated and topological phenomena in moiré quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper models resonance Raman scattering in TMD monolayers, focusing on the role of inter-valley phonon scattering and strain. Using a microscopic Hamiltonian approach, the authors predict that inter-valley scattering induces destructive interference between different Raman orders. This results in a quantifiable suppression of the incoming resonance, matching recent experimental observations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics by modeling exciton-phonon coupling and spectral line shape modification due to coupling to lattice vibrations, which is a core topic in quantum optics and open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how inter-valley phonon scattering and strain affect the resonance Raman scattering profiles in monolayer Transition Metal Dichalcogenides (TMDs).

**Main result.** Inter-valley scattering causes destructive interference between first and third-order Raman processes, leading to a measurable suppression of the incoming resonance in WSe2.

**Method.** The analysis employs a microscopic model using an effective deformation potential coupling for the exciton-phonon interaction, calculated via diagrammatic approaches and Green's functions.

**Model / system.** The system is monolayer TMDs (like WSe2) exhibiting strong exciton-phonon coupling. The model uses a Hamiltonian encompassing free exciton, free phonon, and exciton-phonon coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Resonance Raman profile, scattering spectrum S(Omega), and the exciton self-energy $\Sigma_\alpha(E, K)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain (modeled as an energy shift) and the relative strength of $A_1$-phonon emission between K- and Q-valleys.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculation is performed in the linear response regime, and approximations like the rotating wave approximation are used for the correlation function derivation.

**Figures summary.** Figures 10 and 11 illustrate the impact of strain and inter-valley scattering on the resonance Raman profiles for MoSe2 and WS2, respectively.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the theory by defining the Hamiltonian and deriving the scattering spectrum using Green's functions, then calculates the self-energies self-consistently, culminating in the prediction of the suppressed resonance profile.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The optical properties of monolayer transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDCs) are strongly impacted by exciton-phonon coupling, especially inter-valley scattering in the case of tungsten-based materials. Together with strain-tunability of the energetic K-Q valley separation this material class therefore provides a platform for semiconductor optomechanics. Here we investigate the influence of inter-valley scattering and strain on resonance Raman scattering in monolayer TMDCs using a microscopic model based on an effective deformation potential coupling for the exciton-phonon interaction. Considering exemplarily the resonance Raman profile of the A$_1$-mode in hBN-encapsulated monolayer WSe$_2$, we find that inter-valley scattering between the electronic K- and Q-valley leads to destructive interference between a first order and a third order Raman process. As a result we obtain a suppression of the incoming resonance in WSe$_2$, consistent with recently published experimental data.

</details>
